INDIAN ECONOMY
From 1979-2025

UPSC PYQs - 1979
Q1. Consumer welfare is indicated by
(a) Savings
(b) Disposable income
(c) Expenditure
(d) None of the above
Answer: (d)
• Consumer welfare = overall benefit or
satisfaction individuals derive from
consuming goods and services.
• Savings only show the part of income not
spent; it doesn't reflect utility gained.
• Disposable income shows purchasing capacity
but not how much benefit is obtained from
spending.
• Expenditure indicates spending levels, not
necessarily the welfare outcomes.
• None of these directly measures consumer
well-being or utility, which is what welfare
truly reflects.
• Hence, option d is correct
Q2. Which hydro-electric project produces maximum
power energy?
(a) Bhakra Nangal
(b) Koyna
(c) Hirakud
(d) Salal
Answer: (b)
• Koyna Hydroelectric Project (Maharashtra) is
India’s largest completed hydroelectric power
plant.
• Total installed capacity ≈ 1,960 MW.
• Comprises four stages of power generation
built deep inside the Western Ghats with
underground powerhouses.
• Plays a vital role in Maharashtra’s power
supply, often called the “Lifeline of
Maharashtra.”
• Its massive capacity surpasses Bhakra, Hirakud
and Salal projects.
• Hence, option b is correct

Q3. What is the most remarkable aspect of Indian
industry since independence?
(a) More employment opportunities
(b) Increase in production
(c) Capacity utilisation
(d) Diversification
Answer: (b)
• After independence, India focused on rapid
industrialisation and self-reliance.
• Industrial production grew steadily across
sectors like steel, cement, fertilisers, and
consumer goods.
• GDP grew from ₹2.7 lakh crore to ₹57 lakh
crore, reflecting massive output expansion.
• Exports, infrastructure and agricultural
support contributed to increased industrial
output.
• While employment and diversification
improved too, the most striking feature has
been production growth.
• Hence, option b is correct

Q4. Productivity being low, cottage industry
produced a lot because
(a) It employs large number of people
(b) No power is needed to manufacture most of the
products
(c) Raw material is generally available locally
(d) It caters mostly to the rural population
Answer: (a)
• Cottage industries are labour-intensive, often
home-based, requiring low investment.
• Even if per-worker productivity is low, the
total output is high due to large employment.

•
•

•
•

Example: Handloom, pottery, handicrafts.
These industries provide livelihood to many
rural households, compensating low
productivity with high workforce numbers.
Other options are true but not the main
reason for high total production.
Hence, option a is correct

Q5. Which two of the following are animal products?
(a) Leather and meat
(b) Silk and polyester
(c) Linen and silk
(d) Cotton and silk
Answer: (a)
• Leather is obtained by tanning animal hides
and skins, mainly cattle, goats, pigs, deer, etc.
• Meat is directly sourced from animals for
human consumption.
• Silk comes from silkworms (animal origin) but
is not paired with another animal product in
options except polyester (synthetic) and linen
(plant-based).
• Cotton & linen are plant-derived; polyester is
synthetic.
• Only leather and meat are both clearly animal
products.
• Hence, option a is correct
Q6. What is Government’s trade policy?
(a) Export promotion
(b) Import substitution
(c) Both of the above
(d) None of the above
Answer: (c)
• Post-independence, India adopted a mixed
trade policy focusing on self-reliance and
foreign exchange conservation.
• Import substitution aimed to reduce
dependence on foreign goods by promoting
domestic industries, using tariffs, quotas, and
licensing to discourage imports.
• Export promotion sought to earn foreign
exchange, boost domestic production and
integrate India with global markets through
incentives, SEZs, tax benefits, etc.

•

•

The combination helped protect infant
industries while encouraging global
competitiveness.
Hence, option c is correct

Q7. Cause of inflation is
(a) Increase in money supply
(b) Fall in production
(c) Increase in money supply and fall in production
(d) Decrease in money supply and fall in production
Answer: (c)
• Inflation = sustained increase in the general
price level of goods and services.
• When money supply increases, more money
chases the same quantity of goods →
demand-pull inflation.
• When production falls, supply reduces while
demand stays the same → cost-push
inflation.
• When both happen simultaneously, price rise
becomes persistent and structural, leading to
higher inflationary pressure.
• Hence, option c is correct
Q8. “Gresham’s Law” in Economics relates to
(a) Supply and demand
(b) Circulation of currency
(c) Consumption and supply
(d) Distribution of goods and services
Answer: (b)
• Gresham’s Law: “Bad money drives out
good.”
• When two forms of money with the same
face value but different intrinsic values
circulate, people hoard the valuable one and

•

•

•
•

spend the lesser one → circulation of bad
currency increases, good currency disappears.
Example: If gold and debased coins are both
legal tender, people will spend debased coins
and hoard gold.
Named after Sir Thomas Gresham (1519–
1579); principle existed earlier (e.g.,
Copernicus Law).
It deals with currency circulation, not
production or demand.
Hence, option b is correct

Q9. The countries of the world are economically
interdependent. This fact is proved
(a) By turmoil in Iran
(b) By hike in oil prices by OPEC and rise in taxi fares
all over
(c) Because India has political relations with almost all
countries of the world
(d) Because multinationals have set up a number of
projects in India
Answer: (a)
• The Iran turmoil (1979 Revolution) disrupted
global oil supply.
• Result: Oil prices skyrocketed worldwide,
affecting inflation, growth, trade balances
across both developed and developing
countries.
• This crisis showed how events in one region
can ripple through the entire global
economy, proving deep economic
interdependence.
• While OPEC price hikes also show
interdependence, Iran’s crisis was the
clearest proof at the time.
• Political ties or MNCs indicate connectivity,
not direct interdependence in economic
functioning.
• Hence, option a is correct
Q10. The Government’s gold auction policy is aimed
at
(a) Checking of smuggling and reducing the budgetary
deficit of the Central Government
(b) Promoting jewellery exports

(c) Making gold available to consumers for ornaments
(d) Price stabilization
Answer: (d)
• The gold auction policy was introduced to
control domestic gold prices by auctioning
imported gold through RBI and authorized
agencies.
• Aim was to bring stability to gold prices and
curb black market operations.
• While checking smuggling and earning
revenue were secondary benefits, the primary
objective was to stabilize prices in the
domestic market.
• By auctioning gold officially, the government
tried to align domestic prices with
international trends and control speculative
spikes.
• Hence, option d is correct
Q11. In the first half of the year 1978, the relations
between India and U.S.A. suffered setback on
account of
(a) U.S. declining for the supply of uranium for
Tarapur atomic power plant
(b) U.S. pressurising India to sign the Non-Proliferation
Treaty
(c) Banning the consumption of Coca Cola in the
country
(d) All factors listed above
Answer: (d)
• In 1978, Indo–U.S. relations faced multiple
simultaneous strains:
o Uranium supply issue: The U.S.
stopped supplying uranium fuel for
the Tarapur Atomic Power Plant,
affecting India’s nuclear energy
programme.
o Non-Proliferation Treaty (NPT): U.S.
exerted pressure on India to sign the
NPT, which India refused, citing
discriminatory provisions.
o Coca Cola ban: India’s decision to ban
Coca Cola as part of foreign exchange
regulations and Swadeshi policy
added to tensions.

•
•

These three developments together led to a
diplomatic setback in bilateral ties.
Hence, option d is correct

Q12. The per capita income of India in 1977-78 at
1970-71 prices was
(a) Rs. 590
(b) Rs. 690
(c) Rs. 790
(d) Rs. 890
Answer: (a)
• In 1977–78, India’s per capita income at
constant 1970–71 prices was approximately
Rs. 571–590.
• Per capita income = Total national income ÷
Total population.
• This figure reflects the low standard of living
and modest economic base of India during
the late 1970s.
• It’s an important indicator used for comparing
economic growth and development over
time.
• Hence, option a is correct
Q13. Sudden decrease of birth rate would cause
(a) Increase in investment
(b) Increase of savings
(c) Increase in per capita income
(d) Increase in production
Answer: (c)
• Per capita income rises when population
growth slows while total income remains
constant or increases.
• A decrease in birth rate reduces population
pressure, so the same income is distributed
among fewer people.
• This leads to higher per capita availability of
resources and income, even if total
production doesn’t change immediately.
• Over time, it can improve savings and
investment indirectly, but the immediate
direct impact is on per capita income.
• Hence, option c is correct

Q14. Temporary control of inflation can be effected
by
(a) Increasing the prices
(b) Increasing the taxes
(c) Restraint on the growth
(d) Reducing the prices
Answer: (a)
• Inflation = persistent rise in prices.
• A temporary control measure is to increase
administered prices of essential
goods/services to match demand and supply
in the short term.
• This measure helps to stabilize markets
temporarily, prevent hoarding, and ease
panic buying.
• Tax hikes or growth restraint are longer-term
monetary–fiscal tools, not quick fixes.
• Reducing prices artificially can worsen
shortages.
• Hence, option a is correct

Q15. Buyers’ market denotes the place where
(a) The demand exceeds the supply
(b) The supply exceeds the demand
(c) The demand and supply are well balanced
(d) Commodities are available at competitive rates
Answer: (b)
• A buyer’s market occurs when supply >
demand, giving buyers more bargaining
power.
• Sellers compete to attract buyers → lower
prices and better terms for consumers.
• Common in real estate, commodities during
glut, or industrial goods during downturns.

•
•
•

Opposite is a seller’s market, where demand
exceeds supply, giving sellers pricing power.
Competitive rates are a consequence, not the
definition.
Hence, option b is correct

Q16. India spent most of its foreign exchange
reserves in 1977-78 for
(a) Import of chemicals and fertilisers
(b) Import of crude petroleum
(c) Import of newsprint
(d) Combating inflation
Answer: (b)
• During 1977–78, India’s foreign exchange
spending was heavily directed toward
importing crude oil.
• Rapid industrialisation and urbanisation
increased energy needs, but domestic
production was inadequate, forcing reliance
on imports.
• Global oil shocks (1973 and late 1970s) had
raised international oil prices, making imports
expensive.
• Petroleum was essential for transport, power
generation and industry, hence received top
priority in forex allocation.
• Hence, option b is correct
Q17. Which of the following fertiliser plants is not in
the public sector?
(a) Gorakhpur
(b) Nangal
(c) Kota
(d) Bhatinda
Answer: (c)
• Most major fertiliser plants like Gorakhpur,
Nangal, and Bhatinda were set up under
public sector undertakings to support
agricultural growth post-Green Revolution.
• Kota fertiliser plant, however, is owned by
Shriram Fertilisers and Chemicals (SFC) — a
private sector enterprise.
• Fertiliser production was considered strategic
for food security, hence mostly under
government control.

•

Hence, option c is correct

Q18. India earns maximum foreign exchange by the
export of which of the following commodities?
(a) Iron
(b) Tea
(c) Jute
(d) Sugar
Answer: (b)
• Tea was India’s largest foreign exchange
earner for decades, especially in the 1960s–
70s.
• India was a leading global producer and
exporter, with major markets in the UK,
USSR, Middle East, and Europe.
• Iron, jute, and sugar were important but
earned comparatively less forex during this
period.
• Tea exports were strategically promoted
through the Tea Board and government
incentives.
• Hence, option b is correct
Q19. Maximum number of workers are employed in
which of the following industries in India?
(a) Sugar
(b) Textiles
(c) Jute
(d) Iron and Steel
Answer: (b)
• Textile industry is one of the largest
employers in India, both in organised and
unorganised sectors.
• It includes cotton, woollen, silk, handlooms,
powerlooms, garmenting etc., employing
millions of workers, especially in rural and
semi-urban areas.
• Sugar, jute, and iron & steel are important but
are capital intensive or regionally limited.
• Textiles contribute significantly to industrial
production, employment and exports.
• Hence, option b is correct
Q20. Which among the following States depends
primarily on thermal power?

(a) Kerala
(b) West Bengal
(c) Tamil Nadu
(d) Karnataka
Answer: (b)
• West Bengal historically depended heavily on
thermal power plants (coal-based), due to
proximity to coal fields (e.g., Raniganj).
• It has a large share of thermal power in total
generation, unlike states like Kerala and
Karnataka which rely more on hydropower
due to rivers and rainfall.
• Thermal plants were set up near coal belts to
minimise transport costs.
• Tamil Nadu has mixed (thermal + hydro + later
nuclear), but West Bengal was primarily
thermal during this period.
• Hence, option b is correct
Q21. Underground Railways are being built in India
at
(a) Bombay
(b) Calcutta
(c) Hyderabad
(d) Madras
Answer: (b)
• Calcutta (now Kolkata) was the first city in
India to get an underground metro railway
system.
• The Kolkata Metro project began in the early
1970s and the first trains started running in
1984, making it a pioneer urban transport
project.
• Kolkata was chosen due to dense population,
narrow roads, and need for efficient mass
transit.
• Other cities like Bombay (Mumbai),
Hyderabad, and Madras (Chennai) developed
metros much later.
• Hence, option b is correct
Q22. The construction of Marmugao port has been
fast in recent years because of
(a) Increased Imports
(b) Exporting iron ore from hinterland

(c) Decreasing the congestion caused due to heavy
traffic at the Bombay port
(d) All factors listed above
Answer: (d)
• Marmugao (Goa) is one of India’s major
ports, developed rapidly post-independence.
• Increased imports demanded expansion to
handle growing cargo efficiently.
• It became India’s leading iron ore exporting
port, catering to hinterland mining belts.
• To decongest Bombay (Mumbai) port, some
traffic was diverted to Marmugao, boosting its
development.
• All these factors collectively accelerated port
construction and capacity enhancement.
• Hence, option d is correct
Q23. Allocation for power sector during the 6th FiveYear Plan (1978–83) is
(a) 23%
(b) 28%
(c) 33%
(d) 38%
Answer: (c)
• Power sector was a top priority during India’s
6th Plan to support rapid industrialisation,
agriculture (irrigation), and urban
development.
• A substantial 33% of total plan allocation
went to power development — generation,
transmission and distribution.
• This reflected the emphasis on energy
infrastructure to meet growing demand.
• This investment laid the foundation for grid
expansion and rural electrification drives in
the 1980s.
• Hence, option c is correct
Q24. “Socialistic Pattern” comes through
(a) Free Economy
(b) Mixed Economy
(c) Public Sector
(d) None of these
Answer: (c)

•

•

•

•

•

The term “Socialistic Pattern of Society” was
adopted in India after the Avadi Session of
the Indian National Congress (1955).
It emphasised public sector dominance in key
industries, planning for equitable growth, and
reducing income disparities.
Public ownership and control of major
industries like steel, power, transport, banking
etc. were seen as vehicles to achieve social
justice and planned development.
Mixed economy includes both public and
private sectors, but socialistic pattern
specifically stresses public sector leadership.
Hence, option c is correct

Q25. What is a Scheduled Bank?
(a) A bank having Rs. 1000 crore deposits
(b) A bank included in the Second Schedule of the RBI
Act
(c) A bank having Rs. 10 crore deposits
(d) A bank having Rs. 100 crore deposits
Answer: (b)
• A Scheduled Bank is one that is listed in the
Second Schedule of the Reserve Bank of India
Act, 1934.
• Such banks must meet certain criteria like
minimum paid-up capital and reserves, and
their operations are regulated by the RBI.
• Scheduled banks are eligible for loans and
refinancing from the RBI and enjoy certain
privileges.
• These include nationalised banks, private
banks, foreign banks, RRBs, and some
cooperative banks.
• Deposit amounts are not the defining criteria;
legal inclusion in the schedule is.
• Hence, option b is correct

Q26. China intends to purchase from U.K.
(a) Hunters
(b) Harriers
(c) Sabers
(d) Jaguars
Answer: (b)
• In the late 1970s, China intended to buy
“Harrier” fighter jets from the United
Kingdom.
• Harriers were Vertical/Short Take-Off and
Landing (V/STOL) aircraft developed by British
Aerospace — known for their unique ability
to hover and operate without long runways.
• This was part of China’s gradual military
modernisation and efforts to diversify
weapon imports post–Mao era.
• Other options:
o Hunters were older British fighters
(1950s).
o Sabers were American.
o Jaguars were British-French but later
built under Indo-French collaboration,
not bought by China.
• Hence, option b is correct
Q27. What is the likely impact of decrease of
population on economy?
(a) Increase in per capita income
(b) Decrease in per capita income
(c) Increase in investment
(d) Decrease in savings
Answer: (a)
• A decrease in population, with income
remaining constant, raises per capita income
— since fewer people share the same output.
• It can ease pressure on resources, improve
living standards, and create surplus savings in
the long term.
• Short term, production may remain constant,
but average income per person increases.
• Investment and savings impacts are indirect
and long-term; per capita income rise is the
direct effect.
• Hence, option a is correct

Q28. Which State is the biggest producer of
cashewnuts?
(a) Tamil Nadu
(b) Assam
(c) Kerala
(d) Punjab
Answer: (c)
• In 1979, Kerala was the top producer of
cashewnuts in India.
• Cashew cultivation in Kerala benefited from
favourable climate, coastal soil, and
processing industries established in the state.
• Later, states like Maharashtra and Andhra
Pradesh overtook Kerala in total production,
but during this period, Kerala led both in area
and yield.
• Cashew is an important export crop for India,
earning valuable foreign exchange.
• Hence, option c is correct
Q29. Unemployment insurance will result in
(a) Maintenance of consumption level
(b) Decrease in consumption level
(c) Decrease in savings
(d) Increase in savings
Answer: (a)
• Unemployment insurance provides cash
benefits to workers who lose their jobs
through no fault of their own.
• This ensures basic income support, enabling
families to maintain their consumption levels
despite temporary income loss.
• It prevents sudden drops in aggregate
demand and supports economic stability
during downturns.
• Over time, this system reduces extreme
hardship while jobseekers search for new
employment.
• Decrease/increase in savings is indirect; the
primary effect is stabilising consumption.
• Hence, option a is correct

UPSC PYQs - 1980
Q1. Which statement about Colombo Plan is
incorrect?
(a) The headquarters of the Colombo Plan are at
Colombo
(b) This is a plan of economic development for South
and South East Asian countries
(c) It is going to expire in 1981.
(d) The Colombo Plan celebrated its Silver Jubilee in
1976
Answer: (b)
• The Colombo Plan is a regional
intergovernmental organisation focused on
the economic and social development of
Asia-Pacific countries, not just South and
Southeast Asia.
• It was established in 1951, headquartered in
Colombo, Sri Lanka.
• Celebrated its Silver Jubilee in 1976.
• It is not a time-bound plan; it continues as a
cooperative programme between member
governments.
• The incorrect statement is that it’s exclusively
for South and South-East Asia — it includes
broader Asia-Pacific countries.
• Hence, option b is correct
Q2. What was the impact of Western Industrial
Revolution on India?
(a) Handicrafts of India were ruined
(b) Machines were introduced in textile industry
(c) Heavy import duty was imposed on foreign goods
(d) All technical hands got employment
Answer: (a)
• The Industrial Revolution in the West,
especially in Britain, had a devastating impact
on Indian handicrafts:
o Machine-made textiles from Britain
flooded Indian markets.
o British imposed free trade for British
goods and high duties on Indian
exports, destroying competitiveness.
o India, once a global textile hub,
became a raw material supplier

•
•

(cotton) and a market for British
finished goods.
o Traditional artisans and weavers lost
livelihoods → decline of cottage
industries and rise of deindustrialisation.
Other options are either irrelevant or factually
wrong.
Hence, option a is correct

Q3. Oil has been struck in which of the following
places recently by the ONGC?
(a) Gunupur
(b) Belonia
(c) Ankleshwar
(d) Krishna Godavari basin
Answer: (d)
• Oil and Natural Gas Corporation (ONGC)
struck oil in the Krishna–Godavari (KG) basin
in the late 1970s.
• The KG basin, located off the coast of Andhra
Pradesh, is a major sedimentary basin known
for large reserves of oil and natural gas.
• This discovery marked a significant step
towards domestic energy exploration.
• Gunupur, Belonia are not major oil fields;
Ankleshwar (Gujarat) was discovered earlier
(1960s), not “recent” in the 1980 context.
• Hence, option d is correct
Q4. Rolling Plan can be best defined in context of
India as
(a) Formulation of annual plans
(b) Perspective of Five-Year Plan with the provision of
extending by one year at a time so that there would
be a constant planning horizon of five years
(c) Aims and achievements reviewed every year in a
Five-Year Plan
(d) Plan for full 5 years
Answer: (b)
• The Rolling Plan (1978–80) was introduced by
Morarji Desai’s Janata Government.
• Key feature: It had a 5-year perspective, but
every year the plan was extended by one

•

•
•

year, hence maintaining a rolling 5-year
horizon continuously.
It had three components:
1. Annual Plan for the current year.
2. Fixed-term plan (3–5 years) updated
annually.
3. Long-term perspective plan (10–15
years).
This provided flexibility, unlike rigid 5-year
plans.
Hence, option b is correct

Q5. Highest per capita income is in
(a) Punjab
(b) West Bengal
(c) Tamil Nadu
(d) Gujarat
Answer: (a)
• During the late 1970s, Punjab had the highest
per capita income among the states listed.
• Reasons:
o Green Revolution success — high
productivity in wheat and rice.
o Rural prosperity, better irrigation,
infrastructure, and marketing
systems.
o Small population relative to its high
agricultural output led to high per
capita figures.
• Gujarat and Tamil Nadu were industrially
strong but Punjab led in per capita terms
then.
• Hence, option a is correct

Q6. Hong Kong is
(a) A free port
(b) An independent state
(c) A free exchange market
(d) All of the above
Answer: (d)
• Hong Kong has traditionally been:
o A free port — no tariffs on imports;
only a few excise duties (e.g., on
alcohol, tobacco, oil).
o A free exchange market — its
economy is highly open, with free
flow of capital, goods, and services.
o Though not fully independent, it has
enjoyed high economic and
administrative autonomy (especially
pre-1997) with its own trade, financial
and customs systems.
• Its currency is linked to the US dollar, and it’s
known for being one of the most open,
competitive, and laissez-faire economies in
the world.
• All given options describe features of Hong
Kong’s status, especially in the 1980 context.
• Hence, option d is correct
Q7. Antyodaya scheme is meant
(a) To help the Muslims
(b) To help the Harijans
(c) To help the minorities
(d) To help the economically weakest sections of the
society
Answer: (d)
• Antyodaya Anna Yojana or Antyodaya
scheme, launched initially in Rajasthan (1977)
and later adopted nationally (1980), targeted
the poorest of the poor.
• Aim: Uplift the last person in the line
(“Antyodaya” = the rise of the last person).
• It focused on providing economic assistance,
employment, and essential services to the
most economically deprived households,
irrespective of caste, community or religion.

•

•

Though Harijans and minorities often
benefited, the core criterion was economic
weakness.
Hence, option d is correct

Q8. Mixed economy refers to
(a) The coexistence of heavy, small scale and cottage
industries
(b) The promotion of agriculture as well as cottage
industries
(c) The coexistence of rich as well as poor
(d) Co-existence of public as well as private sector
Answer: (d)
• A mixed economy combines features of
capitalism and socialism.
• In India, this meant:
o Public sector dominance in core
industries (e.g., steel, banking,
power).
o Private sector encouraged in
agriculture, consumer goods, small
industries.
o Regulation by the state, but not full
control — allowing market forces to
function alongside planning.
• This model was adopted post-Independence
to achieve growth with social justice.
• The other options describe sectoral or social
combinations, not economic structure.
• Hence, option d is correct
Q9. The only State in India that shows excess of
females over males is
(a) U.P.
(b) Kerala
(c) Maharashtra
(d) Tamil Nadu
Answer: (b)
• Kerala has historically been the only Indian
state with a sex ratio favouring females.
• Reasons:
o High female literacy, better
healthcare, and low female
mortality.

o

•

•

•

Progressive social indicators,
matrilineal traditions in some
communities, and higher life
expectancy among women.
Other states have traditionally had maledominated sex ratios due to socio-economic
factors and skewed gender norms.
Kerala’s demographic trend continues even
today with a sex ratio above 1000 females
per 1000 males.
Hence, option b is correct

Q10. Qantas Airways belongs to
(a) Australia
(b) West Germany
(c) East Germany
(d) New Zealand
Answer: (a)
• Qantas Airways is the flag carrier of
Australia, founded in 1920.
• It stands for “Queensland and Northern
Territory Aerial Services.”
• Qantas is known as Australia’s largest and
oldest airline, operating domestic and
international flights.
• It played a key role in linking Australia with
Asia, Europe, and America.
• Other options are unrelated — Qantas has
always been Australian.
• Hence, option a is correct

Q11. Whose signature is found on a hundred rupee
note?
(a) President of India
(b) Governor of Reserve Bank of India
(c) Prime Minister
(d) Finance Minister
Answer: (b)
• All banknotes (except Re. 1) issued in India
bear the signature of the Governor of the
Reserve Bank of India.
• This signifies that the RBI is the monetary
authority, and the notes are issued under the
RBI Act, 1934.
• The Re. 1 note is an exception — it carries the
signature of the Finance Secretary since it’s
issued by the Government of India.
• The President and Prime Minister have no
role in currency issuance.
• The Governor’s signature represents the
guarantee of the Central Bank to honour the
note’s value.
• Hence, option b is correct
Q12. To ensure the efficiency, economic condition,
etc. there are Control Boards for each major project
except one for which a statutory corporation has
been set up. That project is
(a) Farakka Project
(b) Bhakra Nangal Project
(c) Damodar Valley Project
(d) Ramganga Project
Answer: (d)
• Most major irrigation and hydroelectric
projects in India have Control Boards to
oversee planning, execution, and
maintenance.
• Ramganga Project, however, is unique —
instead of a control board, a statutory
corporation was not set up, making it an
exception to the usual practice.
• Ramganga Dam (Kalagarh Dam) is located on
the Ramganga River in Uttarakhand, within
Jim Corbett National Park.

•

•

Farakka, Bhakra Nangal, and Damodar Valley
projects had dedicated control boards or
authorities.
Hence, option d is correct

Q13. The Bhilai Steel Plant was set up with the
collaboration of the U.S.S.R., the Rourkela Steel Plant
with the collaboration of West Germany, the
Durgapur Steel Plant was constructed with the
collaboration of
(a) Britain
(b) Japan
(c) U.S.A.
(d) Romania
Answer: (a)
• India’s three major integrated steel plants in
the 1950s were set up with foreign
collaboration as part of Nehru’s
industrialisation strategy:
o Bhilai – USSR
o Rourkela – West Germany
o Durgapur – Britain (with technical
and financial assistance)
• These collaborations were strategic — aimed
at developing core heavy industries with
global support.
• Durgapur’s collaboration with Britain
reflected strong Indo–British economic ties
post-independence.
• Hence, option a is correct
Q14. Farakka agreement between Bangladesh and
India implies
(a) Sharing of river water of Ganges
(b) Free navigation
(c) Mutual understanding of border areas
(d) A peace treaty
Answer: (a)
• The Farakka Agreement (signed 1977; later
renewed 1996) is a bilateral treaty between
India and Bangladesh regarding the sharing
of Ganga river waters at the Farakka Barrage.
• Objective: Ensure fair distribution of Ganga
waters, particularly during the dry season, to

•

•
•

meet Bangladesh’s downstream needs and
India’s navigation and irrigation needs.
It represents cooperative water
management, not border or military
arrangements.
Free navigation is a separate issue under
bilateral protocols.
Hence, option a is correct

Q15. Largest nationalised Bank in India is
(a) Central Bank of India
(b) Reserve Bank of India
(c) State Bank of India
(d) Bank of India
Answer: (c)
• State Bank of India (SBI), established in 1955,
is India’s largest nationalised commercial
bank.
• Key facts:
o Majority government-owned.
o Has the largest asset base, branch
network, and customer base among
Indian banks.
o Plays a major role in government
borrowing, development projects,
and financial inclusion.
• RBI is not a commercial bank but the central
bank.
• SBI’s size dwarfs other nationalised banks like
Central Bank or Bank of India.
• Hence, option c is correct

UPSC PYQs - 1981

Q1. What is the best way of increasing agricultural
output in India?
(a) By using more fertilisers
(b) By using more water
(c) By increasing the area under cultivation
(d) By using better seeds, requisite amount of
fertilisers and modern technology
Answer: (d)
• India’s agricultural productivity can’t increase
substantially just by expanding area or using
one input like water/fertilisers.
• The Green Revolution showed that the most
effective method is a package approach:
o Use of high-yielding variety (HYV)
seeds.
o Balanced fertiliser application and
irrigation.
o Adoption of modern technology like
mechanisation, pest control, and
extension services.
• Expansion of cultivated area has natural limits
and water/fertiliser alone won’t give
sustained gains without good seeds.
• Hence, option d is correct

•

Q2. Why the main oil refineries in India are situated
near seaports?
(a) Because India needs much crude oil which is
mostly imported
(b) Because most of the crude oil is exported to other
countries
(c) Because sea water is required for refining crude oil
(d) Because petrol is available near sea ports
Answer: (a)
• India imports a large portion of its crude oil
to meet domestic demand.
• Locating refineries near ports:
o Reduces transportation costs of
imported crude.
o Allows for easy unloading and
processing at refineries.
o Ensures quick supply of petroleum
products inland.

•

Sea water is not essential for refining, and
India doesn’t export crude on a large scale.
Major refineries: Jamnagar, Vizag, Chennai,
Mumbai — all coastal.
Hence, option a is correct

Q3. ‘Grants-in-aid’ is given to the States for
(a) Improving the Centre-State relationship
(b) Reducing regional imbalances
(c) Improving the development of backward areas
(d) Various development schemes and rehabilitation
purposes
Answer: (b)
• Grants-in-aid are funds given by the Central
Government to State Governments under
Article 275 of the Constitution.
• Primary objective: reduce regional
imbalances by assisting economically weaker
or backward states.
• These grants help ensure uniform
development, financial stability, and support
centrally sponsored schemes.
• Finance Commission recommends such
transfers based on need, backwardness, and
revenue capacity.
• While they do support schemes, the main
constitutional purpose is bridging inter-state
disparities.
• Hence, option b is correct
Q4. What is the trade policy of India?
(a) Exports promotion and Import substitution
(b) Export oriented
(c) Import oriented
(d) None of the above
Answer: (a)
• India’s post-independence trade policy had
two main pillars:
o Import substitution — encourage
domestic industries by restricting
imports through tariffs, quotas,
licensing, and promote self-reliance.
o Export promotion — earn foreign
exchange, diversify markets, and
integrate India into global trade.

•

•
•

This dual approach protected infant industries
while incentivising export growth, particularly
in the 1970s–80s.
Policy was neither purely export-oriented nor
import-oriented.
Hence, option a is correct

Q5. What is the main purpose of the International
Development Association?
(a) To make loans on more flexible terms for
important development projects in underdeveloped
countries
(b) To make loans on more flexible terms for
important development projects in developed
countries
(c) To participate in development of irrigation projects
in developing countries
(d) None of the above
Answer: (a)
• The International Development Association
(IDA) is part of the World Bank Group,
established in 1960.
• It provides concessional loans and grants to
the poorest developing countries with:
o Low or zero interest rates.
o Long repayment periods.
o Focus on infrastructure, poverty
alleviation, and development
projects.
• It complements the International Bank for
Reconstruction and Development (IBRD),
which lends to middle-income countries.
• Developed countries don’t receive IDA funds.
• Hence, option a is correct

Q6. What is land ceiling?
(a) To fix a land area for irrigation purposes
(b) To fix rural land holdings at the existing level
(c) To fix urban land holdings at the existing level
(d) To fix the quantum of land held by a family
Answer: (d)
• Land ceiling refers to the legal limit on the
amount of land that an individual or family
can own.
• Purpose:
o Prevent concentration of land in a
few hands.
o Promote equitable distribution and
agrarian reforms.
o Allow redistribution of surplus land
to landless and marginal farmers.
• Land ceiling acts were passed by states from
1960s onward, with national guidelines in
1972 setting uniform limits (10–54 acres
depending on land type).
• It applies to both urban and rural areas, but
original concept was agrarian.
• Hence, option d is correct
Q7. The average rate of increase of per capita
income in 1978–79 was
(a) 4%
(b) 4.7%
(c) 6%
(d) 5.1%
Answer: (b)
• India’s per capita income rose at an average
of about 4.7% in 1978–79, higher than earlier
years.
• Growth driven by:
o Recovery in agriculture after
monsoon fluctuations.
o Improvement in industrial output and
exports.
o Moderate population growth
compared to income growth.
• Per capita income growth is a key indicator of
standard of living, as it adjusts for population
growth unlike GDP growth.
• Hence, option b is correct

Q8. How many nuclear power plants are there in
India now?
(a) 3
(b) 4
(c) 9
(d) 21
Answer: (d)
• By the 2010s, India had 21 operational
nuclear reactors in 7 power stations.
• These include stations like Tarapur,
Rawatbhata, Kalpakkam, Kakrapar, Kaiga,
Kudankulam etc.
• Installed capacity ≈ 5,780 MW, generating
around 30,000+ GWh annually.
• 6 more reactors were under construction
adding another 4,300 MW.
• This reflects India’s gradual but steady growth
in the nuclear power sector, under both
domestic programmes and international
collaboration.
• Hence, option d is correct
Q9. What is the main objective of the Rural
Development Programme?
(a) To provide roads in the rural areas
(b) To meet the growing and complex needs of rural
people
(c) To assist the poorest among the poor in rural areas
in employment
(d) To bring about overall development of rural India
covering resources and cultural and economic aspects
of the community life
Answer: (d)
• Rural development programmes aim at
holistic development of rural areas —
economic, social, cultural and infrastructural.
• Objectives include:
o Poverty alleviation and employment
generation.
o Building basic infrastructure (roads,
schools, irrigation, health).
o Improving agriculture productivity,
education, healthcare, and
livelihoods.

o

•

•

Encouraging local participation and
resource mobilisation.
Employment and infrastructure are
components, but the core aim is overall
integrated rural development.
Hence, option d is correct

Q10. Which of the following is incorrect?
(a) 1951–56 First Five Year Plan
(b) 1956–61 Second Five Year Plan
(c) 1961–66 Third Five Year Plan
(d) 1966–71 Fourth Five Year Plan
Answer: (d)
• Correct timeline of Plans:
o First Plan (1951–56) — agriculture,
irrigation focus.
o Second Plan (1956–61) —
Mahalanobis model, rapid
industrialisation.
o Third Plan (1961–66) — self-reliance,
mixed results (wars, droughts).
o Plan holiday (1966–69) — three
annual plans.
o Fourth Plan (1969–74) — growth with
stability.
• So, the Fourth Plan was 1969–74, not 1966–
71.
• Hence, option d is correct
UPSC PYQs - 1982
Q1. The recent IMF loan to India will help
immediately
(a) To meet the expenditure of 20-point programme
(b) To meet the expenditure of Sixth Five-Year Plan
(c) To correct the adverse balance of payments
(d) To remove poverty in India
Ans. (c)
• IMF loans are generally extended to support
countries facing BoP (Balance of Payments)
crises.
• In 1981–82, India borrowed SDR 3.9 billion
under the Extended Fund Facility, the largest
arrangement in IMF history at the time.

•

•

•

The main purpose was to rectify Current
Account Deficit and stabilize foreign exchange
reserves.
Such loans are not meant for plan
expenditure or poverty eradication directly,
but to meet short-term external payment
obligations.
IMF assistance improves foreign exchange
liquidity and stabilizes the rupee in crisis
years.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q2. Which of the following was not a factor for
retardation of industrial growth last year?
(a) Energy crisis
(b) Non availability of raw materials
(c) Labour disturbances
(d) Non-availability of funds
Ans. (c)
• Industrial growth slowdown in late 1970s and
early 1980s was due to energy shortages, raw
material bottlenecks, and financial
constraints, not labour unrest.
• Wars of 1965 & 1971, back-to-back droughts,
and licence raj complexities were major
structural causes of industrial deceleration.
• Low farm growth restricted raw material
supply to industries.
• Inefficient infrastructure and control
mechanisms aggravated the problem.
• Labour disturbances were not a significant
factor during this slowdown phase.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q3. NABARD, which is set up to provide loans for
rural development, is a
(a) Bank
(b) Board
(c) Block
(d) Department
Ans. (a)
• NABARD stands for National Bank for
Agriculture and Rural Development, set up in
1982.

•
•
•

•

It functions as an apex development bank for
financing agriculture and rural sectors.
It was established with an initial capital of
₹100 crore, later increased to ₹5000 crore.
It focuses on policy, planning and operations
in rural credit, refinancing cooperative banks
and RRBs.
It plays a crucial role in rural infrastructure
development and priority sector lending.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q4. Which of the following shipyards makes
warships?
(a) Cochin Shipyard
(b) Mishra Dhatu Nigam Ltd.
(c) Mazagon Dock
(d) Hindustan Shipyard
Ans. (c)
• Mazagon Dock Shipbuilders Ltd., Mumbai is
India’s premier warship building yard.
• It manufactures warships and submarines for
the Indian Navy, along with offshore
platforms.
• It has built major naval vessels like destroyers,
frigates, and submarines.
• It plays a strategic role in India’s defence
production and indigenisation.
• Other shipyards like Cochin build commercial
vessels but Mazagon specializes in combat
ships.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q5. North-South dialogue has reference to
(a) Developed and Developing countries
(b) Developing and Under-developed countries

(c) North and South countries of the world
(d) North and South America
Ans. (a)
• The North–South Dialogue refers to
negotiations between developed (North) and
developing (South) countries during the
1970s–80s.
• It focused on reforming international
economic relations to make them more
equitable.
• Developing countries demanded a New
International Economic Order (NIEO) for
fairer trade and finance systems.
• The dialogue took place in forums like
UNCTAD and the UN General Assembly.
• It reflected growing assertiveness of Third
World countries in global economic
governance.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q6. The largest source of energy in India is
(a) Hydro-electric
(b) Solar
(c) Thermal
(d) Nuclear
Ans. (c)
• Thermal power contributes the largest share
in India’s total installed capacity.
• It primarily relies on coal (about 70–72% of
domestic coal is used for power generation).
• Thermal plants provide base-load electricity,
essential for industry and urban use.
• Hydel and nuclear capacities are smaller; solar
was minimal in 1982.
• India’s energy mix has historically been coaldominated due to availability and cost
factors.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q7. Irrigation facilities should be improved urgently
in India because
(a) Irrigation yields better output
(b) Monsoon is irregular
(c) Rivers are dry most of the period in the year

(d) Land under irrigation is very small
Ans. (b)
• Indian agriculture is monsoon-dependent,
which is seasonal and uncertain.
• Erratic monsoon patterns lead to droughts or
floods, impacting farm productivity.
• Improving irrigation infrastructure ensures
stable water supply and reduces vulnerability.
• Better irrigation leads to higher cropping
intensity and productivity gains.
• Monsoon irregularity is the most urgent
reason for irrigation development, especially
for food security.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q8. White paper is a
(a) Paper of good quality
(b) Paper having white colour
(c) Document published by the government on
matters of national importance
(d) Paper used for bearer bonds
Ans. (c)
• A White Paper is an authoritative
government document on policy issues of
national importance.
• It explains problems, policy positions, or
proposed legislation.
• Used to inform the public and Parliament,
and often precedes reforms.
• Examples include white papers on economic
reforms, telecom policy, etc.
• It is not about physical paper but a policy
communication instrument.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q9. Mixed economy means
(a) Coexistence of small and large scale industries
(b) Coexistence of private and public sectors
(c) Promotion of agriculture as well as industries
(d) Coexistence of rich and poor
Ans. (b)
• A mixed economy involves both public and
private sectors working together.

•

•
•
•

India adopted this model post-independence
— strategic industries under public sector,
consumer & small industries under private.
It aims to combine social welfare with market
efficiency.
The Planning Commission directed resource
allocation in key sectors.
This structure dominated Indian economy
until liberalisation in 1991.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

•

•

•

•

•
•

Q10. The rupee exchange rate is currently linked
with
(a) Pound Sterling
(b) Dollar
(c) Basket of currencies
(d) —
Ans. (d) (i.e., Basket of currencies)
• By early 1980s, India shifted from a sterling
peg to a basket-peg system to stabilise the
rupee.
• The exchange rate was linked to a basket of
currencies of major trading partners.
• This diversified currency risk compared to
pegging with a single currency.
• The system allowed more realistic exchange
rate movements in line with trade patterns.
• It was a managed floating regime, precursor
to later liberalised exchange systems.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q11. First Five-Year Plan laid stress on
(a) Agriculture
(b) Industry
(c) Power
(d) Uplifting of poor people
Ans. (a)

The First Five-Year Plan (1951–56) focused on
agriculture and irrigation to achieve food
security in the post-Independence period.
The economy faced acute food shortages,
refugee rehabilitation issues, and foreign
exchange constraints after Partition.
Major emphasis was on community
development, land reforms, irrigation
projects like Bhakra-Nangal, and agricultural
extension services.
Industry received secondary priority, mainly
focusing on consumer goods and
rehabilitation of existing industries.
It targeted a growth rate of 2.1%, and
achieved 3.6%, largely due to good harvests.
The strategy was based on Harrod–Domar
growth model, stressing capital accumulation
through agriculture.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q12. States earn more revenue directly through
which of the following taxes?
(a) Sales tax
(b) Custom duties
(c) Excise duties
(d) Income tax
Ans. (a)
• Before GST, sales tax (now subsumed into
SGST) was the largest source of tax revenue
for states.

•

•
•
•

•

It was a state subject, levied on intra-state
sale of goods, collected by retailers and
remitted to state governments.
Customs duties are a Union subject, collected
by the Centre at ports.
Excise duties (Central Excise) were also levied
by the Union on manufacturing.
Income tax (except agricultural income) is
levied by the Centre, though states receive a
share through devolution.
Sales tax formed a substantial proportion of
states’ own tax revenue, financing their
expenditure.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q13. Which of the following States in India has the
highest density of population?
(a) Kerala
(b) Assam
(c) Uttar Pradesh
(d) Bihar
Ans. (d)
• As per the 2011 Census, Bihar has the highest
population density among Indian states.
• Bihar’s population density: 1102 persons per
sq. km, surpassing West Bengal (1029), Kerala
(859), Uttar Pradesh (828).
• High density is due to high fertility rates,
agrarian rural settlements, and limited outmigration relative to population growth.
• The Gangetic plain’s fertile soil supports
intensive agriculture and dense habitation.
• Infrastructure and resources face heavy
pressure due to this density.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q14. Which tax is not levied by the Central
Government?
(a) Wealth tax
(b) Profession tax
(c) Income tax
(d) Excise duty
Ans. (b)

•

•

•

•
•

Profession tax is levied by state governments
and local bodies under Article 276 of the
Constitution.
It applies to individuals engaged in
professions, trades, callings, and
employment.
The power to levy excise duty, income tax
(non-agricultural), and wealth tax rests with
the Union Government.
Wealth tax (abolished in 2015) was a Central
levy on net wealth above a threshold.
Profession tax is usually a small, fixed amount
collected by state governments as part of
their own revenue sources.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q15. Operation Flood refers to
(a) Use of preventive measures for flood devastation
(b) A study about floods
(c) Dairy development for increased milk production
(d) None of these
Ans. (c)
• Operation Flood, launched in 1970, was a
project of the National Dairy Development
Board (NDDB).
• It was the world’s largest dairy development
programme, aimed at creating a “White
Revolution” in India.
• It linked rural milk producers with urban
markets through a National Milk Grid,
ensuring stable prices and remunerative
returns.
• It was implemented in three phases (1970–
1996), leading India to become the largest
milk producer in the world.
• The model was based on village cooperatives,
empowering rural farmers, increasing
incomes, and ensuring regular supply.
• It transformed India from a milk-deficient
country to a milk-surplus nation.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q16. Multinational means
(a) A person having passports and visas of many
countries

(b) A person having property in many countries
(c) A person holding citizenship of many countries
(d) A company operating in more than one country
Ans. (d)
• A multinational corporation (MNC) is an
enterprise operating in two or more
countries.
• It owns or controls production, marketing, or
service facilities abroad, beyond its home
country.
• Examples include Unilever, IBM, Toyota, etc.
• MNCs bring capital, technology, and
managerial know-how, but also may
influence domestic policies.
• Their operations are often regulated by
Bilateral Investment Treaties (BITs) and
national laws.
• They are key players in globalisation and
international trade flows.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q17. Wealth tax on agricultural property is levied by
(a) Central Government
(b) State Government
(c) Both the Central and State Governments
(d) None of the above
Ans. (b)
• Agricultural taxation is a state subject under
the Constitution.
• Wealth tax on agricultural land can only be
imposed by state governments, not the
Centre.
• The Central Wealth Tax Act excluded
agricultural land from its ambit, respecting the
constitutional division of powers.
• States may impose taxes on agricultural
income, land, and property, though in
practice such taxes are often minimal.
• This reflects the federal structure where
Centre handles non-agricultural taxation and
States handle agricultural.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q18. Main source of India’s national income is from
(a) Agriculture
(b) Industry
(c) Forestry
(d) None of these
Ans. (d)
• By the early 1980s, services sector had
become the largest contributor to India’s
national income.
• Agriculture, once dominant, saw its share
decline steadily since the 1950s.
• Industry also grew but remained second to
services in contribution.
• Transport, trade, communications, finance,
and public administration were key service
contributors.
• This structural change reflects economic
diversification and urbanisation trends.
• Since none of the three options matches this,
the correct answer is “none of these.”
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q19. Capital intensive industries
(a) Increase employment in rural areas
(b) Increase unemployment
(c) Give blow to the growth of large scale industries
(d) Provide opportunities for workers’ participation in
management
Ans. (c)
• Capital intensive industries rely more on
machines and technology than labour.
• They are typically large-scale industries like
steel, petrochemicals, power, and
automobiles.
• Their growth may divert investment and
resources, affecting the expansion of labourintensive sectors.
• They can reduce employment opportunities,
but increase productivity.
• The option (c) reflects that they impact the
development pattern, influencing large-scale
industrial structure.
• Other options either relate to rural
employment or labour participation, which is

not their primary characteristic.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q20. Nehru Model of Development laid greater
stress on
(a) Collective use of land
(b) Using imported seeds
(c) Agricultural development
(d) Rapid industrialisation with particular emphasis on
the development of basic and heavy industries
Ans. (d)
• The Nehru–Mahalanobis model, adopted
from the Second Five-Year Plan (1956–61)
onwards, stressed rapid industrialisation.
• It focused on basic and heavy industries
(steel, power, machine tools) as the
‘commanding heights’ of the economy.
• The strategy was based on the belief that
capital goods industries would generate selfreliant growth.
• Public sector was assigned a dominant role in
strategic industries.
• Agriculture received subsidiary attention,
with industry-led growth being the main
strategy.
• This laid the foundation for India’s planned
industrial base post-independence.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q21. Melbourne declaration was connected with
(a) Environmental pollution
(b) Energy crisis
(c) Reducing the economic inequality between the
developed and the developing countries
(d) Human rights
Ans. (c)
• The Melbourne Declaration (1981) was
adopted at the Commonwealth Heads of
Government Meeting (CHOGM) in
Melbourne.
• It reaffirmed the Commonwealth’s
commitment to a fairer economic deal for
developing countries.
• It focused on reducing global economic
inequality through North–South cooperation.
• Emphasis was on restructuring international
trade, aid, finance, and technology transfer.
• The declaration aligned with demands for a
New International Economic Order
advocated by developing countries.
• It was not about energy or human rights but
about economic justice between developed
and developing worlds.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q1. The correct sequence of the following densely
populated States is
1. Uttar Pradesh
2. Kerala
3. Bihar
4. West Bengal
Select the correct answer from the codes given below:
(a) 2, 3, 1, 4
(b) 4, 1, 2, 3
(c) 1, 4, 2, 3
(d) 2, 4, 3, 1
Answer: (b)
• In the early 1980s, the population density
ranking among the given states was:
1. West Bengal – highest density (over
600 persons/sq. km).

•

2. Uttar Pradesh – second highest, due
to large population and moderate
area.
3. Kerala – third, high density due to
small area and significant population.
4. Bihar – fourth among these options at
that time (later overtook UP in
density).
Hence, option b is correct

Q2. The death rate according to 1981 census is ____
per thousand persons
(a) 14.8
(b) 15.8
(c) 10.8
(d) 9.8
Answer: (a)
• According to the 1981 Census, the death rate
in India was around 14.8 per 1000 persons
annually.
• Over the decades, death rates in India have
declined steadily due to:
o Improvements in public health.
o Expanded immunisation and medical
access.
o Better sanitation and nutrition.
• For comparison: By 2011, India’s death rate
had dropped to around 7.4 per 1000.
• Hence, option a is correct
Q3. The number of French Mirage-2000 fighter
aircraft that France has agreed to supply to India
between 1984–86 is
(a) 30
(b) 40
(c) 50
(d) 60
Answer: (a)
• In the early 1980s, India signed an agreement
with France to purchase 30 Mirage-2000
fighter aircraft, to be delivered between 1984
and 1986.
• Purpose:
o Strengthen the Indian Air Force post1971 war.

o

•

•

Balance regional power, especially
after Pakistan’s acquisition of F-16s.
Mirage-2000 became one of India’s frontline
multi-role fighters, known for its agility and
advanced avionics.
Hence, option a is correct

Q4. The number of international airports in India is
(a) 4
(b) 5
(c) 7
(d) 10
Answer: (b)
• In the early 1980s, India had 5 international
airports:
1. Mumbai (Bombay)
2. Delhi
3. Chennai (Madras)
4. Kolkata (Calcutta)
5. Thiruvananthapuram (Trivandrum)
• These were the primary international
gateways for trade, travel, and connectivity at
the time.
• Since then, India has expanded to 26
international airports (e.g., Hyderabad,
Bengaluru, Cochin, Goa).
• Hence, option b is correct
Q5. The rate of inflation in India in 1981–82 went
down to the extent of approximately
(a) 5%
(b) 10%
(c) 14%
(d) 16%
Answer: (b)
• In 1981–82, India’s inflation rate dropped to
around 10%, after experiencing higher levels
in the late 1970s.
• Reasons for decline:
o Improved agricultural output due to
good monsoons.
o Tighter monetary policy by RBI.
o Moderation in global oil prices
compared to the 1979 crisis.

•

•

This period saw stabilisation efforts to control
price rise through fiscal discipline and supplyside measures.
Hence, option b is correct
UPSC PYQs - 1984

Q1. What type of trade is being undertaken by India
with East European countries?
(a) Rupee trade
(b) Free trade
(c) Barter trade
(d) Bilateral trade
Ans. (d)
• Bilateral trade uses government-togovernment protocols specifying lists of
goods, quantities, and settlement modalities;
reduces exposure to volatile third-country
policies.
• Enables reciprocal market access for priority
items (e.g., machinery from them,
pharmaceuticals/tea/textiles from India),
often with multi-year commitments that
stabilize orders and prices.
• Can incorporate clearing/compensation
accounts, currency swap/clearing rupee
accounts, and state credit lines, easing hardcurrency constraints.
• Distinct from free trade (multilateral, low
barriers), barter (direct goods-for-goods swap
without monetary settlement), or purely
rupee trade (a specific settlement form
sometimes embedded inside bilateral deals).
• Supports strategic autonomy, technology
access, and spare-parts assurance even amid
global disruptions; aligns with importsubstitution and diversification objectives.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q2. New high yielding varieties of food crops are
different from the conventional in that they need
(a) Less water
(b) Less fertilisers
(c) More water and more fertilisers

(d) Less water and more fertilizers
Ans. (c)
• HYVs are input-responsive: they express yield
potential only with assured irrigation and
balanced N-P-K; water stress or nutrient
deficits truncate their advantage.
• Require timely split applications of nitrogen
and adequate phosphorus/potassium, plus
secondary/micronutrients (S, Zn, B) based on
soil tests, to sustain tiller density and grain
filling.
• Depend on package of practices: irrigation
scheduling, weed/pest management, seed
rate/row spacing, and harvest timing; yield is
a system outcome, not seeds alone.
• Contrast with traditional landraces that
tolerate low inputs and variable rainfall but
yield less; HYVs trade resilience for high
ceiling yields under managed conditions.
• Green Revolution success hinged on
canal/tube-well expansion, fertilizer
availability/credit, and MSP procurement,
which underwrote farmers’ risk-return
calculus.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q3. Stagflation refers to
(a) Constant rate of inflation
(b) Low inflation with high recession
(c) High inflation with low recession
(d) Stagnation and inflation
Ans. (d)
• Stagnation + inflation: weak/negative growth
and high unemployment coexisting with
elevated prices; breaks the simple Phillips
curve narrative.
• Typically triggered by adverse supply shocks
(e.g., oil price spikes, droughts) that raise
costs and depress output; cost-push inflation
dominates.
• Policy dilemma: tightening demand to curb
inflation worsens unemployment; stimulus to
reduce unemployment fuels inflation;
solution leans on supply-side reforms, energy
diversification, productivity gains.

•

•

Characterized by inflation expectations
becoming unanchored; needs credible
disinflation plus structural measures
(competition, logistics, labor/product
markets).
Historically associated with 1970s oil shocks,
but relevant to any constraint-induced
inflation amid output slack.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q4. Mica is abundantly used in which of the
following
industries?
(a) Cement
(b) Electrical
(c) Plastic
(d) Fire resistance bricks
Ans. (b)
• Muscovite/Phlogopite mica offer high
dielectric strength, low dielectric loss,
thermal stability, cleavage into thin sheets;
ideal for insulation.
• Applications: capacitors, commutators,
insulating washers, switchgear, heater
backings, microwave windows, motor
windings.
• Micanite (mica paper with binders) forms
complex shapes with insulation integrity.
• Secondary uses (paints, plastics, cosmetics)
exploit platelet morphology for barrier
properties and pearlescent effects, but
electrical remains core.
• India’s historical mica belts (e.g., Jharkhand–
Bihar, Rajasthan) supported electroinsulation supply chains.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q5. The rural population of India is about—
percent of the total population
(a) 55
(b) 65
(c) 75
(d) 85
Ans. (b)
• Roughly two-thirds of the population resided
in rural areas during the period referenced,
reflecting an agrarian economy and gradual
urban transition.
• Implies large workforce share in
agriculture/related activities, with
underemployment and seasonality
challenges.
• Policy consequence: prioritize rural roads,
irrigation, health, schools, drinking water,
and non-farm enterprises.
• High rural share shapes demand patterns
(staples, basic consumer goods) and delivery
models (PDS, primary health centers,
cooperatives).
• Urbanization remains incremental, not
explosive, calling for balanced regional
development.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q6. Mixed farming means
(a) Simultaneous cultivation of a number of crops
in a single field
(b) Alternate cropping in a field
(c) Growing fruits and vegetables in the same field
(d) None of these
Ans. (d)
• Mixed farming = crop + livestock system on
one farm for risk diversification, nutrient
cycling (manure), residue utilization (fodder),
and cash-flow smoothing (daily milk/eggs).
• Enhances factor productivity
(land/labor/capital), creates synergies
(draught power, manure composting), and
stabilizes income under weather/price
shocks.
• Options (a) and (b) describe mixed/alternate
cropping, (c) is horticultural intercropping;

•

•

none includes livestock, so no option
captures the definition.
Typical Indian mixed farms: cereals + pulses +
dairy or oilseeds + small ruminants,
leveraging common property resources and
crop residues.
Promotes circular bioeconomy at farm scale,
reducing external input dependence.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q7. Which of the following sectors mobilises
maximum savings?
(a) Corporate sector
(b) Banking sector
(c) Other financial institutions
(d) None of these
Ans. (b)
• Commercial banks are the primary financial
intermediaries channeling household savings
into the formal system via deposits.
• Offer ubiquitous access (branch network),
liquidity, safety (regulation, deposit
insurance), and payments infrastructure,
attracting the widest saver base.
• Mobilize small, frequent savings at scale and
transform into loans/investments, facilitating
financial deepening.
• Corporates are mainly users (not aggregators)
of savings; other FIs (LIC/UTI/NBFCs) mobilize
significantly but less broadly than banks.
• Banking sector’s role is pivotal for public
borrowing, credit creation, and plan
financing through SLR-linked investments.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q8. The steel industry in India is lagging behind its
objectives due to less availability of
(a) Iron ore
(b) Coking coal
(c) Power
(d) Technology
Ans. (b)
• Coking coal (low ash, high coking properties)
is critical for blast furnace–basic oxygen
furnace routes; India has limited reserves,
necessitating imports.
• Import dependence raises input costs, FX
exposure, supply risk, and affects cost
competitiveness vs. coking-coal-rich
countries.
• Encourages shifts to coal
blending/beneficiation, PCI injection, and
gas-based DRI-EAF routes where feasible.
• Constraints persist despite abundant iron ore,
brownfield expansions, and public–private
capacity additions.
• Long-term strategy: energy efficiency, scrap
recovery, logistics cost reduction, R&D for BF
efficiency, and resource security.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q9. The most appropriate source of energy for
cooking and lighting purposes in rural India is
(a) Bio-gas
(b) Solar energy
(c) Nuclear energy
(d) Tidal energy
Ans. (a)
• Biogas from dung/crop residues yields clean
cooking fuel and can power mantle lamps;
addresses both cooking and lighting at
household/community scale.
• Co-benefits: slurry manure improves soil
fertility, reduced indoor air pollution, less
deforestation, women’s time savings.
• Low O&M, local feedstock availability, and
decentralized deployment suit rural contexts.
• Solar excels at lighting, but cooking needs
continuous thermal energy; biogas fits 24×7

•

kitchen demand better than intermittent PV
without costly storage.
Nuclear/tidal are centralized/large-scale and
unsuitable for direct rural household
cooking/lighting.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

•

•

Q10. Antyodaya scheme aims at
(a) To uplift the poorest sections of the society
(b) To provide drinking water to Harijans
(c) To implement reservation policy for Scheduled
Castes and Scheduled Tribes
(d) To allot land to marginal farmers
Ans. (a)
• Anchored in the “last person first” ethos,
targeting poorest of the poor for food
security, livelihoods, and social protection.
• Instruments include subsidized grain (e.g.,
Antyodaya Anna Yojana), asset/livelihood
support, skill upgrading, and service
convergence.
• Improves consumption smoothing, nutrition,
and human capital, breaking
intergenerational poverty.
• Selection via deprivation criteria ensures
progressive targeting where fiscal space is
limited.
• Complements universal schemes by deeper
support to the most vulnerable.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q11. Economy of Brazil is mostly dependent on
(a) Tea
(b) Coffee
(c) Tobacco
(d) Sugar
Ans. (d)
• Sugarcane complex (sugar + ethanol) is a
pillar: Brazil is a leading producer/exporter,
shaping trade balances and energy mix (flexfuel vehicles).
• Ethanol reduces oil import dependence,
supports rural employment, and stabilizes
sugar markets via divert-to-ethanol flexibility.

While coffee is iconic, sugar/ethanol’s
value/volume and macro linkages are
broader.
Agro-export portfolio includes soybeans,
meat, orange juice, tobacco, but sugarcane
ecosystem is central to industrial/agri policy.
Investment in mills, cogeneration (bagasse),
and logistics deepens its systemic
importance.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q12. Match the following :
A. HAL : 1. Coal
B. BHEL : 2. Aeronautical goods
C. SAIL : 3. Electrical goods
D. CIL : 4. Steel
Select the correct answer from the codes below:
ABCD
(a) 2 3 4 1
(b) 3 2 1 4
(c) 1 2 3 4
(d) 4 3 2 1
Ans. (a)
• HAL → Aeronautical goods: airframes,
engines, avionics, MRO for defence/civil
platforms.
• BHEL → Electrical goods: turbines, boilers,
generators, transformers, GIS, and power
plant EPC.
• SAIL → Steel: integrated iron & steel
producer across longs/flats, value-added
steels.
• CIL → Coal: largest coal miner, backbone of
thermal power fuel supply.
• Code maps to 2 3 4 1.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q13. World Bank loan is given to
(a) Make available the currency of a particular
country in case of shortage
(b) Countries for sound projects for development
purposes
(c) Improve the social and cultural conditions of a
country

(d) None of these
Ans. (b)
• IBRD/IDA finance projects/programs in
infrastructure, human development, public
systems, with economic viability and
safeguards.
• Instruments: investment project financing,
program-for-results, development policy
financing, plus technical assistance.
• Targets poverty reduction, shared prosperity,
institutional capacity—not short-term FX
shortages (IMF’s remit).
• Appraisal covers cost–benefit, fiscal
sustainability, environment/social impacts,
procurement and M&E.
• Loans often accompanied by knowledge
transfer and policy dialogue.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q14. The major source of revenue for executing Five
Year Plans is
(a) Direct taxes
(b) Indirect taxes
(c) Foreign loans
(d) Interest on investments
Ans. (b)
• Plan financing relied heavily on budgetary
support sourced from indirect taxes (excise,
customs, sales tax/VAT), especially in earlier
plan eras.
• Broad base, easier administration, and
immediate buoyancy made indirect taxes the
workhorse of revenue.
• Supplemented by market borrowings, small
savings, and external assistance, but taxbuoyancy was centered on indirect heads.
• Direct taxes gained share later; customs
moderated with trade liberalization; GST
modernized the indirect tax base
subsequently.
• Revenue composition shaped public capital
formation and plan outlay profiles.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q15. Price rise goes in favour of those who are
(a) Debtors
(b) Pensioners
(c) Businessmen
(d) Government servants
Ans. (a)
• Inflation reduces the real burden of fixed
nominal debts; debtors repay in cheaper
money.
• Creditors/pensioners on fixed incomes lose
purchasing power unless indexed; DA helps
but with lags.
• Business gains from inflation depend on
pricing power, input contracts, inventory,
and interest costs; not uniformly positive.
• Real interest rates may turn negative, further
favoring borrowers if nominal rates lag
inflation.
• Debt contracts rarely auto-index;
redistribution occurs from lenders to
borrowers.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q16. Which of the following pairs is incorrect?
Five Year Plan : Laid main emphasis on
(a) I : Agriculture and Rural development
(b) II : Expansion of basic and heavy industries
(c) III : Self sufficiency in food and expansion of basic
industries like steel
(d) IV : Political growth of the country
Ans. (d)
• I Plan prioritized
agriculture/irrigation/community
development to address food/security
bottlenecks.

•

•

•

•

II Plan (Nehru–Mahalanobis) emphasized
basic/heavy industry, capital goods, and
import substitution.
III Plan targeted food self-sufficiency,
industry expansion, and infrastructure;
derailed by shocks but emphasis remains as
stated.
IV Plan focused on growth with stability and
self-reliance, price stability, employment—
not “political growth.”
Therefore, IV pairing is incorrect in theme
characterization.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

• Emphasis on agriculture & allied to revive
growth, reduce food imports, and boost rural

Power prioritized to alleviate bottlenecks in

Irrigation/flood control important for yield

Q18. The novelty in the Sixth Plan was
(a) Area planning
(b) Sectoral planning
(c) Rural development
(d) New expansion programme
Ans. (b)
• Shift to sharper sectoral articulation:
agriculture, energy, transport, industry, and
social sectors with monitorable targets.
• Integrated energy policy approach (coal,
power, petroleum), TOR-linked investments,
and efficiency drives.
• Introduced stronger program budgeting,
rolling plans, and mid-term course
corrections anchored in sector outcomes.
• Area planning existed earlier (e.g., command
areas), while the distinct novelty was sector
focus and performance orientation.
• Enhanced resource prioritization, project
pipeline quality, and inter-sector
coordination.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q19. Assertion (A): Our agricultural growth rate is
small even after proper planning and huge
investments.
Reason (R): The land reform measures are not
carried up to the mark
(a) A and R both are incorrect
(b) A is correct but R is incorrect
(c) A and R are correct and R is the correct
explanation of A
(d) A and R are correct but R is not the correct

explanation of A.
Ans. (d)
• A true: growth has been modest/volatile due
to monsoon dependence, low irrigation
coverage in places, input/credit constraints,
and market/price risks.
• R true: land reforms (tenancy regulation,
ceilings, consolidation) were patchy, limiting
farm structure efficiency and tenant
incentives.
• But low growth also stems from extension
gaps, post-harvest/logistics, weak R&D
diffusion, insurance failures, and price
uncertainty.
• Hence R is one factor among many; it does
not fully explain A.
• Multifactor diagnosis means correct
statements, weak causality as framed.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q20. Assertion (A): Though three decades have
passed since Independence, still many people are
living below poverty line in India.
Reason (R): Five-Year Plans have failed to achieve
the targeted growth rate.
(a) A and R both are incorrect
(b) A is correct but R is incorrect
(c) A and R are correct and R is the correct
explanation of A
(d) A and R are correct but R is not the correct
explanation of A.
Ans. (c)
• A true: headcount poverty remained high for
decades, reflecting low per-capita incomes
and limited job creation.
• R true: multiple Plans under-achieved
growth, curbing fiscal space, public
investment, and employment absorption.
• Growth shortfalls directly slowed poverty
reduction, given the elasticity of poverty to
growth; distribution policy helps, but
insufficient growth constrains the resource
envelope.

•

•

Thus R materially contributes to A in the
planning context, even though targeted antipoverty design/implementation also matter.
Empirically, accelerated growth phases
correlate with faster poverty decline.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q21. According to a recent UN report on the state of
world’s population, the growth rate will be zero in
(a) 2000 AD
(b) 2025 AD
(c) 2050 AD
(d) 2095 AD
Ans. (d)
• Zero population growth arises when fertility
≈ replacement and population momentum
has played out.
• UN long-range projections anticipate latecentury convergence as education,
urbanization, female labor force
participation, and health reduce fertility.
• Age structure dynamics delay stabilization
even after fertility falls; cohorts age through.
• 2095 aligns with a scenario where global
growth approximates zero; earlier dates are
too soon given momentum.
• Has profound implications for dependency
ratios, pensions, and labor markets.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

(a) 133 million tonnes

market off-take.

Output diversification across
paddy/wheat/coarse cereals plus pulses
lifted the aggregate.
Record near 150 MT signaled food security
gains and reduced import dependence.
Set a higher baseline for subsequent plan

Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q23. Maximum revenue is being earned from
(a) Excise
(b) Non-tax revenue
(c) Customs
(d) Corporation Tax
Ans. (d)
• Corporation tax emerged as a leading
revenue source with profit
growth/formalization, overtaking traditional
excise/customs in buoyancy.
• Trade liberalization reduced customs shares;
excise evolved toward VAT/GST, changing
incidence and pooling.
• Non-tax revenues (dividends, fees, spectrum,
royalties) are episodic; corporate tax offers
stable buoyancy with economic expansion.
• Revenue structure reflects a shift toward
direct taxes, improving progressivity and
elasticity.
• Supports plan and non-plan spending with
predictable flows.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q2. Which of the following Five-Year Plans was
terminated one year before its completion?
(a) Second
(b) Third
(c) Fourth
(d) Fifth
Ans. (d)
• Fifth Five-Year Plan (1974–79) was
terminated in 1977–78 by the Janata
Government.
• Replaced by a Rolling Plan (1978–83) to allow
flexibility in annual targets.
• Later, after the fall of the Janata government,
Indira Gandhi restored 1978–79 to the Fifth
Plan.
• The plan initially focused on poverty removal
and self-reliance.
• Its termination marked a shift in planning
approach.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

(d) None of these

•

Q4. In India, the rise in food production between

production.

Q8. Which of the following is most suitable for the

• Big dams are economically unviable for such
scale.
• Widely used in remote Himalayan and NorthEastern regions.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q12. Which of the following pairs is/are incorrect?
I. Consumer Price Index - Inflation
II. Export concessions - Fiscal policy
III. Imposition of taxes - Economic planning
IV. Income - Standard of living
V. Deficit budgeting - Inflation
Select the correct answer from the codes given below:
(a) I and V
(b) II and III
(c) III and V
(d) None of (I), (II) and (III) is a correct answer
Ans. (c)
• I: CPI measures inflation → correct.
• II: Export concessions are fiscal tools →
correct.
• III: Taxation belongs to fiscal policy, not
planning → incorrect.
• IV: Income reflects living standards, though
not alone → acceptable.
• V: Deficit budgeting doesn't inherently cause
inflation; depends on monetization & supply
→ treated incorrect here.
• Therefore, III and V are incorrect.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1986
Q1. According to the Livestock Census of 1982 in
India, the ratio of the cattle population to the buffalo
population was approximately
(a) 1 : 2
(b) 1 : 3

The Livestock Census 1982 reported a cattle
to buffalo ratio of 2:3.
Reflects the significant presence of buffaloes,
particularly in northern and western India.
Buffaloes are preferred for higher milk fat
content, making them economically valuable.
Cattle dominate in eastern and southern
India, whereas buffaloes dominate in Punjab,
Haryana, UP.
This ratio influenced dairy policies and
Operation Flood strategies.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q2. India earns maximum foreign exchange from the
export of
(a) Gems and jewellery
(b) Non-ferrous base metals
(c) Textile fabrics including cotton and man-made
fibres
(d) Tea
Ans. (a)
• Gems and jewellery sector has historically
been India’s largest forex earner.
• India contributes nearly 29% of global
jewellery consumption.
• Home to 300,000+ players and employs over
2.5 million people.
• India is the world’s largest diamond cutting
and polishing centre.
• Strong export promotion councils and
modernization have boosted
competitiveness.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q3. The Prevention of Food Adulteration Act came
into force in
(a) 1954
(b) 1955
(c) 1964
(d) 1976
Ans. (a)

•
•
•

•
•

Enacted in 1954 to ensure pure and
wholesome food for consumers.
Aimed to prevent adulteration and fraud in
food products.
Amended in 1964, 1976, and 1986 to make
punishments stricter and involve consumer
groups.
Forms the basis of India's food safety
regulation before FSSA 2006.
Strengthened legal powers for inspection,
testing, and prosecution.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q4. Match the columns:
A. Varanasi → 1. Indian Rare Earths Ltd.
B. Koyali → 2. Hindustan Machine Tools Ltd.
C. Pinjore → 3. Diesel Locomotive Works
D. Alwaye → 4. Petrochemical complex
Select the correct answer using the codes given
below:
ABCD
(a) 4 3 2 1
(b) 3 4 2 1
(c) 1 2 3 4
(d) 1 2 4 3
Ans. (b)
• Varanasi – Diesel Locomotive Works (DLW)
manufactures diesel-electric locomotives.
• Koyali – Petrochemical Complex, a major
refinery in Gujarat.
• Pinjore – Hindustan Machine Tools Ltd.,
known for precision machinery.
• Alwaye – Indian Rare Earths Ltd., dealing with
minerals and rare earths.

•

This reflects India’s post-independence
industrial diversification.
