Q5. Standard of living of a nation can be judged by
(a) Increase in GNP at factor cost
(b) Increase in GNP at cost including taxes and duties
(c) Increase in NNP at factor cost
(d) None of the above
Ans. (d)
• Standard of living goes beyond just income
indicators.
• It includes education, healthcare,
employment quality, infrastructure, housing,
and leisure.
• Pure economic indicators like GNP/NNP don’t
fully capture distribution, access, or quality of
life.
• Modern measures use HDI, MPI, or composite
indices.
• So none of the given options alone
determines standard of living
comprehensively.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q6. The important function of taxation in India is to
(a) Reduce black money
(b) Generate funds for Government expenditure
(c) Reduce inflation
(d) None of the above
Ans. (b)
• Primary objective is mobilizing resources to
fund public goods and development.
• Supports infrastructure, social welfare,
defence, and governance.
• Secondary objectives include redistribution
and economic regulation.
• Taxation plays a central role in India’s
planned development model.
• While it can help curb inflation or black
money indirectly, its main function is revenue
generation.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q7. Direct taxation was introduced by the ____
committee?
(a) Wanchoo
(b) Chetty
(c) Kaldor
(d) Raja Chelliah
Ans. (d)
• Raja Chelliah Committee (1991–93) laid the
framework for modern direct tax reforms.
• Suggested rationalization of tax rates,
widening the base, VAT, and
computerization.
• Led to reduction in marginal tax rates and
corporate tax rationalization.
• Integrated customs, excise, and service taxes,
paving way for GST.
• Considered a turning point in India’s fiscal
reforms.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q8. Operation Flood II refers to
(a) Krishna–Cauvery link
(b) Cleaning of Ganga water
(c) Decreasing floods in the country
(d) None of the above
Ans. (d)
• Operation Flood II (1981–85) expanded milksheds from 18 to 136, and urban markets to
290 towns.
• Strengthened village milk cooperatives and
self-reliant dairy networks.
• Increased domestic milk powder production
massively.
• Funded through EEC grants and World Bank
loans.
• Core part of India’s White Revolution, making
India the largest milk producer.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q9. Mixed farming means
(a) Growing two crops at one time
(b) Growing different crops in succession
(c) Using different soils
(d) Agriculture involving crops and livestock
Ans. (d)

•
•
•
•
•

Mixed farming involves simultaneous crop
cultivation and livestock rearing.
Provides diversified income, better resource
use, and natural manure recycling.
Stabilizes farm income by reducing risk from
crop failure.
Common in Punjab, Haryana, and many
traditional rural systems.
Integral to sustainable agriculture practices.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q10. Which of the following statements about
Kalpakkam Atomic Power Plant is/are true?
1. It used for the first time the uraniumplutonium monocarbide fuel
2. It can generate up to 3,50,000 MW of power
3. It used the enriched uranium-plutonium oxide
fuel
4. Only two other countries have the distinction
of having a centre for atomic research like the
one at Kalpakkam
(a) 1, 2, 4
(b) 1, 2, 3 and 4
(c) 1, 2, 3
(d) 1 and 2 only
Ans. (b)
• All statements are correct regarding
Kalpakkam.
• It’s a comprehensive nuclear complex
including reactors, reprocessing, and fast
breeder programmes.
• First use of uranium–plutonium monocarbide
fuel for fast breeder research.
• It has significant power capacity and research
uniqueness, shared by only a few countries.
• Represents India’s indigenous nuclear
technology capability.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q11. Rotation of crops means
(a) Different crops are grown in succession to
maintain the soil fertility
(b) Growing two crops at the same time
(c) Same crop is grown after some time

(d) None of the above
Ans. (a)
• Crop rotation involves planned sequence of
different crops on the same land.
• Maintains soil fertility by balancing nutrient
use and replenishment.
• Helps in controlling pests and weeds and
reducing erosion.
• Leguminous crops restore nitrogen, cereals
utilize it.
• A key sustainable agricultural practice.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q12. In 1984–85, the approximate percentage of
energy produced from the following sources was
Thermal | Hydel | Nuclear
(a) 65% 34% 1%
(b) 63% 34.5% 2.5%
(c) 60% 32.5% 7.5%
(d) 61% 35% 3.5%
Ans. (b)
• Thermal power dominated (63%), due to
abundant coal-based plants.
• Hydel power (34.5%) significant in hilly
regions like Himachal, Uttarakhand.
• Nuclear power (2.5%) was emerging, limited
to few installations.
• Reflects the energy mix before liberalization.
• Thermal remained the backbone of electricity
generation.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q13. The maximum cropped area of the country is in
(a) Maharashtra
(b) Punjab

(c) Madhya Pradesh
(d) Uttar Pradesh
Ans. (b)
• Punjab has maximum cropped intensity and

• Efficient land use and intensive farming led to
high cropping ratios.

policies.

Q14. The areas in India that receive approximately
an average of more than 200 cm of rainfall annually
are
(a) Meghalaya, Assam, Nagaland, Arunachal Pradesh
(b) Orissa, Madhya Pradesh, Gujarat, Maharashtra
(c) Meghalaya, Assam, Rajasthan, Jammu & Kashmir
(d) Meghalaya, Delhi, Punjab, Rajasthan
Ans. (a)
• Meghalaya, esp. Mawsynram & Cherrapunji,
receive world’s highest rainfall.
• Assam, Nagaland, Arunachal Pradesh also
receive very heavy monsoonal rainfall.
• Due to orographic effects of hills intercepting
monsoon winds.
• Supports dense forests, shifting cultivation,
and unique ecosystems.
• Other options include regions with much less
rainfall.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q15. Assertion: Sex ratio in India is in favour of males
Reason: In females there is a high rate of mortality
during maternity.
(a) if A and R both are correct and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) if A and R both are correct but R is not the correct
explanation of A
(c) if A is correct and R is incorrect
(d) if A is incorrect and R is correct
Ans. (a)

•

•
•
•
•

Sex ratio in India is skewed towards males —
historically less than 950 females per 1000
males.
High female mortality, especially during
maternity and childbirth, is a major factor.
Other factors include gender bias, female
infanticide, and health neglect.
Maternal mortality rates were high due to
lack of healthcare access.
Both A and R are correct, and R explains A.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1987

Q1. Which of the following pairs is correct?
(a) IDBI- Short-term financing
(b) NABARD- Industrial financing
(c) RBI- Long-term financing
(d) RRB- Dispensing rural credit
Ans. (d)
• RRBs (Regional Rural Banks) were created
(from 1975) to provide credit and services in
rural areas to small & marginal farmers, agrilabourers, artisans, micro-entrepreneurs.
• IDBI was primarily an apex development
finance institution for industry (long/medium-term), not short-term.
• NABARD focuses on agriculture and rural
development (refinance to
cooperatives/RRBs, rural infra), not general
industrial finance.
• RBI is the central bank; it does not provide
long-term project finance; it lends short-term
liquidity to banks and manages monetary
policy.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q2. India’s contribution to Mathematics includes
1. Number system
2. Decimal system
3. Concept of zero
Select the correct answer using the codes given
below:
(a) 1, 2 & 3
(b) 1 & 2

(c) 2 & 3
(d) 1 & 3
Ans. (a)
• Hindu–Arabic numerals and the place-value
decimal system evolved in India and spread
via the Islamic world to Europe.
• The concept and symbol of ‘zero’ (śūnya)
were formalized by Indian mathematicians
(e.g., Brahmagupta, 7th c.).
• These innovations enabled positional
arithmetic, algebraic developments, and later
calculus advances globally.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q3. Crude oil was exported from India in 1985
because
(a) Of production surplus
(b) Alternative source of energy has been developed
in India
(c) Import of petroleum products was much cheaper
in comparison to the refined oil in India
(d) Of insufficient refining capacity in India
Ans. (d)
• India had limited domestic refining capacity
relative to crude availability and product
demand patterns.
• Export of some crude occurred while refined
products were imported, reflecting capacity
and configuration gaps.
• The era was marked by BoP stress (second oil
shock, 1979; Iran–Iraq war disruptions),
intensifying import dependence.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q4. Which of the following statements is not correct
for economically underdeveloped countries?
(a) Involvement of a very little proportion of labour in
primary occupations
(b) Lower percentage of literacy
(c) Mass unemployment
(d) High birth rate
Ans. (a)
• In underdeveloped economies, a large share
of labour is in primary sector (agriculture &
allied), not “very little”.

•

Such economies typically show lower literacy,
high unemployment/underemployment, high
fertility & dependency ratios.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q5. Mixed cropping is useful when
(a) Crops with varying maturity periods are sown
together.
(b) Crops with same maturity period are sown
together.
(c) Different soils are used for the same crops.
(d) Crops with different nutritional requirements are
grown.
Ans. (a)
• Mixed cropping/intercropping with staggered
maturity spreads risk, utilizes resources
across seasons, and ensures continuous
ground cover.
• Different maturity reduces simultaneous peak
water/nutrient demand and harvest-time
bottlenecks.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q6. Largest allocation in the Seventh Five- Year Plan
has been given to
(a) Agriculture
(b) Transport
(c) Education
(d) Energy
Ans. (c)
• The Seventh Plan (1985–90) stressed human
resource development and social justice,
leading to high allocations to education
(including Navodaya Vidyalayas, literacy).
• Energy and agriculture remained priorities,
but education saw the largest allocation
thrust in social sector terms to underpin long-

term growth.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q7. Deficit Financing means
(a) Difference of total expenditure and income by
revenue from all sources.
(b) Government spends in excess of revenues so that
a budget deficit is incurred which is financed by the
borrowings.
(c) Difference in borrowing and external and internal
resources.
(d) Capital expenditure on items of public
construction, public enterprises and public
borrowings.
Ans. (b)
• Deficit financing: when expenditure exceeds
revenue, the gap is financed by
borrowing/monetization.
• Used to support plan outlays, smooth cycles;
excessive use risks inflation and crowding
out.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q8. Which of the following is the correct group of
Kharif crops?
(a) Rice, Millet, Maize, Cotton
(b) Groundnut, Bajra, Barley, Sorghum, Wheat
(c) Jowar, Bajra, Rice, Cotton, Jute, Gram
(d) Wheat, Barley, Gram, Mustard
Ans. (a)
• Kharif (monsoon) crops: Rice, Maize,
Jowar/Sorghum, Bajra/Millet, Cotton,
Groundnut, Jute, Soybean.
• Rabi (winter) crops include Wheat, Barley,
Gram, Mustard (rules out (d)).
• (a) lists classic kharif crops together.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q9. Which of the following is true?
(a) Physical planning is more important than financial
planning
(b) Financial planning is more important than physical
planning
(c) Physical planning and financial planning are equally
important
(d) Physical planning and financial planning are
complementary.
Ans. (d)
• Physical planning (targets, capacity, projects)
and financial planning (resources, budgeting)
are interdependent.
• Effective plans require coherence between
physical objectives and financial feasibility—
they are complementary.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q10. Which of the following is the correct ascending
order of contribution to domestic savings?
(a) Household sector, Corporate sector, Private sector,
Public enterprises
(b) Corporate sector, Household sector, Public
enterprises, Government and Public enterprises
(c) Government and Public enterprises, Public
enterprises, Household sector, Corporate sector
(d) Public enterprises, Corporate sector, Government
and Public enterprises, Household sector.
Ans. (a)
• Historically in India, households contribute
the largest to savings, followed by private
corporate, then public sector/government.
• The order presented in (a) is ascending
(smallest to largest) across the items as
grouped.
• Reflects dominance of household financial &
physical savings.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q13. Which of the following industries was affected
most due to partition?
(a) Cotton and jute
(b) Small scale industries
(c) Steel industries
(d) Tea and cotton
Ans. (a)

Bengal/East Pakistan) from jute mills (West
Bengal), severely disrupting supply chains.
• Cotton trade/logistics were also impacted
across Punjab/Sindh.
• This dual hit makes cotton & jute the most
affected.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

• Reflects hydrocarbon windfalls vs diversified
advanced economies.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

20. Assertion (A) : Five-Year Plans form the basis of
Indian economic planning.

(b) Both A and R are correct but R is not the correct

(c) A is correct but R is incorrect
(d) A is incorrect but R is correct

strategy (till 2017) was anchored in Five-Year
Plans.

outlays, targets), hence FYPs.
• The Soviet model influence explains the FYP
basis.

(d) 1969

monetary policy, FX management, financial
stability.

UPSC PYQs - 1988
Q1. Out of the following industries in India, the
maximum number of workers are employed in
(a) Jute
(b) Sugar
(c) Textiles
(d) Iron and Steel
Ans. (d)
• The iron and steel industry involves a large
number of direct and indirect workers,
including mining, transportation, processing,
and allied services.
• Large integrated steel plants like Bhilai,
Durgapur, Rourkela and Bokaro employ
thousands directly.
• The backward and forward linkages (e.g.,
coal, limestone, engineering goods) generate
additional indirect employment.
• In comparative industrial statistics of the
period, iron and steel employed more
workers than jute, sugar or textiles.
• Hence option (d) is correct.
Q2. The rate of exchange of Indian rupee is linked
with
(a) Dollar
(b) Sterling/Pound
(c) A basket of foreign currencies
(d) Yen
Ans. (c)

•

•
•
•
•

India shifted from a single currency peg to a
currency basket system to better reflect trade
patterns.
This helped reduce volatility arising from
fluctuations in any one currency.
The basket approach was introduced in the
late 1970s–80s to stabilise external payments.
It includes major currencies like USD, GBP,
JPY, EUR in weighted form.
Hence option (c) is correct.

Q3. Which one of the following bodies finalises the
Five-Year Plans proposals?
(a) Planning Commission
(b) Union Cabinet
(c) National Development Council
(d) Ministry of Planning
Ans. (c)
• The Planning Commission prepared draft
plans.
• The Union Cabinet approved the draft.
• The National Development Council (NDC) —
comprising the Prime Minister, Union
Ministers, and Chief Ministers — gave the
final approval.
• The NDC ensured Centre–State coordination
and federal participation in plan finalisation.
• Hence option (c) is correct.

Q4. By sex ratio, we mean
(a) The number of females in the population per 1000
male population.
(b) The number of males in the population per 1000
female population.

(c) The number of married females in the population
per 1000 male population.
(d) The number of married males in the population
per 1000 female population.
Ans. (a)
• Sex ratio = females per 1000 males, as per
Indian census usage.
• It reflects gender balance in a population.
• This indicator is influenced by mortality rates,
migration patterns, and discrimination.
• The 2011 Census recorded 940 females per
1000 males, showing slight improvement from
2001.
• Hence option (a) is correct.

Q5. An ad valorem duty is a tax on the basis of
(a) The price of a commodity
(b) The value added
(c) The advertisement expenditure
(d) The unit of the commodity
Ans. (b)
• “Ad valorem” means “according to value”.
• Tax is charged on the value added, not on
quantity.
• It is typically used in VAT and excise
structures.
• This system ensures proportional taxation as
prices change.
• Hence option (b) is correct.
Q6. About 1.5 crore people are added every year to
the total population of India. This means that we are
adding every year a population equal to that of
(a) Bangladesh
(b) Singapore
(c) Pakistan

(d) Afghanistan
Ans. (d)
• In the 1980s, Afghanistan’s population was
around 2 crore, similar to India’s annual
population addition.
• Bangladesh and Pakistan had much larger
populations; Singapore was too small.
• This comparison highlights the massive
demographic pressure India faced annually.
• Hence option (d) is correct.
Q7. Which one of the following has the lowest
density of population per sq km according to the
1981 census?
(a) Meghalaya
(b) Nagaland
(c) Sikkim
(d) Arunachal Pradesh
Ans. (d)
• Arunachal Pradesh has the lowest population
density due to its large area and hilly terrain.
• Other Northeastern states have low densities
but higher than Arunachal.
• Sparse settlement and difficult geography are
key reasons.
• Hence option (d) is correct.
Q8. Among the sources of energy listed below which
one is non-conventional in present day rural India?
(a) Fuel wood
(b) Cow-dung cake
(c) Biogas
(d) Hydel
Ans. (c)
• Biogas is classified as non-conventional
because it’s a modern renewable energy
source.
• Fuel wood and cow-dung are traditional fuels.
• Hydel is conventional, grid-based.
• Biogas plants provide clean energy and
manure, reducing dependence on forests.
• Hence option (c) is correct.
Q9. Assume that the consumer price index (1970 =
100) is at present 500. This means that

(a) Prices of all goods and commodities have
increased five times.
(b) Prices of all consumer goods have increased five
times.
(c) Prices of all items in a specified list have increased
by 400%.
(d) Prices have increased and the weighted mean
price increase for specified items is 400%.
Ans. (c)
• CPI 500 means prices have increased by 400%
over base year.
• It measures change in weighted average
prices of a fixed basket of goods.
• It doesn’t apply to all goods universally, only
those in the specified list.
• Hence option (c) is correct.
Q10. A private company is faced with the problem of
setting up a cement plant. Which of the factors given
below should be taken into consideration for this
venture?
1. Abundant electricity
2. Abundant limestone/dolomite
3. Abundant labour force
4. Abundant gas
5. Accessibility to transportation
6. Abundant coal
Choose the correct answer from the codes given
below:
(a) 1, 2 and 3 only
(b) 1, 2 and 4 only
(c) 1, 2 and 5 only
(d) 3, 4 and 6 only
Ans. (c)
• Limestone/dolomite is the primary raw
material for cement manufacture, so
availability is crucial.
• Electricity is essential for running crushers,
kilns, and grinding units.
• Transport connectivity (rail/road) is critical
for movement of bulky raw materials and
finished cement.
• Labour needs are relatively low due to
mechanisation.

•
•

Gas is not a necessary input in cement
production.
Hence option (c) is correct.

Q11. Which of the following are under the purview
of Industrial Development Bank of India?
1. Unit Trust of India
2. Life Insurance Corporation of India
3. Export-Import Bank
4. State Finance Corporation of India
Choose the correct answer from the codes given
below:
(a) 1, 2 and 3
(b) 2, 3 and 4
(c) 1, 2 and 4
(d) 1, 3 and 4
Ans. (d)
• IDBI acts as a financial apex institution
coordinating various development finance
agencies.
• UTI, EXIM Bank and State Finance
Corporations were linked to IDBI for financial
and policy coordination.
• LIC is an independent statutory corporation
and not under IDBI’s purview.
• IDBI provided refinance, guarantees, and
credit to these bodies.
• Hence option (d) is correct.
Q12. Which one of the following is NOT an affiliate of
the Reserve Bank of India?
(a) Agricultural Refinance Corporation
(b) Deposit Insurance Corporation
(c) The Industrial Development Bank of India
(d) Unit Trust of India
Ans. (d)
• UTI was created through a separate
Parliamentary Act (1963).
• It is a public financial institution, not an
affiliate of the RBI.
• Agricultural Refinance Corporation, DIC, and
IDBI were all established under the RBI’s aegis
initially.
• Hence option (d) is correct.

Q13. One of the elements mentioned as the strategy
for industrial development in the Seventh Five-Year
Plan is to usher in ‘sunrise industries’. ‘Sunrise
industries’ refers to those industries which have
(a) High growth potential and relevance to our needs
(b) High employment potential
(c) Capacity to face international competition
(d) High import substitution potential
Ans. (a)
• Sunrise industries are new and emerging
sectors expected to dominate in future.
• They are characterised by rapid growth,
innovation, and strategic importance.
• Examples: electronics, biotechnology,
information technology, renewable energy.
• Seventh Plan emphasised nurturing these for
self-reliance and global competitiveness.
• Hence option (a) is correct.

Q14. Mechanisation of agriculture will lead to which
of the following?
1. Higher per unit cost of operation
2. Higher per unit productivity
3. Higher dose of employment
4. Higher utilisation of resources
Select the correct answer from codes given below:
(a) 1 and 4
(b) 1 and 2
(c) 3 and 4
(d) 1, 2 and 3
Ans. (b)
• Mechanisation often increases fixed costs,
leading to higher per unit operational cost
initially.

•

It also leads to higher per unit productivity
due to efficiency in ploughing, sowing,
harvesting.
It usually reduces employment, not increases.
It may not automatically increase resource
utilisation unless integrated with planning.
Hence option (b) is correct.

• RRBs function under NABARD supervision.

for more and more markets.

(b) Both A and R are true but R is NOT a correct
explanation of A
(c) A is true but R is false
(d) A is false but R is true

India became a victim of British colonialism
during the 19th century.
The Industrial Revolution in Britain created a
demand for raw materials and markets.
India was turned into a supplier of raw
materials and a market for British goods.
This is a direct explanation of colonial
exploitation.

Q19. Which one of the following commodities has
the highest production in India?
(a) Rice
(b) Wheat
(c) Tobacco
(d) Gram
Ans. (a)
• Rice is India’s largest produced crop in terms
of volume and area.
• It is a staple food for majority of Indians,
grown in diverse agro-climatic zones.
• India ranks among the top producers and
exporters of rice globally.
• Wheat ranks second; tobacco and gram are
comparatively much lower.
• Hence option (a) is correct.
Q20. Even when our foodgrain production has
increased from 50 to 150 million tonnes during the
last three decades, the progress has been quite
unsatisfactory on agricultural development front
because
(a) Only the production of a few items has gone up
largely.
(b) Only the production of pulses and edible oil seeds
has recorded large increase.
(c) Nutritional diet and 16 oz norm is still not available
to our growing number of people having low
purchasing power.
(d) This has been achieved on the basis of huge
quantities of imported inputs which cannot be
sustained.
Ans. (a)
• The increase in foodgrain production was
largely due to Green Revolution crops like
wheat and rice.
• Pulses, coarse cereals, oilseeds saw limited
productivity growth.

Regional imbalances persisted — growth was
concentrated in Punjab, Haryana, western
UP.
Hence overall agricultural development
remained narrow-based and skewed.
Hence option (a) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1989

Q1.The beginning of urbanisation of the Ganges from
the 6th century was mainly attributed to
(a) Fertility of soil
(b) Trade improvement
(c) Population growth
(d) None of the above
Ans. (a)
• The Gangetic plains are among the most
fertile regions in the world due to the alluvial
soil deposited by rivers like the Ganga,
Yamuna, and their tributaries.
• This fertility supported surplus agricultural
production, which is a precondition for
urbanisation — surplus allows people to
engage in non-agricultural professions.
• 6th century BCE saw the rise of
Mahajanapadas, which were largely
concentrated in the Ganga plains because of
this fertile environment.
• The surplus grain enabled trade, craft
specialisation, and administrative centres,
leading to the emergence of towns and cities
like Vaishali, Rajagriha, and Pataliputra.
• Improved transportation through navigable
rivers further facilitated the movement of
goods and people, accelerating urbanisation.
• Archaeological excavations show a significant
rise in urban settlements and material
culture in this period.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q2.Recently India suffered unfavourable balance of
trade due to
(a) Import of oil and minerals
(b) Import of oil and foodgrains
(c) Import of oil and machines

(d) Import of oil and fertilisers
Ans. (a)
• India’s import bill rose sharply due to the
rising international oil prices and higher
mineral imports, widening the trade deficit.
• Petroleum crude and mineral fuels have
historically been the largest share of India’s
imports, making the trade balance vulnerable
to price shocks.
• The 1970s–80s oil shocks led to sustained
CAD (Current Account Deficit) issues for India.
• High dependence on imported crude oil
(around 70–80%) made India’s trade deficit
structurally unfavourable.
• Minerals such as fertilizers, metallic ores, and
certain industrial inputs were also imported in
large quantities due to insufficient domestic
availability.
• During this period, India’s export base
remained narrow, worsening the BoT
(Balance of Trade) position further.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q3.Which of the following is the most industrialised
country?
(a) Bangladesh
(b) Malaysia
(c) Nepal
(d) Singapore
Ans. (d)
• Singapore is known for its highly developed
industrial economy, making it the most
industrialised among the given options.
• It has a strategic location along major
shipping routes, making it a global trade and
logistics hub.
• The country has focused on high-value
industries like electronics, petrochemicals,
pharmaceuticals, and precision engineering.
• With stable governance, advanced
infrastructure, and liberal trade policies,
Singapore attracted massive FDI, boosting its
industrial growth.
• By the 1980s, Singapore had already
transformed from a port-based economy to a

manufacturing and financial powerhouse in
Asia.
• Its per capita income and industrial output
far exceed those of Malaysia, Nepal, or
Bangladesh.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q4.Real owners of the company are
(a) Debenture holders
(b) Directors of the company
(c) Equity shareholders
(d) Bearer bond holders
Ans. (c)
• Equity shareholders are the actual owners of
a company as they hold ownership interest.
• They have voting rights, allowing them to
participate in decision-making, elect
directors, and influence company policy.
• They receive dividends based on profits and
have residual claim on assets if the company
is liquidated after all obligations.
• Debenture holders and bondholders are
creditors, not owners — they receive fixed
interest and repayment of principal, but no
ownership or control.
• Directors are responsible for managing the
company on behalf of shareholders, not
owners themselves.
• The concept of limited liability applies to
shareholders, meaning their risk is limited to
their investment amount.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q5.Which of the following agricultural holdings have
a largest percentage in India?
(a) Marginal holdings
(b) Small holdings
(c) Medium holdings
(d) Large holdings
Ans. (a)
• Marginal holdings are defined as less than 1
hectare of land, and they form the largest
share of landholdings in India.

•

As per Agricultural Census, marginal farmers
account for around 65–70% of total
operational holdings.
• This reflects the fragmented landholding
structure of Indian agriculture.
• Historical factors like inheritance laws,
population pressure, and lack of land reforms
led to division into small plots.
• Marginal farmers often face issues like low
productivity, limited access to credit, and
lack of economies of scale.
• Small holdings (1–2 ha) form the second
largest group, while medium and large
holdings are minimal.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q6.The structural changes in exports means
(a) Change in the commodities of export.
(b) Increasing exports along with the imports.
(c) Increasing imports.
(d) Change in the commodities of imports
Ans. (a)
• Structural change in exports refers to a shift
in the composition of goods or services that a
country exports over time.
• For example, if a country moves from
exporting raw materials (like cotton, iron ore)
to manufactured or value-added products
(like textiles, steel), it indicates structural
transformation.
• It reflects changes in industrial capacity,
technological advancement, and economic
diversification.
• In India’s case, the post-Independence period
witnessed a gradual shift from primary goods
to manufactured and service exports.
• Structural change is different from mere
volume increase — it focuses on what is being
exported rather than how much.
• This shift also shows economic maturity, as
more advanced economies export processed
or high-tech goods.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q7.The Ministry of Industries takes the
administrative advice regarding the improvement of
the performance of public enterprises from
(a) Bureau of Public Enterprises
(b) MRTP Commission
(c) Estimates Committee
(d) Planning Commission
Ans. (a)
• The Bureau of Public Enterprises (BPE) is the
nodal agency for policy formulation and
coordination concerning public sector
undertakings (PSUs).
• It provides administrative and managerial
guidance to improve efficiency, profitability,
and accountability of PSUs.
• Functions of BPE include:
o Implementing policies on
privatisation and commercialisation.
o Advising on capital restructuring,
management, and post-transaction
monitoring of enterprises.
o Overseeing financial discipline and
performance agreements between
government and enterprises.
o Preparing enterprises for
privatisation and advising NCP
(National Council on Privatisation).
• BPE serves as the secretariat to the NCP,
ensuring smooth coordination between
ministries and enterprises.
• Other bodies like MRTP and Planning
Commission have different roles — MRTP
deals with monopolies/trade practices, and
Planning Commission with overall planning,
not day-to-day enterprise performance.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q8.Which of the following iron & steel plants is
closer to coal deposits than iron ore deposits?
(a) Bhilai & Bokaro
(b) Jamshedpur & Bhadrawati
(c) Burnpur & Hirapur
(d) Durgapur & Rourkela
Ans. (a)

•

Bhilai and Bokaro were strategically located
closer to coal reserves, which are essential for
iron and steel production.
• Bokaro Steel Plant is located in the Damodar
Valley, a major coal belt region, reducing
transport costs for fuel.
• Bhilai Steel Plant, located in Chhattisgarh, is
close to Singrauli coal fields and also benefits
from proximity to Korba coal fields.
• Iron ore, though essential, is less expensive to
transport compared to coal — hence, plants
were often set up near coalfields.
• Energy (from coal) is a key input in blast
furnaces, making coal proximity more critical
than iron ore proximity.
• Plants like Jamshedpur, on the other hand,
are closer to iron ore mines of Singhbhum.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q9. The first Trade Union in India was
(a) Madras Labour Union
(b) Bombay Postal Union
(c) Printers Union of Calcutta
(d) All India Trade Union Congress
Ans. (a)
• The Madras Labour Union (MLU), founded in
1918 by B.P. Wadia, is regarded as India’s
first registered trade union.
• It emerged in response to poor working
conditions and exploitation in Buckingham
and Carnatic Mills in Madras.
• The union organized workers, negotiated
with employers, and laid the foundation for
trade unionism in India.
• The All India Trade Union Congress (AITUC)
was formed later in 1920 as a federation of
several trade unions.
• MLU's formation was part of the larger labour
awakening in India during colonial times,
influenced by both nationalist movements
and global labour trends.
• The union played a pivotal role in spreading
the idea of collective bargaining and workers’
rights.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q10.The ‘Interest Rate Policy’ is a component of
(a) Fiscal Policy
(b) Monetary Policy
(c) Trade Policy
(d) Direct Control
Ans. (b)
• Interest Rate Policy is a tool used in monetary
policy, which is managed by the Reserve Bank
of India (RBI).
• It involves setting repo rate, reverse repo,
bank rate, CRR, etc., to control money supply,
credit availability, and inflation.
• By increasing interest rates, RBI can curb
inflation by making borrowing more
expensive; lowering rates stimulates
economic activity.
• Interest rate changes also affect investment,
exchange rates, and capital flows.
• Fiscal policy, in contrast, deals with
government revenue and expenditure, not
interest rates.
• Interest rate policy is thus a quantitative tool
of monetary control.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q11.A suitable site of a cement plant requires
proximity to
(a) Coal and iron
(b) Limestone and manganese
(c) Limestone and gypsum
(d) Coal and gypsum
Ans. (c)
• Limestone is the primary raw material for
cement manufacture, making up about 60–
65% of the mix.
• Gypsum is used as a setting retarder in
cement, essential for controlling the
hardening time.
• Cement plants are therefore located near
limestone and gypsum deposits to reduce
transportation costs.
• Coal is needed as fuel, but it can be
transported economically, unlike limestone
which is needed in large volumes.

•

Proximity to raw materials ensures cost
efficiency, steady supply, and reliable
production.
• Example: Chhattisgarh, Andhra Pradesh,
Madhya Pradesh are major cement-producing
regions due to limestone abundance.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q12.Who among the following is most benefitted
from inflation?
(a) Creditors
(b) Debtors
(c) Savings bank account holders
(d) Government pensioners
Ans. (b)
• During inflation, the value of money
decreases over time.
• Debtors benefit because they repay loans in
money that is worth less than when they
borrowed it.
• For example, if a debtor takes a ₹1,00,000
loan when inflation is 3% and repays after a
year when inflation is 10%, the real value of
repayment is lower.
• Creditors lose because the money they
receive has less purchasing power.
• Fixed income groups, like pensioners or
savings account holders, also lose during
inflation as their income doesn’t rise with
prices.
• This is why inflation tends to redistribute
wealth from creditors to debtors.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q13.Which sector of development has the highest
percentage of the total outlay in the public sector?
(a) Agriculture
(b) Industry
(c) Energy
(d) Defence
Ans. (d)
• In India, the defence sector has consistently
received the highest share of public sector
outlay, reflecting its strategic importance.

•

Defence is entirely under government
ownership — private participation was
restricted until the 2000s, making it a core
public sector domain.
• High expenditure goes into modernisation of
armed forces, procurement of arms and
equipment, salaries, pensions, infrastructure,
and R&D.
• Defence allocation is considered non-plan
expenditure but occupies a large share of
total budget, surpassing agriculture or
industry.
• In the late 1980s, India was modernising its
forces (e.g., INS Viraat induction, MiG aircraft
procurement), leading to large defence
capital outlays.
• Sectors like agriculture and energy receive
significant shares but defence remains
dominant in terms of total public sector
outlay due to security imperatives.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q14.HUDCO aims at providing
(a) Low cost sanitation
(b) Loan finance for housing and urban development
(c) Rural development
(d) Urban development
Ans. (d)
• HUDCO stands for Housing and Urban
Development Corporation, established in
1970 as a public sector enterprise.
• It operates under the Ministry of Housing and
Urban Affairs and is fully government-owned.
• Its primary aim is to finance housing and
urban infrastructure projects, promote
planned urbanisation, and develop satellite
towns.
• Key functions include:
o Financing construction of affordable
housing for economically weaker
sections and low-income groups.
o Supporting urban infrastructure like
roads, sewerage, drainage, sanitation,
and water supply.

o

Subscribing to bonds and debentures
of state housing boards and
development authorities.
o Providing technical & consultancy
services for urban planning and
infrastructure.
• HUDCO plays a key role in urban
development schemes and low-cost housing
programmes initiated by both Central and
State governments.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q15.Who among the following is a famous
economist?
(a) Max Muller
(b) Macaulay
(c) Gunnar Myrdal
(d) Henry Francis Carry
Ans. (c)
• Gunnar Myrdal (1898–1987) was a Swedish
economist, sociologist, and politician known
for his pioneering work in economic
development and institutional analysis.
• He won the Nobel Prize in Economic Sciences
in 1974, jointly with Friedrich Hayek, for their
analysis of monetary theory and the
interdependence of economic and social
phenomena.
• His famous work “Asian Drama: An Inquiry
into the Poverty of Nations” (1968) examined
the developmental problems of South Asia,
especially India, in depth.
• He was a strong critic of Western
development models and emphasised the
role of institutions, social systems, and
politics in economic growth.
• His theory of “Cumulative Causation”
explained how economic inequalities tend to
perpetuate themselves.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

UPSC PYQs - 1990
Q1.“Super301” envisages retaliatory action by the
United States of America against the countries it
believes to be
(a) Violating human rights.
(b) Involved in the production and distribution of
narcotics.
(c) Against free enterprise.
(d) Blocking free international trade practices
Ans. (d)
• “Super 301” refers to a provision under
Section 301 of the U.S. Trade Act of 1974,
empowering the U.S. to take retaliatory trade
action against foreign countries.
• It targets nations that violate international
trade agreements, adopt unjustifiable or
discriminatory trade practices, or block fair
trade with the U.S.
• The U.S. President can take “all appropriate
action”, including imposing tariffs, quotas, or
withdrawing trade concessions.
• The measure was used aggressively in the late
1980s and early 1990s, especially against
Japan, India, and Brazil in disputes over
market access and trade barriers.
• Its primary objective was to protect U.S.
commercial interests by forcing trade
liberalization abroad.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q2.Stock-farming refers to
(a) Accent on agricultural production to build a buffer
stock
(b) Cooperative farming.
(c) Farming concentrating on the rearing of cattles and
sheep
(d) Large scale capital-intensive commercial farming
Ans. (c)
• Stock farming means livestock farming — the
practice of raising animals such as cattle,
sheep, goats, pigs, and poultry for food, wool,
dairy, leather, and other products.

•

It focuses more on animal husbandry than
crop cultivation, often involving pastoral
lands or mixed farms.
• In Europe and parts of India, stock farming is
done in areas unsuitable for intensive crop
farming, making animal rearing economically
viable.
• It provides farmers with continuous income
and food security (milk, meat, wool, etc.)
throughout the year.
• Recent years have seen scientific advances in
veterinary care, breeding, and feeding
practices to increase productivity.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q3.Which of the following is a cash crop?
(a) Groundnut
(b) Wheat
(c) Rice
(d) Gram
Ans. (a)
• Groundnut is classified as a cash crop, grown
primarily for commercial sale rather than
subsistence consumption.
• Cash crops include those cultivated for
industrial use or export, e.g., cotton, jute,
sugarcane, tobacco, coffee, tea, and oilseeds
like groundnut.
• Groundnut is mainly cultivated in Tamil Nadu,
Gujarat, Andhra Pradesh, and Maharashtra,
needing warm climate and well-drained soil.
• It serves as a major raw material for the
edible oil industry, contributing significantly
to India’s agricultural export earnings.
• Subsistence crops, like wheat or rice, are
grown for household consumption, while
groundnut is grown for market demand and
profit.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q4.At present, India’s share of the world exports of
tea is
(a) More than 40%
(b) Between 30% and 35%
(c) Between 10% and 20%

(d) About 10% or less
Ans. (c)
• India contributes around 12–13% of global
tea exports, placing it among the top five tea
exporters in the world.
• The three main tea-growing regions —
Assam, Darjeeling, and Nilgiris — produce
distinctive varieties with global demand.
• India accounts for ~31% of world tea
production, but a large part is consumed
domestically, leaving a moderate share for
export.
• Since 1947, production has grown 250%, but
land expansion has been only 40%, indicating
intensive cultivation and yield improvements.
• India’s labour-intensive tea industry employs
1.1 million workers, contributing significantly
to foreign exchange earnings.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q5. Consider the following commodities :
1. Tea
2. Cotton cloth
3. Cereals
4. Edible oils
5. Sugarcane
Among these, whose per capita availability has
remained more or less static between 1980-81 and
1987-88 in India?
(a) 1, 2 and 3
(b) 1, 2, 3 and 4
(c) 1, 4 and 5
(d) 2, 3, 4 and 5
Ans. (c)
• The per capita availability of Tea, Edible oils,
and Sugarcane showed no significant change
between 1980–81 and 1987–88, indicating
stagnant production relative to population
growth.
• For tea, domestic consumption matched
production, leaving little room for per capita
growth.
• Edible oils faced production bottlenecks, and
India relied heavily on imports during the
1980s to meet rising demand.

•

Sugarcane, while increasing in total output,
was mostly used for industrial sugar
production, leaving per capita availability
static.
• In contrast, cereals and cotton cloth saw
improvements in per capita availability due to
Green Revolution gains and textile expansion.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q6.The country which has the largest foreign debt at
present is
(a) Argentina
(b) Brazil
(c) Chile
(d) Mexico
Ans. (d)
• Mexico had the largest foreign debt among
developing countries during the 1980s,
leading to the Latin American Debt Crisis.
• Foreign debt refers to outstanding loans
owed by one country to foreign creditors,
including governments, international
institutions (e.g., IMF, World Bank), and
private lenders.
• Mexico borrowed heavily during the 1970s oil
boom, but falling oil prices and rising global
interest rates in the 1980s made debt
servicing unsustainable.
• In 1982, Mexico announced it could not meet
its repayment obligations, triggering a global
financial panic and IMF-led restructuring.
• Mexico’s debt crisis became a catalyst for
debt rescheduling programs and policy
reforms in other developing countries.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q7.India experienced a sharp decline in foodgrain
production when compared to the previous year,
during
(a) 1982-83, 1984-85 and 1986-87
(b) 1982-83, 1985-86 and 1987-88
(c) 1983-84, 1985-86 and 1986-87
(d) 1983-84, 1984-85 and 1987-88
Ans. (c)

•

The years 1983–84, 1985–86, and 1986–87
witnessed sharp dips in foodgrain production
due to monsoon failures, drought-like
conditions, and uneven rainfall.
• India’s agriculture is heavily monsoon
dependent, and fluctuations in rainfall
directly affect kharif crop yields, especially
rice and coarse cereals.
• These declines impacted food security, buffer
stocks, and agricultural growth rates, causing
rural distress in several states.
• Government measures during this period
included increased imports, public
distribution strengthening, and drought relief
programs.
• The period highlighted the vulnerability of
Indian agriculture despite Green Revolution
advances.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q8.The Indian caste system in its classical form is
characterised by
(a) Vertical hierarchy
(b) Horizontal hierarchy
(c) Intercaste occupational competition
(d) Equality
Ans. (c)
• The classical caste system in India is marked
by inter-caste occupational competition,
where different jatis (sub-castes) traditionally
competed for economic functions.
• Each caste historically had specific
occupations (e.g., Brahmins – priests,
Kshatriyas – warriors, Vaishyas – traders,
Shudras – laborers).
• Over time, competition for resources,
patronage, and power among castes
intensified, especially during colonial and
post-colonial periods.
• Urbanization, industrialization, and education
led to mobility and occupational overlap,
breaking rigid caste roles but increasing intercaste competition in modern sectors.
• This competition is also visible in political
mobilization and reservation policies, where

caste identities influence access to
opportunities.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q9.The Uruguay round of negotiations is primarily
concerned with reduction in
(a) The debt burden of developing countries
(b) Restrictions on the operation of a flexible
exchange rate system
(c) Restrictions on trade in agriculture and services
(d) Restrictions on fishing rights on the high seas
Ans. (c)
• The Uruguay Round (1986–1994) was the 8th
multilateral trade negotiation under GATT,
involving 123 countries.
• It expanded global trade rules to cover
agriculture, textiles, services, intellectual
property, and investment — areas earlier
outside GATT’s scope.
• A key focus was reducing trade barriers in
agriculture and services, which faced heavy
subsidies, tariffs, and non-tariff restrictions in
developed countries.
• The negotiations led to the establishment of
the WTO in 1995, transforming global trade
governance.
• The round emphasized tariff reduction,
market access, trade liberalization, and fair
competition, especially benefiting developing
countries seeking agricultural market entry.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q10.The term “Fiscal Crisis” in India currently refers
primarily to
(a) Increase in non-developmental government
expenditure
(b) Increase in public debt
(c) Recurring deficit on current account in the
government budget
(d) Phenomenal increase in external indebtedness
Ans. (c)
• Fiscal crisis in India refers to the situation of
recurring fiscal deficits — when government
expenditure exceeds revenue year after year.

•

In the late 1980s, India faced widening fiscal
deficits, leading to a balance of payments
crisis in 1991.
• Types of deficit include:
o Revenue deficit = Revenue
expenditure – Revenue receipts.
o Fiscal deficit = Total expenditure –
(Revenue receipts + Non-debt capital
receipts).
o Primary deficit = Fiscal deficit –
Interest payments.
• Persistent deficits reflect structural
weaknesses in revenue generation and high
subsidies, defense spending, and
administrative costs.
• Such a situation leads to higher borrowing,
crowding out private investment,
inflationary pressures, and external
vulnerability.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q11.Consider the following objectives of the Budget
proposals :
1. Encouraging the use of indigenous
intermediate products
2. Reducing the incidence of indirect taxes
3. Simplifying the procedure for collecting
indirect taxes
4. Integrating all indirect taxes in one
coordinated tax
MODVAT was introduced to achieve
(a) 2, 3 and 4
(b) 1, 2 and 3
(c) 2 and 3
(d) 1 and 2
Ans. (b)
• MODVAT (Modified Value Added Tax) was
introduced in 1986 to reduce cascading of
excise duties and promote efficient tax credit
mechanisms.
• It aimed to:
o Encourage indigenous intermediate
goods by allowing credit for duties
paid on inputs.

o

Reduce indirect tax burden on final
products by offsetting input tax.
o Simplify excise procedures, making
compliance easier for manufacturers.
• MODVAT applied to a selected list of items
and later evolved into CENVAT, and finally
into the GST regime.
• It represented a major reform in India’s
indirect taxation structure.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q12.Consider the following policy decisions of the
Government :
1. Giving autonomy to the electronic media
2. Appointing an industrialist as the Chairman of
the Indian Airlines
3. Hiring computer services on contract basis
from the market
4. Selling equity shares of the State Bank of India
in the market
Of the above, which would constitute privatisation?
(a) 1 and 2
(b) 2 and 3
(c) 3 and 4
(d) 2 and 4
Ans. (c)
• Privatisation involves transferring ownership,
control, or operations from public sector to
private entities.
• Statement 3 (hiring computer services)
reflects outsourcing of government functions
to the private sector.
• Statement 4 (selling SBI equity shares)
involves disinvestment, a core tool of
privatisation, reducing government stake.
• Giving autonomy (1) is not privatisation, as
ownership remains public.
• Appointing an industrialist as chairman (2) is a
management decision, not ownership
transfer.
• Privatisation gained momentum in post-1991
reforms, focusing on efficiency,
competitiveness, and fiscal consolidation.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q13.How many major ports are there at present in
India?
(a) 8
(b) 10
(c) 12
(d) 15
Ans. (c)
• India has a coastline of 7,516.6 km and
operates 12 major ports and over 200
minor/intermediate ports.
• Major ports are under Central Government
jurisdiction (Ministry of Ports, Shipping &
Waterways), while minor ports fall under
state governments.
• Major ports include Kandla, Mumbai,
Jawaharlal Nehru, Mormugao, New
Mangalore, Cochin, Chennai, Ennore,
Visakhapatnam, Paradip, Kolkata, and
Haldia.
• These ports handle around 55–60% of India’s
total maritime cargo, playing a key role in
international trade.
• Several ports have undergone modernization
and PPP projects to enhance capacity and
efficiency.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1991
Q1. A common currency for the members of the
Common Market in Europe is known as
(a) Dollar
(b) Euro
(c) Franc
(d) Pound
Ans. (b)
• The Euro (€) is the official currency of the
Eurozone, which consists of 19 member
states of the European Union.
• It was introduced in 1999 as an electronic
currency and in 2002 as physical notes and
coins.
• The Euro was created to facilitate trade,
economic integration, and monetary stability
among EU member nations.

•

Denmark and the UK opted out legally, while
other members are required to adopt the
Euro once they meet convergence criteria.
• The Euro is also used by some non-EU
countries and is the second most traded
currency globally after the US dollar.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q2. Which one of the following industries is not a
raw material-localised industry?
(a) Cotton Textile Industry
(b) Sugar Industry
(c) Cement Industry
(d) Glass Industry
Ans. (d)
• Raw material-localised industries are those
located near raw material sources due to
weight loss during production or transport
costs.
• Glass industry primarily requires sand, soda
ash, and limestone, which are easily available
in multiple locations.
• The final product is bulky and fragile, making
it more economical to locate near markets
than near raw materials.
• Industries like sugar, cement, cotton textiles
are typically raw-material oriented due to
perishability (sugarcane), weight (limestone),
or supply concentration (cotton).
• Glass industry location depends on market
proximity, transport convenience, and
energy availability.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q3. Which one of the following functions of the
Reserve Bank of India is not performed by the other
banks?
(a) Making advances to agriculturists
(b) Dealing in foreign exchange
(c) Industrial Refinancing
(d) Acting as banker to the government
Ans. (d)
• The Reserve Bank of India (RBI) is the central
bank and performs unique monetary and

fiscal functions not shared by commercial
banks.
• Acting as Banker to the Government involves
managing government accounts, public debt,
issuing new loans, and representing India at
IMF & World Bank.
• Other banks can give agricultural advances or
engage in foreign exchange and refinancing
activities, but they cannot perform sovereign
banking roles.
• RBI handles receipts and payments on behalf
of both Central and State governments.
• It also acts as debt manager, issuing treasury
bills and bonds, and facilitates ways and
means advances to governments.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q4. In which of the following products has
Bangladesh emerged as the main competitor to India
in the world market?
(a) Cotton garments
(b) Jute
(c) Cement
(d) Pharmaceuticals
Ans. (b)
• Bangladesh is the second largest producer of
jute after India, with high-quality fiber grown
in its deltaic plains.
• It has a 70% share in global jute exports,
making it a dominant player internationally.
• India is the largest consumer of jute
domestically due to mandatory packaging
laws, reducing its export capacity.
• Historically, India, China, and Bangladesh
have been top jute producers, but Bangladesh
leads in exports due to focused production
and processing.
• Its low production costs and specialization
have made it India’s main competitor in jute
products globally.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q5. The main watchdog of international trade is
(a) IMF
(b) World Bank

(c) GATT
(d) IFC
Ans. (c)
• GATT (General Agreement on Tariffs and
Trade) was established in 1947 to promote
free and fair trade by reducing tariffs and
other barriers.
• It laid down global trade rules for goods,
forming the legal basis for modern
international commerce.
• Through multiple GATT Rounds (especially
Uruguay Round 1986–94), global trade
regulations expanded to agriculture, services,
and intellectual property.
• In 1995, WTO (World Trade Organization)
replaced GATT as the global trade body but
continued GATT principles as its core
agreements.
• GATT’s role as a multilateral trade watchdog
was crucial for creating a predictable, rulebased system.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q6. The data collection for national income
estimation in India is done by
(a) National Sample Survey Organisation
(b) Finance Ministry of the Govt. of India
(c) Central Statistical Institute/Organisation
(d) Indian Statistical Institute
Ans. (a)
• National Sample Survey Organisation (NSSO)
was set up in 1950 under the Ministry of
Finance, later moved to MOSPI (Ministry of
Statistics and Programme Implementation).
• It conducts large-scale sample surveys on
subjects like household expenditure,
employment, industries, health, etc.
• Data collection for national income
estimation is its primary role, while
compilation is done by CSO.
• NSSO also conducts the Annual Survey of
Industries and develops techniques for crop
yield estimation.

•

It provides a nationwide statistical base for
planning, policy making, and GDP
computation.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q7. The largest contribution to the central exchequer
comes from
(a) Customs duties
(b) Income tax
(c) Central excise
(d) Estate duty
Ans. (b)
• Income tax, particularly corporate and
personal income tax, is the largest source of
central revenue.
• Contribution order: Corporate tax > Income
tax > Excise > Customs > Service tax.
• Income tax is a direct tax, providing a stable
and elastic source of revenue for government
expenditure.
• Over the decades, customs and excise have
reduced in importance due to liberalization
and GST introduction.
• Income tax base has expanded with
digitalization and compliance measures,
further increasing its contribution.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q8. If the capital stock was everlasting, then the rate
of depreciation would be
(a) High
(b) Low
(c) Infinite
(d) Zero
Ans. (d)
• Depreciation refers to the gradual reduction
in the value of capital assets due to wear and
tear, obsolescence, or technological change.
• If capital stock were everlasting, there would
be no physical deterioration and no loss of
productive capacity over time.
• Depreciation rate is calculated as the
proportion of capital value lost annually — if
there’s no loss, this rate becomes zero.

•

Net investment is calculated as Gross
Investment – Depreciation. With zero
depreciation, gross and net investment
become equal.
• Such a situation is theoretical, used in
economic models for simplifying growth
calculations.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q9. Inflation tends to reduce the share of labour in
the GNP (Gross National Product) because
(a) Wages lag behind prices
(b) Prices lag behind wages
(c) Profits lag behind prices
(d) Profits lag behind wages
Ans. (a)
• Inflation is a sustained rise in the general
price level of goods and services, reducing
purchasing power of money.
• Wages are often sticky in the short term; they
do not increase at the same speed as prices
of goods and services.
• When prices rise faster than wages, real
wages (purchasing power of wages) decline,
reducing labour’s share in GNP.
• Meanwhile, profits and returns to capital
often rise during inflation, since prices of
outputs increase faster than costs.
• This shifts income distribution in favour of
capital owners, reducing the labour share of
national income.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q10. The main source of financing the
implementation of development programmes is
(a) Internal resources from taxes and other measures
(b) External finance as borrowings/loans from foreign
governments
(c) External finance as loans from international
finance agencies
(d) External finance as loans from foreign
governments and international finance agencies
Ans. (a)

•

In India, internal resources, mainly tax
revenues and non-tax receipts, form the core
source of development financing.
• These include income tax, corporate tax,
indirect taxes, dividends from PSUs, and
fees/royalties.
• External borrowings are supplementary and
used when fiscal gaps arise — the primary
reliance is on domestic mobilization.
• This approach ensures self-reliance, reduces
vulnerability to foreign conditions, and aligns
with India’s planned economy strategy.
• Deficit financing (borrowing from the RBI) is
also used, but it has inflationary impacts and
is controlled carefully.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q11. Betterment levy is imposed by the government
because
(a) Of the improvement in soil/land quality as a result
of irrigational facilities provided by the government.
(b) Of improved industrial production due to various
infrastructural facilities provided by the government.
(c) Of increased capacity utilisation due to electricity
supplied by State Electricity Boards.
(d) Of increased raw material imports possible due to
availability of foreign currency provided by the
government.
Ans. (a)
• Betterment levy is a tax on landowners for
increased land value resulting from
government investment in infrastructure.
• When irrigation projects, roads, metros, or
airports are built, land prices in nearby areas
appreciate sharply.
• Landowners benefit from unearned gains
(windfall profits) due to public expenditure,
not private investment.
• To recover part of these publicly created
gains, governments levy a betterment tax or
development charge.
• This mechanism helps fund future
infrastructure and ensures equitable sharing
of benefits.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q12. The main objective of the Eighth Finance
Commission was
(a) To suggest new pay scales for Central and State
government employees.
(b) To suggest measures to increase tax income to the
government.
(c) To suggest the basis of distribution of Income tax,
Excise duties and Levy duties proceeds between
various States.
(d) To suggest the basis of providing financial
help/assistance to small scale industries.
Ans. (c)
• The Eighth Finance Commission (constituted
in 1984) was tasked with recommending
financial relations between the Union and
States.
• Its primary focus was to distribute proceeds
of income tax, excise duties, and grants-inaid between the Centre and the States.
• It operated under Article 280 of the
Constitution, which mandates Finance
Commissions to recommend revenue sharing.
• It also examined population statistics, states’
financial needs, and proposed changes to
estate duty and excise distribution.
• Its recommendations were crucial for fiscal
federalism, ensuring equity and efficiency in
Centre–State financial transfers.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q13. As the GNP (Gross National Product) of a nation
increases, the share of the tertiary sector in the total
GNP
(a) Decreases
(b) Increases
(c) Remains same
(d) None of the above
Ans. (d)
• There is no fixed linear relationship between
rising GNP and the tertiary sector’s share — it
depends on the structure of growth.
• In developed economies, tertiary sector often
grows with GNP (e.g., services dominate GDP).

•

But in resource-rich economies, growth may
occur in primary or secondary sectors,
keeping tertiary share stable or lower.
• In some cases, tertiary sector may lag if
infrastructure or education is weak, despite
GNP growth.
• Thus, no single pattern fits all — the answer
cannot be definitively increase, decrease, or
remain same.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q14. Based on the ideals of Supportive Economic
Increment, complete nationalisation based planning
was first adopted by
(a) China
(b) USSR
(c) Poland
(d) Cuba
Ans. (b)
• USSR (Soviet Union) was the first country to
adopt complete nationalisation-based
planning following the Bolshevik Revolution
(1917).
• Under Lenin and Stalin, private property and
industries were nationalised, and centralized
five-year plans were launched.
• The USSR followed Soviet-type economic
planning, focusing on state ownership,
central allocation, and production targets.
• This model influenced many countries
including India, which adopted Five-Year
Plans with Soviet inspiration.
• The Soviet model emphasized supportive
economic increment — state-led heavy
industry, collectivised agriculture, and
planned distribution.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q15. Who is the pioneer of ‘White revolution’ in
India?
(a) Dhiru Bhai Ambani
(b) Verghese Kurien
(c) Swaminathan
(d) S.K. Birla
Ans. (b)

•

Dr. Verghese Kurien is known as the Father of
the White Revolution in India.
• He spearheaded Operation Flood (1970) —
the world’s largest dairy development
programme, making India the largest milk
producer globally by the 1990s.
• His vision transformed India from a milkdeficient nation to a self-sufficient, milksurplus country.
• Operation Flood empowered rural farmers,
created millions of jobs, and promoted
gender equality, nutrition, and rural
infrastructure.
• He led the Amul cooperative movement,
establishing a replicable model of farmerowned institutions.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

UPSC PYQs - 1992
Q1. The average growth achieved during the Seventh
Five-Year Plan in India (in terms of GDP at factor
cost) was
(a) 3.5
(b) 4.2
(c) 5.1
(d) 5.6
Ans. (d)
• The Seventh Five-Year Plan period was 1985–
1990, and it closed on 31st March 1990.

•

The targeted growth rate was 5%, but the
achieved growth rate was around 6% per
annum, higher than expected.
• In terms of GDP at factor cost, the average
growth was 5.6% per annum.
• The plan focused on food self-sufficiency,
social justice, and modernization of
industries.
• Plan expenditure exceeded the target,
indicating higher investment in development
projects.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q2. India has the maximum amount of foreign trade
with
(a) USA
(b) Great Britain
(c) Japan
(d) Germany
Ans. (a)
• In 1990–91, USA was India’s largest trading
partner, accounting for a significant portion of
both exports and imports.
• Major Indian exports to the USA included
textiles, gems and jewellery, leather, and
engineering goods.
• Imports from the USA included machinery,
fertilizers, aircraft parts, and high-tech
equipment.
• In present data, China has overtaken the USA
in overall trade volume, but USA remains a
key export destination.
• Trade with USA has historically shaped India’s
external sector and foreign exchange
earnings.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q3. The definition of a small-scale industry is based
on
(a) The market outreach of a unit
(b) The size of the sales of a unit
(c) The fact whether the products manufactured fall in
a list drawn up by the Ministry of Industry
(d) The value of the plant and machinery investment
in a unit.

Ans. (d)
•

The classification of Micro, Small and
Medium Enterprises (MSMEs) in India is
based on investment in plant and machinery
for manufacturing.
• As per MSMED Act, 2006, limits are:
o Micro: ≤ ₹25 lakh in plant &
machinery.
o Small: ₹25 lakh – ₹5 crore.
o Medium: ₹5 crore – ₹10 crore.
• For service enterprises, the limit is based on
investment in equipment.
• Earlier, the small-scale definition was also
used for priority sector lending, reservation
of products, and concessions.
• This investment-based classification ensures
objective measurement and avoids marketbased fluctuations.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q4. Unemployment which occurs when workers
move from one job to another job is known as
(a) Seasonal unemployment
(b) Frictional unemployment
(c) Technological unemployment
(d) Cyclical unemployment
Ans. (b)
• Frictional unemployment arises when
workers are temporarily unemployed while
changing jobs or entering the labour force.
• It happens due to time lags, lack of
information, or mobility issues.
• It is generally short-term and voluntary in
nature.
• Examples include a person leaving one job
and taking a few weeks to find another, or
students searching for their first job.
• It differs from seasonal, technological, and
cyclical unemployment, which are structural
or periodic.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q5. The single largest component in the non-plan
expenditure during 1991-92 was

(a) Interest payment
(b) Compensation to banks in respect of amount spent
as loan waiving
(c) Subsidies on fertiliser
(d) Compensation on the loss incurred by PSUs
Ans. (a)
• Non-plan expenditure refers to regular,
recurring expenditure, not linked to
development schemes.
• In 1991–92, interest payments on past
borrowings formed the largest share of nonplan expenditure.
• This was due to rising internal & external
debt, accumulated over previous plans.
• Interest payments also reflect fiscal stress —
high debt servicing limits funds for
development.
• Other non-plan items include defence,
pensions, subsidies, and administration, but
interest payment dominates.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q6. Deficit financing means
(a) An excess of governments’ current expenditure
over its current revenue.
(b) An excess of government expenditures minus
borrowings other than those from the RBI.
(c) An excess of government’s total expenditure over
its total revenue.
(d) An excess of government revenue over
expenditure.
Ans. (c)
• Deficit financing is when government
expenditure exceeds total revenue, leading
to a budgetary gap.
• This gap is met by borrowing from the
Reserve Bank of India, i.e., printing new
money.
• It is a tool to mobilize resources for
development when tax revenue is insufficient.
• However, excessive deficit financing can be
inflationary, hence it must be controlled.
• It played a major role in India’s early plans to
fund large public investments.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q7. Among the reasons for disguised unemployment
in rural areas is
(a) Choice of a heavy industry model for economic
development
(b) Low levels of technological development in the
country
(c) Heavy pressure of population along with half
hearted implementation of agrarian reforms
(d) High illiteracy rates
Ans. (c)
• Disguised unemployment occurs when more
workers are employed than actually needed,
leading to zero marginal productivity.
• It is common in agriculture, where extra
labour does not increase output.
• Population pressure in rural areas leads to
overcrowding in farming due to lack of
alternative employment.
• Agrarian reforms were inadequately
implemented, failing to redistribute land or
improve productivity effectively.
• This combination creates surplus labour
remaining on farms without contributing to
production.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q8. Which one of the following has the highest
number of female workers as per the 1990 Economic
Census?
(a) Delhi
(b) Kerala
(c) Maharashtra
(d) West Bengal
Ans. (c)
• As per the 1990 Economic Census,
Maharashtra had the highest number of
female workers.
• Maharashtra’s economy included industrial,
service, and agriculture-linked sectors,
offering diverse employment for women.
• Urban centres like Mumbai and Pune
provided opportunities in textiles,
manufacturing, services, and informal
sectors.

•

In later data, Uttar Pradesh emerged with the
largest number of female workers due to
sheer population size.
• The census helps track gender participation in
the labour force for policy planning.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q9. Which one of the following schemes was
intended to tap the black money?
(a) UTI Bonds
(b) India Development Bonds
(c) Long term Operations Scheme
(d) SBI Deposit Scheme
Ans. (b)
• India Development Bonds (IDBs) were
launched during 1991 Balance of Payments
crisis to mobilize NRI funds and tap
unaccounted money.
• They offered attractive interest rates and tax
incentives, encouraging Indians abroad to
invest legally.
• This helped boost foreign exchange reserves,
which were critically low.
• It was part of a larger economic stabilization
package post-1991 reforms.
• IDBs were seen as an instrument to channel
black money into productive financial assets.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q10. The share of village and small-scale industries in
the total output of the manufacturing sector is nearly
(a) 49%
(b) 35%
(c) 25%
(d) 15%
Ans. (b)
• In the early 1990s, village and small-scale
industries contributed about 35% of total
manufacturing output.
• These industries play a crucial role in
employment generation, export earnings,
and rural industrialization.
• They accounted for over 40% of
manufacturing turnover, 45% of

manufactured exports, and 25% of total
exports.
• Sectors include handlooms, handicrafts, agroprocessing, and engineering goods.
• Promotion of SSIs was integral to balanced
regional development and inclusive growth.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1993
Q1. Which one of the following is the best strategy
for environment- friendly sustainable development
in Indian agriculture?
(a) Expansion of cultivable land, increased use of
superphosphate, urea and effective biocides.
(b) Wider popularization of high-yielding crop
varieties, better and more frequent irrigation and
increased frequency of aerial sprays of inorganic
fertilizers and pesticides.
(c) Mixed cropping, organic manures, nitrogen fixing
plants and pest resistant crop varieties
(d) Improved farm implements and machinery, use of
potent insecticides to minimise post-harvest grain
losses and mono culture cropping practices.
Ans. (c)
• Mixed cropping/inter-cropping improves soil
cover, reduces risk, and spreads nutrient
demand.
• Organic manures enhance soil organic
carbon, microbial activity, and water-holding
capacity.
• Nitrogen-fixing legumes (rhizobia symbiosis)
cut synthetic N use and input costs.
• Pest-resistant varieties reduce pesticide load,
preserving beneficial insects and ecosystem
health.
• Strategy aligns with low external input,
resource recycling, and resilience to climate
variability.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q2. The production of cultured pearls is an important
cottage industry of
(a) Belgium
(b) West Indies

(c) New Zealand
(d) Japan
Ans. (d)
• Cultured pearls were pioneered in Japan
(Mikimoto method) using Akoya oysters.
• Industry began as small-scale/cottage
clusters along Japanese coasts before scaling.
• Today China leads volumes, but Japan
remains synonymous with high-quality Akoya
pearls.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q3. As on today, which one of the following
countries has the largest external debt?
(a) India
(b) Brazil
(c) USA
(d) Mexico
Ans. (c)
• In absolute dollar terms, the USA has the
world’s largest external debt.
• External debt reflects sum owed to nonresidents (public + private), not per-capita
burden.
• Advanced economies sustain high debt via
deep capital markets and reserve-currency
status.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q4. When partial convertibility obtains the exchange
rate is
(a) a weighted average
(b) a simple average
(c) fully floating
(d) fully administered
Ans. (a)
• India’s 1992 Dual Exchange Rate (partial
convertibility) split forex: a part at official
rate, rest at market rate.
• Effective receipts to exporters/importers
became a weighted average of official and
market rates.
• Aimed to cheapen essentials while
liberalizing other transactions.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q5. The New Population Bomb refers to
(a) an increase in the population of the aged in the
Third World
(b) rapidly growing urban population in the Third
World
(c) large scale distress migration in the Third World
(d) deluge of Soviet emigrants
Ans. (b)
• Highlights explosive urbanization in
developing countries: megacities, slums,
infrastructure stress.
• Driven by youth bulge, rural–urban
migration, and service-sector pull.
• Implications for jobs, housing, sanitation, and
urban governance.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q6. During 1992-93 there was a record bumper in
(a) rice
(b) sugarcane
(c) pulses
(d) oil seeds
Ans. (d)
• Oilseeds output surged with Technology
Mission on Oilseeds, price support, and area
shift.
• Adoption of improved varieties, better
agronomy, and market incentives boosted
yields.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q7. The growth rate of which one of the following
sectors has very low employment elasticity?
(a) Manufacturing
(b) Construction
(c) Financial services
(d) Mixed farming
Ans. (d)
• Employment elasticity: % change in
employment per 1% output growth.
• Mixed farming (crop + livestock) is laboursaturated; output gains often come from
productivity, not new jobs.

•

Empirically, construction is high-elasticity;
agriculture/mixed typically low.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q8. Which one of the following types of borrowings
from the IMF has the softest servicing conditions?
(a) Second tranche loan
(b) SAF
(c) ESAF
(d) Oil Facility
Ans. (c)
• ESAF (Enhanced Structural Adjustment
Facility) provided concessional, longmaturity, low-interest support to low-income
countries.
• Softer than SAF; later replaced by PRGF/ECF
with similar concessionality.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q9. In India the Public Sector is most dominant in
(a) steel production
(b) organised term-lending financial institutions
(c) transport
(d) commercial banking
Ans. (a)
• Historically, core steel dominated by SAIL,
RINL; private presence (e.g., TISCO) smaller
relative to public capacity.
• Steel classified as basic/core industry with
heavy public investment post-Independence.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q10. The earnings of India from diamond export is
quite high. Which one of the following factors has
contributed to it?
(a) Pre-independence stock-piling of diamonds in the
country which are now exported
(b) Large production of industrial diamonds in the
country
(c) Expertise available for cutting and polishing of
imported diamonds which are then exported.
(d) As in the past, India produces huge quantity of
gem diamonds which are exported
Ans. (c)

•

•

India imports rough diamonds, adds value via
cutting & polishing, and re-exports as
polished stones.
Strong skill base (Surat, Mumbai), cluster
efficiencies, and global value-chain
integration.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q11. When the Couple Protection Rate is going up
(a) birth rate must necessarily fall
(b) birth rate will fall only if couples are of younger
age
(c) death rate must necessarily fall
(d) infant mortality rate must be falling
Ans. (a)
• CPR measures share protected against
pregnancy (contraceptive prevalence) among
women 15–49.
• Rising CPR correlates with declining TFR and
lower crude birth rate through fertility
control.
• Other options mis-specify mortality or age
conditions.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q12. The Employment Guarantee Scheme, a rural
work programme, was first started in
(a) West Bengal
(b) Punjab
(c) Kerala
(d) Maharashtra
Ans. (d)
• Maharashtra EGS pioneered right-to-work
style rural employment in the 1970s.
• Became template for later national
programmes, culminating in MGNREGA.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q13. A zero rate of Inflation obtains necessarily in a
year where the annual rate of inflation
(a) in every week of the year is zero
(b) is falling in every week of the year
(c) is both falling and rising in a year
(d) is constant in every week of the year
Ans. (c)

•
•

•

Zero annual inflation means average price
level unchanged over the year.
Weekly rates can be positive some weeks and
negative others so that the net over 52
weeks ≈ 0%.
Constant zero every week is not necessary.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q14. Which one of the following sets of economists
strongly favoured a market economy?
(a) Adam Smith, Keynes, Hicks
(b) Adam Smith, Marx, Strumlin
(c) Adam Smith, Hayek, Friedman
(d) Adam Smith, Ricardo, J.K. Galbraith
Ans. (c)
• Market economy is classically championed by
Adam Smith (invisible hand), Hayek (prices &
knowledge), Friedman (monetarism &
choice).
• Keynes/Galbraith/Marx emphasized state
role/critique of markets, not pure market
primacy.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q15. Which one of the following pairs is correctly
matched?
(a) Rationing : Fiscal control
(b) Cash Reserve Ratio : Selective Credit Control
(c) Licensing : Comprehensive Control
(d) Import Quota : Physical control
Ans. (d)
• Import quota is a physical (quantitative)
restriction on import volumes.
• CRR is a general monetary tool, not selective
credit control.
• Rationing is an administrative allocation, not
fiscal measure.
• Industrial licensing is selective, not an
economy-wide “comprehensive” control in
this pairing sense.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q16. Most-Favoured-Nations (MFN) clause under
GATT implies

(a) most favour to some countries
(b) most favour to all countries
(c) no favour to any country
(d) no favour to some countries
Ans. (b)
• MFN (non-discrimination): any
tariff/concession granted to one member
must be extended to all members.
• Core principle of GATT Article I, also
embedded in GATS and TRIPS with limited
exceptions.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q17. Which one of the following modes of
privatisation is the most comprehensive and
complete?
(a) Introduction of private capital in public sector
(b) Contracting out management of public enterprises
to the private sector
(c) Transferring ownership and management to the
workers
(d) Transferring ownership and management to the
private sector
Ans. (d)
• Complete transfer of both ownership and
management to private sector is the full
privatisation model.
• Other modes (partial equity, management
contracts, worker co-ops) are partial/limited.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q18. Variable reserve rates and Open Market
Operations are instruments of
(a) Fiscal Policy
(b) Monetary Policy
(c) Budgetary Policy
(d) Trade Policy
Ans. (b)
• Variable reserve ratios (CRR/SLR) and Open
Market Operations (OMO) are central bank
tools to manage liquidity and credit.
• Used to achieve price stability, influence
interest rates, and smooth money supply.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q19. Which of the following are the main causes of
slow rate of growth of per capita income In India?
1. High capital-output ratio
2. High rate of growth of population
3. High rate of capital formation
4. High level of fiscal deficits
