Select the correct answer from the codes
given below:
(a) 1, 2, 3 and 4
(b) 2, 3 and 4
(c) 1 and 4
(d) 1 and 2
Ans. (d)
• High ICOR signals inefficient investment →
more capital needed per unit of output.
• High population growth dilutes per-capita
gains even when GDP rises.
• High capital formation would raise growth;
not a cause of slow per-capita growth.
• Fiscal deficits impact macro-stability but are
indirect here; core drivers are (1) and (2).
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q20. Eurodollars are
(a) a currency issued by European Monetary Union
(b) special currency issued by federal government of
U.S.A. to be used only in Europe
(c) U.S. dollars circulating In Europe
(d) European currencies exchanged for the U.S. dollar
in U.S.
Ans. (c)
• Eurodollars are USD-denominated deposits
held outside the U.S. (originally in Europe).
• Escape Federal Reserve reserve
requirements; part of the eurocurrency
market.
• Not a special currency; simply U.S. dollars
offshore.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q21. Which arrangement of the following show the
correct sequence of demographic transition as
typically associated with economic development?
1. High birth rate with high death
2. Low birth rate with low death

3. High birth rate with low death
Choose the answer from the codes given
below
Codes:
(a) 1, 2, 3
(b) 1, 3, 2
(c) 3, 1, 2
(d) 2, 1, 3
Ans. (b)
• Stage 1: High birth, high death → low
growth.
• Stage 2: High birth, falling/low death →
population surge.
• Stage 3: Low birth, low death →
stabilization/aging.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q22. At the present state of our knowledge and
resources position, India will remain self-sufficient
for the next three decades in
(a) Tin
(b) Coking Coal
(c) Copper
(d) Petroleum
Ans. (c)
• Among the options, tin, coking coal, and
petroleum are clearly deficient domestically.
• Copper resources/reserves and refining
capacity provide comparatively better selfreliance prospects versus the other three,
historically making it the best fit here.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q23. The basic difference between imperative and
indicative planning is that
(a) In the case of the imperative planning the market
mechanism is entirely replaced by a command
hierarchy, while in the case of indicative planning, it is
looked upon as a way to improve the functioning of
the market system
(b) In the case of indicative planning there is no need
to nationalise any industry
(c) In the case of imperative planning all economic
activities belong to public sector, while in the other
type they belong to the private sector

(d) It is easier to achieve targets in imperative type of
planning
Ans. (a)
• Imperative (command) planning: state
directives replace market signals;
allocations/targets are binding.
• Indicative planning: guidance/signals to
influence private decisions; markets remain
primary.
• Mixed economies (like India) historically used
indicative elements to steer sectoral
priorities.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1994
Q1. The main function of the I.M.F is to
(a) arrange international deposits from banks
(b) help to solve balance of payments problems of
member countries
(c) act as a private sector lending arm of the World
Bank
(d) finance investment to developing countries
Ans. (b)
• IMF’s main function is to assist countries
facing temporary balance of payments
difficulties by providing short-to-medium
term financial support.
• It works to maintain global monetary
stability, facilitates international trade, and
promotes exchange rate stability.
• It provides financial assistance through
various lending facilities and closely monitors
economic policies of member countries.
• It is not a private lending agency like the
World Bank, nor does it finance development
projects directly.
• It conducts surveillance of monetary and
exchange rate policies and issues policy
recommendations to ensure global financial
stability.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q2. To know whether the rich are getting richer and
the poor getting poorer, it is necessary to compare

(a) the wholesale price index over different periods of
time for different regions
(b) the distribution of income of an identical set of
income recipients in different periods of time
(c) the distribution of income of different sets of
income recipients at a point of time
(d) the availability of foodgrains among two sets of
people, one rich and the other poor, over different
periods of time
Ans. (b)
• To measure changes in inequality,
economists compare income distribution of
the same group over time.
• This helps identify whether income
concentration has shifted towards the rich or
the poor.
• The Gini coefficient is a common measure
used to assess the degree of inequality in
income or wealth distribution.
• Comparing different groups at a single time
(option c) only gives a snapshot, not a trend.
• Wholesale price index or food availability do
not directly reflect income distribution
patterns.
• Only tracking the same set of income
recipients over time can reveal whether the
rich are becoming richer and the poor
poorer.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q3. Which of the following statements about the
Dunkel draft is/are correct?
1. It is mandatory for the Government of India to
accept all its proposals in all the sectors.
2. In the field of agriculture, the main proposal is
to cut agricultural subsidies.
3. In the field of textiles, it reiterates the
operation of the multifibre agreement.
4. The proposals have already been accepted by
the Parliament.
Select the correct answer from the codes given below:
(a) 1 only
(b) 1 and 2 only
(c) 1, 2 and 4 only

(d) 3 and 4 only
Ans. (c)
• The Dunkel Draft emerged from the Uruguay
Round of GATT negotiations and contained
comprehensive proposals for trade
liberalisation.
• It aimed to open up world trade through
binding commitments on subsidies, tariffs
and intellectual property.
• In agriculture, the draft proposed reduction
of subsidies and increased market access.
• It was mandatory for member countries to
accept the proposals to join the new trade
regime.
• The Indian Parliament accepted the
proposals, which paved the way for India’s
commitments under the WTO later.
• The Multifibre Agreement (textiles) was
actually proposed to be phased out, not
reiterated.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q4. Fiscal deficit in the Union Budget means
(a) The sum of budgetary deficit and net increase in
internal and external borrowings
(b) The difference between current expenditure and
current revenue
(c) The sum of monetized deficit and budgetary deficit
(d) Net increase in Union Government’s borrowing
from the Reserve Bank of India
Ans. (a)
• Fiscal deficit represents the total borrowing
requirement of the government.
• It is calculated as:
Fiscal Deficit = Total Expenditure – (Revenue
Receipts + Recoveries of Loans + Other
Receipts)
• It includes both internal and external
borrowings, making it the most
comprehensive measure of budget deficit.
• It indicates the gap between government’s
income and expenditure, excluding
borrowings.
• It differs from budget deficit and revenue
deficit, which are narrower concepts.

•

Controlling fiscal deficit is crucial to maintain
macroeconomic stability, manage inflation
and avoid excessive debt.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q5. Convertibility of the rupee implies
(a) being able to convert rupee notes into gold
(b) allowing the value of the rupee to be fixed by
market forces
(c) freely permitting the conversion of rupee to other
major currencies and vice versa
(d) developing an international market for currencies
in India
Ans. (c)
• Convertibility refers to the freedom to
exchange domestic currency for foreign
currency and vice versa without restrictions.
• It indicates how freely the rupee can be
traded in the foreign exchange market.
• India introduced current account
convertibility in 1993, allowing rupee to be
converted freely for trade and current
transactions.
• Capital account convertibility (for
investments and borrowing) remains partial
in India.
• It enhances international trade, improves
investor confidence, and aligns the currency
with market exchange rates.
• It is not related to gold backing or developing
a new currency market.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q6. Devaluation of a currency means
(a) reduction in the value of a currency vis-a-vis major
internationally traded currencies
(b) permitting the currency to seek its worth in the
international market
(c) fixing the value of the currency in conjunction with
the movement in the value of a basket of
predetermined currencies
(d) fixing the value of a currency in multilateral
consultation with the IMF, the World Bank and major
trading partner
Ans. (a)

•

•

•

•
•

Devaluation is a deliberate policy decision to
lower the value of a country’s currency under
a fixed exchange rate system.
It is different from depreciation, which
happens due to market forces under a
floating exchange rate.
Devaluation aims to make exports cheaper
and imports costlier, improving trade
balance.
It is often used to combat balance of
payments crises.
However, it can lead to imported inflation,
reduce purchasing power, and may cause
economic instability if not managed well.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q7. Which one of the following governmental steps
has proved relatively effective in controlling the
double digit rate of inflation in the Indian economy
during recent years?
(a) Enhanced rate of production of all consumer goods
(b) Streamlined public distribution system
(c) Pursuing an export-oriented strategy
(d) Containing budgetary deficits and unproductive
expenditure
Ans. (d)
• Containing fiscal deficit is a key measure to
control inflation, as high deficits fuel excess
liquidity in the economy.
• By reducing unproductive expenditure, the
government can limit aggregate demand,
helping curb inflationary pressures.
• While public distribution and production
increases help in supply management, they

•

•

are not as immediate or comprehensive in
controlling demand-pull inflation.
Export-oriented strategies primarily affect
foreign trade balances, not domestic price
levels directly.
Fiscal consolidation measures in India have
historically helped moderate double-digit
inflation rates.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q8. The difference between a bank and a NonBanking Financial Institution (NBFI) is that
(a) a bank interacts directly with customers while an
NBFI interacts with banks and governments
(b) a bank indulges in a number of activities relating to
finance with a range of customers, while an NBFI is
mainly concerned with the term loan needs of large
enterprises.
(c) a bank deals with both internal and international
customers while an NBFI is mainly concerned with the
finances of foreign companies
(d) a bank’s main interest is to help in business
transactions and savings/investment activities while
an NBFI’s main interest is in the stabilisation of the
currency.
Ans. (b)
• Banks are financial institutions authorised to
accept deposits, grant loans, and provide a
range of financial services to individuals and
businesses.
• NBFIs (NBFCs), though engaged in financial
services, do not hold a banking licence and
mainly focus on term lending, leasing,
infrastructure finance, and other specific
financial functions.
• NBFCs cannot accept demand deposits, and
their regulation is less stringent compared to
banks.
• Banks play a larger role in monetary policy,
whereas NBFCs cater to niche credit needs,
especially of large enterprises and long-term
finance.
• NBFCs like ICICI Ventures, Kotak Mahindra
Finance, Sundaram Finance emerged in India

in the mid-1980s.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q9. Assertion (A): The USA re-emerged as India’s
single largest import source in the early nineties.
Reason (R): With swift political developments in the
erstwhile Soviet Union, India gradually began to rely
on the USA for its defence requirements.
In the context of the above two statements, which
one of the following is correct?
(a) Both A and R are true, and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) Both A and R are true, but R is not a correct
explanation of A
(c) A is true, but R is false
(d) A is false, but R is true
Ans. (c)
• In the early 1990s, after the collapse of the
USSR, India’s trade pattern shifted
significantly, and the USA became India’s
largest source of imports.
• The Soviet Union’s breakup disrupted
traditional trade ties, especially under the
rupee-rouble agreement, leading India to
diversify trade.
• However, India continued to rely on Russia
(successor of USSR) for defence imports,
especially major equipment.
• Therefore, the assertion is correct, as USA did
re-emerge as the main trade partner.
• The reason is incorrect, since the shift was
due to trade and geopolitical realignment,
not defence dependency.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q10. ‘Self-sufficiency’ in food in the true sense of
freedom from hunger, has not been achieved in India
in spite of a more than threefold rise in food grains
production over 1950–1990. Which of the following
are the reasons for it?
1. The green revolution has been restricted to
small pockets of the country.
2. The cost of food is too high compared to the
earnings of the poor.

3. Too much emphasis is laid on wheat and
paddy compared to the coarse grains.
4. The gains of the green revolution have largely
accrued to the cash crop rather than food
crops.
Select the correct answer from the codes given below:
(a) 1, 2 and 3
(b) 1, 2 and 4
(c) 1, 3 and 4
(d) 2, 3 and 4
Ans. (a)
• Green Revolution benefits were regionally
concentrated mainly in Punjab, Haryana, and
Western Uttar Pradesh, leaving vast regions
with low productivity.
• Food prices remained high relative to the
poor’s income, leading to inadequate access
despite overall availability.
• Policy emphasis on wheat and paddy led to
neglect of coarse cereals and pulses, which
are cheaper and nutritionally rich, limiting
access for poorer sections.
• Although cash crops expanded later, the initial
Green Revolution focused mainly on
foodgrains, making statement 4 less relevant
here.
• Self-sufficiency in aggregate production ≠
nutritional security, especially when
distribution and affordability are unequal.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q11. The latest regional economic block to be
formed is
(a) ASEAN
(b) COMECON
(c) APEC
(d) NAFTA
Ans. (d)
• NAFTA (North American Free Trade
Agreement) came into force in January 1994,
making it the most recent major economic
bloc of that period.
• It involved USA, Canada, and Mexico, aiming
to eliminate most tariffs and liberalize trade
in agriculture, textiles, and automobiles.

•

•

•

It established mechanisms for dispute
resolution, protection of intellectual
property, and environmental safeguards.
ASEAN and COMECON were established
earlier (1967 and 1949 respectively), and
APEC (1989) predated NAFTA.
NAFTA significantly reshaped North American
trade relations, increasing economic
integration between the three nations.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q12. A major shift in the 8th Five-Year Plan from its
preceding ones is
(a) the significant reduction in public sector outlays
(b) the concentration of public investment in
infrastructural sectors
(c) major investment in agriculture with a view to
promote exports
(d) major investment in sectors in which industrial
sickness has been a chronic problem
Ans. (a)
• The Eighth Five-Year Plan (1992–1997) was
formulated after the 1991 economic reforms
and aimed to move from a centrally planned
to a market-led economy.
• A major shift was the reduction in public
sector outlays as the private sector was
expected to play a greater role.
• It emphasized liberalisation, privatisation and
global integration, marking a departure from
the earlier state-dominated investment
model.
• The focus shifted from direct state control to
indicative planning and facilitation.

•

Public investment was more selective,
especially in infrastructure, while private
investment was encouraged in other sectors.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q13. The principal reason why national economic
planning is still being pursued in spite of embracing a
market economy since 1991 is that
(a) it is a Constitutional requirement
(b) the vast quantity of capital already deployed in the
public sector needs to be nurtured
(c) Five-Year Plans can continue to provide a longterm perspective to the economy in market-friendly
fashions.
(d) the market economy is mainly confined to industry
and commerce and central planning in agriculture is
necessary.
Ans. (c)
• Even after 1991 economic liberalisation,
planning continued because it provided
strategic direction and long-term vision.
• The Planning Commission (now NITI Aayog) is
not a constitutional body, hence (a) is
incorrect.
• While public sector capital is important, it is
not the principal reason.
• Planning helps coordinate national priorities,
reduce regional disparities, and guide private
investment through policy signals.
• Indicative planning in a mixed economy
framework allows the government to steer
development without direct control.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q14. The first to start a joint stock company to trade
with India were the
(a) Portuguese
(b) Dutch
(c) French
(d) Danish
Ans. (b)
• The Dutch East India Company was
established in 1602, becoming the first joint
stock company to trade with India.

•

•

•

•

It introduced share trading in Amsterdam,
laying the foundation of the world’s first
stock exchange.
The Dutch were early competitors to the
Portuguese and established trading posts on
India’s west coast, notably at Surat and
Pulicat.
The Portuguese arrived earlier but did not
form joint stock companies. The French
(1664) and Danish (1616) came later.
Dutch companies pioneered futures trading,
profit-sharing, and joint capital pooling for
overseas trade.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q15. Which of the following taxes is/are levied by
the Union and collected and appropriated by the
States?
(a) Stamp Duties
(b) Passenger and Goods Tax
(c) Estate Duty
(d) Taxes on Newspapers
Ans. (a)
• Stamp duties on bills of exchange, cheques,
promissory notes and other instruments are
levied by the Union but collected and
appropriated by the States.
• This division reflects India’s quasi-federal
fiscal structure, ensuring both Centre and
States have revenue powers.
• Passenger and goods tax is a State subject,
while Estate Duty was abolished in 1985.
• Taxes on newspapers are prohibited under
Article 19(1)(a) guaranteeing freedom of
speech.
• The arrangement for stamp duties allows
uniformity in rates nationally while ensuring
state-level revenue.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q16. Which of the following are correctly matched?
Establishment

Place

1. Setting up of the first cotton mill 1854 Bombay
2. Manufacture of the first machine- 1870 Near
made paper
Calcutta
3. Setting up of the first cement
factory

1904 Madras

Choose the correct answer from the codes given
below:
(a) 1 and 2 only
(b) 1 and 3 only
(c) 2 and 3 only
(d) 1, 2 and 3
Ans. (
• The first cotton mill was established at Fort
Gloster near Kolkata in 1818, but it failed.
The true foundation of the modern cotton
industry was the Bombay Spinning and
Weaving Company (1854).
• The first successful paper mill was set up in
1870 near Calcutta (Ballyganj) as the Royal
Bengal Paper Mills.
• The first cement factory was established in
Madras in 1904 by South India Industrial Ltd.,
even before Porbandar’s plant of 1914.
• Therefore, statements 2 and 3 are correctly
matched.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q17. Even though the Railways are the most
pervasive mode of transport in India, it is the road
transport which received the maximum impetus in
most of the post-independence period. Which of the
following are the reasons for this?
1. Railways are cheap to operate but the
associated capital costs are very high.
2. Given the geographic spread of the human
settlements, it is unrealistic that the railways
alone can serve the needs of transport.
3. The per unit (tonne/km) cost of transport is
cheaper in road compared to rail transport.
4. Given the indivisible nature of the Railways, it
is not always convenient for the population to

avail of it with the same ease as private cars,
buses or two-wheelers.
Select the correct answer from the codes given below:
(a) 1, 2 and 3
(b) 1, 2 and 4
(c) 1, 3 and 4
(d) 2, 3 and 4
Ans. (b)
• Railways have low operating costs but high
capital investment requirements, making
expansion slower.
• India’s settlement pattern is widely
dispersed, making it impractical for railways
alone to meet transport needs.
• Road transport has low initial costs, is
flexible, connects remote areas, and has a
shorter gestation period.
• Per unit cost of rail transport is usually lower
than road for bulk, long-distance goods —
hence statement 3 is incorrect.
• Roads allow easy access for personal and
small-scale transport, unlike the fixed and
indivisible railway network.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q18. Which of the following are the purposes to
which the National Renewal Fund has been set up?
1. To restructure sick small scale industries.
2. To help workers likely to be displaced due to
retrenchment in the process of industrial
restructuring.
3. To modernise existing industrial units.
4. To help refugees from Bangladesh, Sri Lanka
etc.
Choose the correct answer from the code given
below:
(a) 1 and 2
(b) 1 and 3
(c) 1 and 4
(d) 2 and 4
Ans. (a)
• National Renewal Fund (NRF) was established
in 1992 under the New Industrial Policy of
1991.

•

•

•

•

It aimed to provide a social safety net to
workers affected by industrial restructuring
and modernisation.
It supported retraining and redeployment of
displaced workers, and compensation for
retrenchment.
It also provided assistance to firms for costs
of restructuring, particularly in the smallscale sector.
It did not deal with refugee assistance, nor
was it meant for direct modernisation
funding.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1995

Q1.To prevent recurrence of scams in Indian Capital
Market, the Government of India has assigned
regulatory powers to
(a) SEBI
(b) RBI
(c) SBI
(d) ICICI
Ans. (a)
Explanation:
• The Securities and Exchange Board of India
(SEBI) was established in 1992 to regulate the
securities market and protect investors.
• It was given statutory powers through the
SEBI Act, 1992 after major scams like the
Harshad Mehta scam exposed regulatory
gaps.
• SEBI performs quasi-legislative, quasi-judicial,
and quasi-executive functions — framing
regulations, conducting investigations, and
passing orders.
• It ensures transparency, checks market
manipulation, and regulates stock exchanges,
brokers, merchant bankers, and other
intermediaries.
• SEBI also compels companies to list, monitors
disclosures, and can inspect accounts and
impose penalties.

•

The strengthening of SEBI’s powers was a key
reform to restore investor confidence and
prevent recurrence of market scams.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q2.Which one of the following Five-Year Plans
recognised human development as the core of all
developmental efforts?
(a) The Third Five-Year Plan
(b) The Fifth Five-Year Plan
(c) The Sixth Five-Year Plan
(d) The Eighth Five-Year Plan
Ans. (d)
Explanation:
• The Eighth Five-Year Plan (1992–1997)
shifted focus from centralized control to
indicative planning in line with the 1991
economic reforms.
• It emphasized human development —
education, health, drinking water, primary
healthcare, employment generation.
• Recognized that economic growth must be
accompanied by improvements in human
welfare, not just production targets.
• Stressed poverty alleviation, population
control, and employment creation through
participatory planning.
• Integrated social sector development into
overall economic strategy for inclusive
growth.
• This plan targeted 5.6% annual growth but
achieved around 6.7%, marking a successful
reform phase.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q3.Which of the following are the objectives of the
Commission for Agricultural Costs and Prices (CACP)?
1. To stabilise agricultural prices
2. To ensure meaningful real income levels to
the farmers.
3. To protect the interest of the consumers by
providing essential agricultural commodities
at reasonable rates through public
distribution system.
4. To ensure maximum price for the farmer

Select the correct answer from the codes given below:
(a) 1, 2 and 3
(b) 1, 2 and 4
(c) 1, 3 and 4
(d) 2, 3 and 4
Ans. (a)
Explanation:
• The CACP was set up in 1965 as Agricultural
Prices Commission, renamed in 1985, to
recommend Minimum Support Prices (MSP).
• It aims to stabilize agricultural prices to
protect both farmers and consumers from
sharp fluctuations.
• Ensures remunerative real income levels for
farmers by recommending fair MSPs based on
cost of cultivation and productivity trends.
• Protects consumer interests by ensuring
essential commodities are available at
reasonable prices through mechanisms like
PDS.
• CACP uses cost-plus approach (C2 cost +
margin) to fix MSPs, influencing production
patterns and food security.
• It plays a critical role in agricultural price
policy to balance farm incentives and
consumer welfare.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q4.As part of the liberalisation programme and with
a view to attracting foreign exchange, the
Government and the RBI have devised two schemes
known as FCNR-’A’ and FCNR-’B’. Which of the
following is/are true regarding these two schemes?
1. Under scheme ‘A’ RBI bears exchange rate
fluctuations.
2. Under scheme ‘B’, other banks are to meet
out the difference in exchange rate
fluctuations.
3. Both the schemes stand withdrawn now.
4. Only scheme ‘A’ has been withdrawn.
Select the correct answer from the codes given below:
(a) 3 only
(b) 1 and 2
(c) 1, 2 and 3
(d) 1, 2 and 4

Ans. (d)
Explanation:
• FCNR(A) (Foreign Currency Non-Resident
Accounts) was launched in 1975 to mobilize
foreign exchange; in this, RBI bore the
exchange risk.
• Later replaced by FCNR(B) in 1993, under
which commercial banks bore the currency
risk, reducing RBI’s liability.
• These are term deposits maintained in
permitted foreign currencies by NRIs with
Indian banks.
• FCNR(A) was withdrawn due to rising
liabilities (over $10 billion) and replaced by
FCNR(B) in the post-liberalization period.
• These schemes helped augment foreign
exchange reserves during the 1991 BOP crisis
and encouraged NRI deposits.
• Currently, only FCNR(B) continues, FCNR(A)
was withdrawn.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q5.Which of the following are among the non-plan
expenditures of the Government of India?
1. Defence expenditure
2. Subsidies
3. All expenditures linked with the previous plan
periods
4. Interest payment
Choose the correct answer from the codes given
below:
(a) 1 and 2
(b) 1 and 3
(c) 2 and 4
(d) 1, 2, 3 and 4
Ans. (d)
Explanation:
• Non-plan expenditure includes all
government spending not related to current
Five-Year Plan schemes.
• Defence expenditure, excluding capital outlay
on equipment, is a major component.
• Subsidies on food, fertilizers, petroleum, etc.,
are regular budgetary commitments outside
plan allocations.

•

Interest payments on internal and external
debt form the largest single item, reflecting
fiscal deficits.
• Committed liabilities from previous plans
(e.g., continuing schemes) are also classified
as non-plan.
• Other components include pensions,
administrative expenses, grants to states,
and economic services.
• This classification was discontinued after
2017-18, but in the 1990s, it was crucial to
analyze fiscal health.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q6.Which of the following pairs are correctly
matched?
1. Increase in foreign : Monetary expansion
exchange reserves
2. Low import growth : Recession in Indian
Industry
3. Euro-issues : Shares held by Indian
companies in European
countries
4. Portfolio investment : Foreign institutional
investors
Select the correct answer by using the following
codes:
(a) 1, 2 and 4
(b) 3 and 4
(c) 1, 2 and 3
(d) 1, 2, 3 and 4
Ans. (a)
Explanation:
• An increase in foreign exchange reserves
allows the RBI to undertake monetary
expansion by releasing more liquidity into the
economy, supporting credit growth and
investment.
• Low import growth is often a symptom of
recession — during a slowdown, industrial
demand for inputs falls, investment slows,
and imports of capital goods and raw
materials decline.

•

Euro-issues refer to financial instruments
issued by Indian companies in international
markets, especially European stock
exchanges, to raise capital — not shares held
by Indian companies abroad.
• Portfolio investment is the purchase of
financial assets (like stocks, bonds) by foreign
institutional investors (FIIs) without taking
direct control or management of companies.
It is short-term and highly liquid compared to
FDI.
• Euro-issues gained popularity in India during
the 1990s liberalisation, enabling Indian firms
to access global capital.
• Portfolio flows influence foreign exchange
reserves, stock markets, and capital account
balances, playing a key role in emerging
market volatility.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q7.What is the annual growth rate aimed at in the
Eighth Five-Year Plan?
(a) 5.6%
(b) 6%
(c) 6.5%
(d) 7%
Ans. (a)
Explanation:
• The Eighth Five-Year Plan (1992–1997)
marked the beginning of India’s postliberalisation planning era, focusing on
structural reforms and human development.
• It targeted an annual GDP growth rate of
5.6%, but actually achieved around 6.7%,
reflecting early liberalisation success.
• It emphasized employment creation,
population control, and social infrastructure
like health, education, drinking water, and
human resource development.
• Fiscal deficit, which was around 8.3% of GDP
in 1990–91, was brought down to around
5.2% by 1996–97.
• Inflation fell sharply from 16.3% (1991) to
below 4% by 1997, showing macroeconomic
stabilization.

•

The plan focused on agriculture
diversification, infrastructure development,
and market-led growth with reduced state
control.
• It was a transitional plan from centralized
planning to indicative planning aligned with
global economic integration.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q8.Which one of the following is the largest mutual
fund organisation in India?
(a) SBI Mutual Fund
(b) GIC Mutual Fund
(c) Industrial Bank Mutual Fund
(d) Unit Trust of India
Ans. (d)
Explanation:
• Unit Trust of India (UTI) was established in
1964 under the UTI Act, 1963 as the first and
largest mutual fund in India.
• UTI’s objective was to mobilize household
savings, particularly from middle and lower
income groups, and channel them into
industrial development through investment
in capital markets.
• It offered unit schemes that provided
investors with tax rebates and income tax
exemptions on returns up to certain limits,
making it highly attractive.
• UTI had a monopoly in mutual funds until the
entry of public sector banks and insurance
companies into the sector in the late 1980s.
• Its investment activities included discounting
bills, merchant banking, foreign exchange
dealings, and leasing services.
• UTI played a critical role in deepening India’s
capital markets and spreading investment
culture among retail investors.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q9.Corporation tax
(a) is levied and appropriated by the States
(b) is levied by the Union and collected and
appropriated by the States
(c) is levied by the Union and shared by the Union and

the States
(d) is levied by the Union and belongs to it exclusively
Ans. (d)
Explanation:
• Corporation tax (or corporate tax) is levied on
profits of companies under the Income Tax
Act, 1961.
• It is levied, collected, and retained
exclusively by the Union Government; states
have no share in its proceeds.
• Other taxes that belong exclusively to the
Centre include customs duties, surcharges on
income tax, and taxes on capital value of
assets.
• Corporate tax applies to both domestic and
foreign companies, with different rates and
surcharge structures.
• States receive no statutory share of corporate
tax unlike personal income tax, which is
shared between the Union and states under
Article 270.
• Corporate tax is a major revenue source for
the Union budget, financing public
expenditure and fiscal programmes.
• It differs from indirect taxes like GST, which
are shared between Centre and States.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q10.Which of the following constitute the World
Bank?
1. International Bank for Reconstruction and
Development
2. International Finance Corporation
3. International Development Association
4. International Monetary Fund
Choose the correct answer from the codes given
below:
(a) 1, 2 and 3
(b) 1 and 2
(c) 3 and 4
(d) 1, 2, 3 and 4
Ans. (a)
Explanation:
• The World Bank is part of the World Bank
Group, which provides loans and grants to

developing countries for capital and
development programmes.
• It consists of five institutions, but the World
Bank proper includes only the first two:
o International Bank for
Reconstruction and Development
(IBRD) – provides loans with
sovereign guarantees.
o International Development
Association (IDA) – provides
concessional loans and grants to the
poorest countries.
• International Finance Corporation (IFC), while
part of the World Bank Group, focuses on
private sector investments.
• IMF is not part of the World Bank, though
both coordinate through the Bretton Woods
system.
• The World Bank supports projects in
infrastructure, education, agriculture, health,
environment, and governance to promote
development.
• It plays a key role in poverty reduction,
capacity building, and technical assistance in
low and middle-income countries.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q11.Which one of the following is not an instrument
of selective credit control in India?
(a) Regulation of consumer credit
(b) Rationing of credit
(c) Margin requirements
(d) Variable cost reserve ratios
Ans. (d)
Explanation:
• Variable cost reserve ratios refer to Cash
Reserve Ratio (CRR) and Statutory Liquidity
Ratio (SLR), which are quantitative tools of
credit control used by the RBI to regulate
overall liquidity in the economy.
• Selective credit controls, also known as
qualitative controls, are used to channel
credit into specific sectors and discourage
lending to speculative or non-priority
activities.

•

Instruments of selective credit control
include:
o Margin requirements – difference
between value of security and loan
amount.
o Regulation of consumer credit –
controlling instalment terms, down
payments etc.
o Rationing of credit – limiting credit to
certain sectors or institutions.
o Moral suasion and directives to banks
on sectoral lending.
• CRR and SLR are used to influence aggregate
credit, not to direct it selectively, hence they
are quantitative, not qualitative measures.
• Selective credit control became particularly
significant during periods of speculative
activity in commodities and shares, enabling
RBI to curb credit misuse without disturbing
overall liquidity.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q12.The Dinesh Goswami Committee was concerned
with
(a) de-nationalisation of banks
(b) electoral reforms
(c) steps to put down insurgency in the North-East
(d) the problem of the Chakmas
Ans. (b)
Explanation:
• The Dinesh Goswami Committee on Electoral
Reforms was constituted in May 1990 to
recommend measures to strengthen India’s
electoral system.
• Its major recommendations included:
o Fresh delimitation of constituencies
on the basis of the 1981 Census.
o Reduction in number of
constituencies from which a
candidate can contest to a maximum
of two.
o Measures to curb criminalization of
politics and misuse of money and
muscle power.

o

State funding of elections in kind, to
reduce dependence on private
money.
o Speedy disposal of election petitions
and independent machinery for
preparation of electoral rolls.
• It also suggested strengthening the Election
Commission and ensuring its independence
through proper appointment and security of
tenure for Election Commissioners.
• Many of its suggestions influenced later
reforms, including amendments to the
Representation of the People Act and
electoral procedures.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q13.Which one of the following is true regarding the
Jawahar Rozgar Yojana (JRY)?
(a) It was launched during the Prime Ministership of
Indira Gandhi
(b) It aims at creating one million jobs annually
(c) The target group of JRY are the urban poor living
below the poverty line
(d) Under the scheme 30% of the employment
generated is reserved for women
Ans. (d)
Explanation:
• Jawahar Rozgar Yojana (JRY) was launched on
April 1, 1989, during the Rajiv Gandhi
government by merging National Rural
Employment Programme (NREP) and Rural
Landless Employment Guarantee Programme
(RLEGP).
• It aimed to provide supplementary
employment to the rural unemployed and
underemployed, focusing on poverty
alleviation and rural infrastructure.
• 30% of the employment generated was
reserved for women, to promote gender
equity in rural development.
• The scheme was implemented through
Panchayati Raj Institutions, ensuring
decentralized planning and eliminating
intermediaries.

•

Funding pattern was 80:20 between Centre
and States, with a major role assigned to
Gram Panchayats in project identification and
execution.
• The scheme emphasized labour-intensive
works like roads, irrigation canals, soil
conservation, to generate employment while
creating durable community assets.
• JRY later evolved into Employment Assurance
Scheme (EAS) and Jawahar Gram Samridhi
Yojana (JGSY).
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q14.Bank Rate implies the rate of interest
(a) paid by the Reserve Bank of India on the Deposits
of Commercial Banks
(b) charged by Banks on loans and advances
(c) payable on Bonds
(d) at which the Reserve Bank of India discounts the
Bills of Exchange
Ans. (d)
Explanation:
• Bank Rate is the official interest rate at which
RBI provides loans to commercial banks and
financial institutions either by lending directly
or rediscounting bills of exchange and
treasury bills.
• It acts as a signal of monetary policy stance:
o An increase in bank rate makes
borrowing costlier for banks,
discouraging credit expansion.
o A decrease in bank rate makes credit
cheaper, encouraging lending and
economic activity.
• It differs from Repo Rate, which is for shortterm lending against government securities,
while Bank Rate involves no collateral
repurchase agreement.
• Changes in bank rate influence the structure
of interest rates in the economy, affecting
investment, consumption, and liquidity.
• Historically, the Bank Rate was a key tool
before repo operations became more
dominant post-1990s reforms.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q15.In which one of the following crops
international trade is low in the context of total
produce?
(a) Rice
(b) Coffee
(c) Rubber
(d) Wheat
Ans. (a)
Explanation:
• Rice has low international trade as a
proportion of global production, unlike
commodities like wheat or coffee.
• This is because major producing countries are
also major consumers — e.g., India, China,
Indonesia, Bangladesh, where rice is the
staple food.
• The global rice trade constitutes only around
7–8% of total production, much lower than
wheat (~20%) or maize.
• Countries prioritize domestic food security,
imposing export restrictions during shortages,
which limits free trade in rice.
• Rice trade is highly policy-sensitive,
influenced by public distribution systems,
procurement policies, and subsidies.
• Unlike coffee or rubber, which are primarily
export-oriented cash crops, rice is primarily
produced for domestic consumption.
• India, despite being a leading rice exporter,
exports only a fraction of its total production.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q16.The Narasimham Committee for Financial Sector
Reforms has suggested reduction in
(a) SLR and CRR
(b) SLR, CRR and Priority Sector Financing
(c) SLR and Financing to capital goods sector
(d) CRR, Priority Sector Financing and Financing to
capital goods sector
Ans. (b)
Explanation:
• The Narasimham Committee (1991), chaired
by M. Narasimham, was set up to reform the
financial sector in the context of economic
liberalisation.

•

It recommended reduction in Statutory
Liquidity Ratio (SLR) and Cash Reserve Ratio
(CRR) to free more funds for banks to lend to
productive sectors.
• It proposed bringing SLR down to 25% and
CRR to 10%, making more credit available to
the private sector and improving banking
efficiency.
• The committee also recommended redefining
the priority sector, narrowing its scope to
focus on genuinely weaker sections, like
marginal farmers, small businesses, transport
operators, tiny industries, etc.
• It emphasized deregulation of interest rates,
allowing banks to determine rates
independently, leading to more marketoriented banking.
• It suggested setting up asset reconstruction
mechanisms, tribunals for bad debt recovery,
and allowing new private banks to increase
competition.
• These recommendations formed the basis of
major banking reforms in the 1990s, leading
to financial liberalization.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q17.One of the reasons for India’s occupational
structure remaining more or less the same over the
years has been that
(a) productivity in agriculture has been high enough to
induce people to stay with agriculture.
(b) ceilings on land holdings have enabled more
people to own land and hence their preference to stay
with agriculture.
(c) people are largely unaware of the significance of
transition from agriculture to industry.
(d) for economic development, investment pattern
has been directed towards capital-intensive
industries.
Ans. (d)
Explanation:
• India's occupational structure has shown
slow transformation from agriculture to
industry and services, unlike many rapidly
industrializing countries.

•

A major reason is the investment pattern
post-Independence, which was directed
mainly towards capital-intensive industries
under the Nehru–Mahalanobis strategy,
focusing on heavy industries and the public
sector.
• This strategy did not generate sufficient
employment in industry to absorb the surplus
agricultural labour, leading to continued
dependence on agriculture.
• Agricultural productivity remained low, so
while many stayed in agriculture,
underemployment and disguised
unemployment persisted.
• The manufacturing sector's employment
elasticity was relatively low, as growth was
concentrated in organized, capital-heavy
segments rather than labour-intensive ones.
• Urban industrial jobs were limited, and
migration barriers (informal sector, housing,
lack of skills) also slowed occupational shift.
• As a result, despite structural changes in GDP,
the workforce remained predominantly in
agriculture through the 20th century.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q18.Which of the following benefits are likely to
accrue to India from the World Trade Organisation?
1. India’s share in the world trade is to go up
from the present 600 million US dollars to 5
billion US dollars by 2000 A.D.
2. It will help boost exports of agricultural
commodities from India.
3. India’s share in the world trade is likely to
triple by the year 2000 A.D.
Select the correct answer using the codes given
below:
(a) 1, 2 and 3
(b) 1 and 2
(c) 1 and 3
(d) 2 and 3
Ans. (d)
Explanation:
• The World Trade Organization (WTO) was
established in 1995, succeeding the GATT,

with the objective to promote free and fair
global trade.
• One of the major gains for India was the
opening up of agricultural markets —
reduction of agricultural subsidies and export
barriers by developed countries was expected
to boost Indian agricultural exports.
• WTO's multilateral rules on anti-dumping,
subsidies, dispute settlement and safeguards
were designed to provide a predictable and
secure trading environment, favourable for
India’s exporters.
• India expected to increase its share in world
trade, especially through diversification of
exports, participation in global value chains,
and leveraging its comparative advantages in
labour-intensive sectors.
• WTO membership also offered India mostfavoured-nation (MFN) treatment, giving
better access to markets in developed
countries.
• The claim of increase to 5 billion USD was
exaggerated; realistic projections were
tripling of India's share, driven mainly by
agriculture and manufacturing exports.
• WTO also pushed India to modernize
domestic trade policy, reduce tariffs, and
improve competitiveness.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q19.The main reason for low growth rate in India, in
spite of high rate of savings and capital formation is
(a) high birth rate
(b) low level of foreign aid
(c) low capital / output ratio
(d) high capital / output ratio
Ans. (d)
Explanation:
• The Incremental Capital-Output Ratio (ICOR)
measures investment efficiency — how much
capital is required to produce an additional
unit of output.
• A high ICOR indicates low efficiency of
capital, meaning more investment is needed
to achieve a unit of growth.

•

In India, despite high savings and investment
rates, capital productivity remained low due
to inefficiencies in the public sector, delays in
project implementation, technological
backwardness, and bureaucratic hurdles.
• Much of the capital went into capitalintensive heavy industries with long
gestation periods, contributing to slow
output growth in the short to medium term.
• Infrastructure bottlenecks, inadequate
planning, and poor utilization of resources
further increased ICOR.
• As a result, economic growth lagged behind
investment levels, unlike in East Asian
economies where ICOR was low and
investment translated efficiently into output.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q20.The largest source of financing the public sector
outlay of the Eighth Five-Year Plan comes from
(a) balance from current revenue
(b) contribution of public enterprises
(c) government borrowings
(d) deficit financing
Ans. (d)
Explanation:
• The Eighth Five-Year Plan (1992–1997)
marked a shift toward a market-oriented
economy, but public sector outlays still
formed a major part of planned expenditure.
• The largest source of financing was domestic
borrowings and deficit financing, including
market borrowings, small savings, provident
funds, and net capital receipts.
• Actual deficit financing during the plan period
was ₹35,626 crore at current prices, more
than double the projected level of ₹14,000
crore.
• Deficit financing was used to bridge the gap
between plan expenditure and available
resources, as tax and non-tax revenues were
insufficient.
• Contribution from public enterprises and
balance from current revenue were lower

than expected due to slow disinvestment and
low profitability of PSUs.
• Heavy reliance on borrowings increased
public debt and interest payment liabilities,
but it was seen as necessary to support
planned investments in infrastructure and
social sectors.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q21.The new Exim Policy announced in 1992, is for a
period of
(a) 3 years
(b) 4 years
(c) 7 years
(d) 5 years
Ans. (d)
Explanation:
• The Export-Import (EXIM) Policy of 1992 was
a landmark in India’s trade liberalisation,
marking a shift from import substitution to
export promotion.
• It was announced under the Foreign Trade
(Development and Regulation) Act, 1992 and
was designed to reflect the extent of trade
liberalisation and measures for export
promotion.
• The policy was framed for a five-year period,
providing long-term stability and direction for
exporters and importers.
• Within this 5-year framework, annual
supplements were issued on March 31 each
year to fine-tune trade incentives and
schemes.
• Key objectives included:
o Boosting exports to achieve at least
1% of global merchandise trade.
o Facilitating access to raw materials,
capital goods, and intermediates for
production.
o Enhancing technological
competitiveness of Indian industry
and agriculture.
o Liberalising imports by removing
quantitative restrictions and reducing
tariffs.

•

The EXIM Policy 1992–97 played a key role in
integrating India with the global economy
post-1991 reforms.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q22.Consider the following:
1. Industrial Finance Corporation of India
2. Industrial Credit and Investment Corporation
of India
3. Industrial Development Bank of India
4. Unit Trust of India
The correct sequence in which the above were
established is
(a) 1, 2, 4, 3
(b) 1, 3, 2, 4
(c) 4, 3, 2, 1
(d) 1, 4, 3, 2
Ans. (a)
Explanation:
• The Industrial Finance Corporation of India
(IFCI) was the first development financial
institution, established in 1948, to provide
medium and long-term credit to industries.
• The Industrial Credit and Investment
Corporation of India (ICICI) was set up in 1955
as a private sector development bank to
support industrial expansion.
• The Unit Trust of India (UTI) was created in
1963 under the UTI Act, 1963, and started
operations in 1964, to mobilise household
savings and invest in capital markets.
• The Industrial Development Bank of India
(IDBI) was established in July 1964 as an apex
financial institution to coordinate and
supplement the role of other financial
institutions.
• Thus, the chronological order is:
1. IFCI (1948)
2. ICICI (1955)
3. UTI (1964)
4. IDBI (1964)
• These institutions played a crucial role in
financing industrial growth during India’s
planned development era.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

UPSC PYQs - 1996
Q1.A redistribution of income in a country can be
best brought about through
(a) progressive taxation combined with progressive
expenditure
(b) progressive taxation combined with regressive
expenditure
(c) regressive taxation combined with regressive
expenditure
(d) regressive taxation combined with progressive
expenditure
Ans. (b)
Explanation:
• Progressive taxation ensures that higherincome groups pay a larger proportion of
their income as tax compared to lowerincome groups. This helps reduce income
inequality at the collection stage itself.
• Regressive expenditure refers to government
spending targeted toward lower-income or
disadvantaged groups, such as subsidies, food
vouchers, or welfare schemes, thereby
redistributing resources.
• Through transfer payments like subsidies,
vouchers (e.g., food stamps), cash transfers,
or welfare schemes, income is redistributed
from high-income taxpayers to low-income
beneficiaries.
• Progressive tax + targeted welfare spending
effectively narrows the income gap,
promoting social equity and reducing poverty
levels.
• This combination is commonly used in
modern democratic economies to ensure
inclusive development.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q2.Which one of the following is correct regarding
stabilization and structural adjustment as two
components of the new economic policy adopted in
India?
(a) Stabilization is a gradual, multi-step process while
structural adjustment is a quick adaptation process
(b) Structural adjustment is a gradual, multi-step

process, while stabilization is a quick adaptation
process
(c) Stabilization and structural adjustment are very
similar and complementary policies. It is difficult to
separate one from the other
(d) Stabilization mainly deals with a set of policies
which are to be implemented by the Central
Government while structural adjustment is to be set
in motion by the State Governments
Ans. (b)
Explanation:
• The 1991 Economic Reforms in India had two
components: macroeconomic stabilization
(short-term) and structural adjustment (longterm).
• Stabilization involved quick, short-term
corrective measures to restore
macroeconomic balance, mainly focusing on
controlling inflation, reducing fiscal deficit,
and improving the balance of payments.
• Structural adjustment aimed at gradual,
multi-step reforms to change the
institutional and policy framework, covering
areas like industrial licensing, trade
liberalization, public sector reforms, and
financial sector reforms.
• Stabilization created a stable economic
environment, while structural reforms aimed
to make the economy efficient, competitive,
and globally integrated in the long run.
• Both components were complementary, but
their timeframes and objectives differed.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q3.Assertion (A): An important policy instrument of
economic liberalization is reduction in import duties
on capital goods.
Reason (R): Reduction in import duties would help
the local entrepreneurs to improve technology to
face the global markets.
In the context of the above two statements, which
one of the following is correct ?
(a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) Both A and R are true and R is not a correct

explanation of A
(c) A is true but R is false
(d) A is false but R is true
Ans. (a)
Explanation:
• Capital goods are goods used to produce
other goods (e.g., machinery, equipment), not
consumed directly by households.
• Under the economic liberalization policy of
1991, import duties on capital goods were
reduced significantly to make modern
technology and equipment cheaper.
• Lower import duties allowed domestic
industries to access advanced technology,
improve productivity, and become globally
competitive.
• This also helped industries modernize their
production processes and reduce costs,
enabling them to compete in international
markets.
• Both the assertion and reason are correct,
and the reason explains the assertion
accurately.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q4.One of the important goals of the economic
liberalisation policy is to achieve full convertibility of
the Indian rupee. This is being advocated because
(a) convertibility of the rupee will stabilize its
exchange value against major currencies of the world
(b) it will attract more foreign capital inflow in India
(c) it will help promote exports
(d) it will help India secure loans from the world
financial markets at attractive terms
Ans. (b)
Explanation:
• Full convertibility means the rupee’s
exchange rate is determined by market
forces with no restrictions on inflow or
outflow of foreign currency.
• This allows foreign investors greater
confidence in moving capital in and out of
India without bureaucratic hurdles, thus
attracting more foreign capital inflows.

•

It promotes foreign direct investment (FDI)
and portfolio investment, as investors seek
economies with freely tradable currencies.
• While full convertibility may also indirectly
boost exports and trade competitiveness, the
main advocacy is to encourage capital inflow
and investment inflows into India.
• India’s 1991 reforms introduced current
account convertibility, and discussions
continue on gradual movement towards
capital account convertibility.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q5.The emerging trading blocs in the world, such as
NAFTA, ASEAN and the like, are expected to
(a) act as constrictions in free trade across the world
(b) promote free trade on the lines laid down by the
WTO
(c) permit transfer of technology between member
countries
(d) promote trade in agricultural commodities
between countries of the North and South
Ans. (b)
Explanation:
• Trading blocs such as NAFTA (North American
Free Trade Agreement) and ASEAN
(Association of Southeast Asian Nations) are
created to reduce trade barriers and promote
free trade among member countries.
• They function on the principles similar to
those of the World Trade Organization (WTO)
— encouraging lower tariffs, freer movement
of goods and services, and greater market
integration.
• NAFTA sought to eliminate tariffs gradually,
especially in agriculture, textiles, and
automobiles, fostering regional integration.
• ASEAN’s goal is to accelerate economic
growth, social progress, and trade
integration in the Southeast Asian region,
aligned with global free trade principles.
• Though regional blocs sometimes lead to
trade diversion, their overall effect is usually
to promote trade liberalization, consistent
with WTO objectives.

Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q6.Which of the following were the aims behind the
setting up of the World Trade Organization (WTO)?
1. Promotion of free trade and resource flows
across countries.
2. Protection of intellectual property rights.
3. Managing balanced trade between different
countries.
4. Promotion of trade between the former East
Block countries and the western world.
Select the correct answer by using the codes given
below:
(a) 1, 2, 3 and 4
(b) 1 and 2
(c) 2 and 3
(d) 1 and 4
Ans. (b)
Explanation:
• The WTO, established in 1995 as the
successor to GATT (1947), was primarily
aimed at promoting free and fair
international trade.
• One major goal was the promotion of free
trade and unhindered flow of resources
across borders through reduction of tariffs
and trade barriers.
• Another central aim was the protection of
intellectual property rights (IPR) through the
TRIPS agreement (Trade-Related Aspects of
Intellectual Property Rights).
• WTO does not manage "balanced trade" —
trade imbalances are handled by
macroeconomic policies, not by WTO directly.
• Similarly, its mandate was not specifically to
promote trade between East and West blocs;
its focus was global multilateral trade.
• It also works in dispute settlement, trade
negotiations, and building trade capacity in
developing countries.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q7.Hawala transactions relate to payments
(a) received in rupees against overseas currencies and
vice versa without going through the official channels

(b) received for sale/transfer of shares without going
through the established stock exchanges
(c) received as commission for services rendered to
overseas investors/buyers/sellers in assisting them to
get over the red tape and/or in getting preferential
treatment
(d) made to political parties or to individuals for
meeting election expenses
Ans. (a)
Explanation:
• Hawala transactions refer to an informal and
illegal system of transferring money, which
operates outside official banking and foreign
exchange channels.
• It involves receiving and paying money in
local currency against foreign currency
without actually moving funds through banks.
• The system relies heavily on trust and a
network of brokers (Hawaladars) who settle
accounts informally, often across
international borders.
• Hawala is often used for money laundering,
tax evasion, financing illegal activities, and
bypassing currency regulations.
• It bypasses RBI’s regulatory framework,
making it untraceable and risky, which is why
it is prohibited under Indian foreign exchange
and anti-money laundering laws.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q8.The Eighth Five-Year Plan is different from the
earlier ones. The critical difference lies in the fact
that:
(a) it has a considerably larger outlay compared to the
earlier plans
(b) it has a major thrust on agricultural and rural
development
(c) considerable emphasis is placed on infrastructure
growth
(d) industrial licensing has been abolished.
Ans. (a)
Explanation:
• The Eighth Five-Year Plan (1992–1997)
marked a strategic shift in India’s planning

approach, coinciding with economic
liberalisation reforms of 1991.
• It had a significantly larger financial outlay
than previous plans, reflecting India’s attempt
to accelerate growth after the balance of
payments crisis.
• The plan aimed to combine indicative
planning with market-led development,
focusing on employment generation, human
development, and infrastructural
improvements.
• It was designed to manage the transition
from a centrally planned to a liberalized
economy, making its scale and scope larger
than earlier plans.
• While agricultural and infrastructure goals
were part of it, the main distinguishing factor
was its massive outlay and reform-oriented
strategy.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q9.In India, rural incomes are generally lower than
the urban incomes. Which of the following reasons
account for this?
1. A large number of farmers are illiterate and
know little about scientific agriculture.
2. Prices of primary products are lower than
those of manufactured products.
3. Investment in agriculture has been low when
compared to investment in industry.
Select the correct answer by using the codes given
below:
(a) 1, 2 and 3
(b) 1 and 2
(c) 1 and 3
(d) 2 and 3
Ans. (a)
Explanation:
• A large number of Indian farmers are
illiterate and lack awareness of modern
scientific agricultural methods, which leads to
low productivity and stagnant rural incomes.
• Primary products, like agricultural
commodities, generally have lower price
elasticity and value addition compared to

manufactured goods, leading to a lower price
realization for rural producers.
• There has been historically low public and
private investment in agriculture, especially
in irrigation, infrastructure, mechanization,
and R&D, compared to the higher capital
investments in industry and services.
• Rural infrastructure such as roads, markets,
cold storage, and credit institutions remains
inadequate, further depressing rural incomes.
• Urban sectors benefit from higher
productivity, better education,
diversification, and market access, widening
the income gap between rural and urban
areas.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q10.Assertion (A): Though India’s national income
has gone up several fold since 1947, there has been
no marked improvement in the per capita income
level.
Reason (R): Sizeable proportion of the population of
India is still living below the poverty line.
In the context of the above two statements which one
of the following is correct?
(a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) Both A and R are true but R is not a correct
explanation of A
(c) A is true but R is false
(d) A is false but R is true
Ans. (a)
Explanation:
• Since Independence, India’s national income
has increased significantly, but the per capita
income growth has been modest, especially
in the first few decades.
• This is mainly because population growth has
outpaced national income growth, leading to
dilution of income per person.
• A large proportion of India’s population
continues to live below the poverty line,
which limits aggregate savings, investment
capacity, and demand for higher-value
goods, constraining per capita income growth.

•

While the economy has diversified and
expanded, income distribution remains
uneven, and the benefits of growth are not
uniformly shared across regions and classes.
• The slow trickle-down effect and high
dependency ratios further explain why rising
national income has not translated into a
proportionate increase in per capita income.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1997
Q1.The economic and monetary union of 15
European Countries is proposed to be made by 1999.
But the currencies of two countries, Franc, have
already the same value and circulate freely in both
the countries. The countries are
(a) France and Switzerland
(b) Switzerland and Luxembourg
(c) Luxembourg and Belgium
(d) France and Belgium
Ans. (a)
Explanation:
• The French franc was the official currency of
France until the introduction of the Euro in
1999 (accounting) and 2002 (cash).
• Switzerland used the Swiss franc, which had
the same face value and could circulate
freely in both countries due to long-standing
bilateral monetary arrangements.
• Before the euro, some European countries
had pegged or aligned their currencies to
stabilize exchange rates, a precursor to the
European Monetary Union.
• The Maastricht Treaty (1992) set the stage for
adopting the Euro, but some bilateral
currency alignments like the franc
arrangement already existed between France
and Switzerland.
• This facilitated cross-border trade and
monetary stability in the region even before
the formal EMU.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q2.The average rate of domestic savings (gross) for
the Indian economy is currently estimated to be in
the range of
(a) 15 to 20 per cent
(b) 20 to 25 per cent
(c) 25 to 30 per cent
(d) 30 to 35 per cent
Ans. (c)
Explanation:
• Gross Domestic Savings refers to GDP minus
final consumption expenditure, reflecting the
total savings of the household sector, private
corporate sector, and public sector.
• Historically, India’s domestic saving rate
increased significantly post-1990s, supporting
higher investment and growth.
• By the mid-1990s and later, the gross
domestic saving rate hovered between 25–
30% of GDP, which was critical for financing
economic development internally.
• According to World Bank data, India’s saving
rate was around 29.8% in 2015, indicating
sustained high savings compared to earlier
decades.
• A higher savings rate supports capital
formation, infrastructure investment, and
economic stability, reducing reliance on
foreign borrowing.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q3.The number of industries for which industrial
licensing is required has now been reduced to
(a) 15
(b) 6
(c) 35
(d) 20
Ans. (b)
Explanation:
• As part of the New Industrial Policy of 1991,
India undertook major industrial
deregulation, drastically reducing the number
of industries requiring compulsory licensing.
• By 1997, only six industries remained under
compulsory licensing due to security,

strategic, safety, and environmental
concerns.
• These included:
o Distillation and brewing of alcoholic
drinks
o Cigars and cigarettes of tobacco
o Aerospace and defense equipment
o Industrial explosives (like detonating
fuses, gun powder, nitrocellulose)
o Hazardous chemicals dangerous to
human health and safety
o Electronics was removed from this
list earlier.
• This deregulation aimed to boost private
investment, promote competition, and
reduce bureaucratic delays, aligning with
liberalization goals.
• Licensing was retained only where public
interest, health, or national security required
strict regulation.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q4.The sum of which of the following constitutes
Broad Money in India?
1. Currency with the Public
2. Demand deposits with banks
3. Time deposits with banks
4. Other deposits with RBI
Choose the correct answer using the codes given
below:
(a) 1 and 2
(b) 1, 2 and 3
(c) 1, 2, 3 and 4
(d) 1, 2 and 4
Ans. (b)
Explanation:
• Broad Money (M3) is the most
comprehensive measure of money supply in
India, indicating the total liquidity in the
economy.
• M3 includes:
o Currency with the public (notes and
coins in circulation),
o Demand deposits with banks (current
and savings accounts),

o

Time deposits with banks (fixed
deposits), which form a major
component.
• Other deposits with RBI are included in M4,
not M3.
• M3 is crucial because it captures both
immediate and near-liquid assets, providing a
fuller picture of money supply than narrow
money (M1).
• This measure is closely monitored by the
Reserve Bank of India (RBI) for monetary
policy formulation, inflation targeting, and
liquidity management.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q5.In India, inflation is measured by the
(a) Wholesale Price Index Number
(b) Consumers Price Index for urban non-manual
workers
(c) Consumers Price Index for agricultural workers
(d) National Income deflation
Ans. (a)
Explanation:
• Inflation refers to the rate of increase in the
general price level of goods and services over
a period of time, and measuring it requires
selecting an appropriate price index.
• In India, for a long time, the Wholesale Price
Index (WPI) was the primary measure of
inflation. It measures price changes at the
wholesale or producer level, not the retail
level.
• WPI includes a basket of about 697
commodities, broadly divided into Primary
Articles, Fuel & Power, and Manufactured
Products, and is published by the Office of
Economic Adviser, Ministry of Commerce and
Industry.
• Although Consumer Price Index (CPI) has
gained prominence since April 2014 when the
RBI adopted CPI (combined) for monetary
policy purposes, the WPI continues to be
widely used for measuring headline inflation,
especially in economic surveys and older
planning data.

•

CPI reflects retail inflation, while WPI reflects
wholesale inflation, and historically, policy
decisions, dearness allowance adjustments,
and industrial inputs were based on WPI
trends.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q6.The Sixth and the Eighth Five-Year Plans covered
the period 1980-85 and 1992-97 respectively. The
Seventh Five-Year Plan covered the period
(a) 1987-92
(b) 1986-91
(c) 1985-90
(d) 1988-94
Ans. (c)
Explanation:
• The Seventh Five-Year Plan of India was
launched under Prime Minister Rajiv Gandhi
and covered the period 1985 to 1990.
• Its main focus was on accelerating food grain
production, increasing employment
opportunities, and achieving self-sufficiency
in key sectors.
• It emphasized social justice, technology
upgrading, modernization of industries, and a
push towards infrastructure development.
• The plan period was followed by two annual
plans (1990–92) due to political instability,
after which the Eighth Five-Year Plan (1992–
97) was launched with economic reforms.
• The Seventh Plan targeted 5% growth rate
but achieved 5.8%, indicating better
performance despite global and domestic
challenges.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q7.Assertion (A): The emergence of economic
globalism does not imply the decline of socialist
ideology.
Reason (R): The ideology of Socialism believes in
universalism and globalism.
In the context of the above two statements, which
one of the following is correct?
(a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct
explanation of A

(b) Both A and R are true, but R is not a correct
explanation of A
(c) A is true, but R is false
(d) A is false, but R is true
Ans. (d)
Explanation:
• Economic globalism refers to the increasing
interconnectedness of economies through
trade, investment, technology, and capital
flows, often driven by liberalization and
market-oriented policies.
• The rise of economic globalism in the late
20th century coincided with the decline of
socialist economic models in many parts of
the world — especially after the collapse of
the Soviet Union in 1991, and the shift of
countries like India and China toward market
reforms.
• Therefore, the assertion that globalism does
not imply the decline of socialism is false,
because globalism actually emerged partly as
a reaction to inefficiencies in socialist
models.
• However, the Reason is true — the ideology
of socialism is indeed based on universalism
and international solidarity, focusing on
equality, collective welfare, and global
cooperation.
• Many socialist thinkers envisioned a global
socialist order, even though in practice,
socialism as an economic system has receded
in many states.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q8.Schemes of (i) Urban Micro Enterprises, (ii) Urban
Wage Employment, and (iii) Housing and Shelter
Upgradation are parts of
(a) Integrated Rural Development Programme
(b) Nehru Rozgar Yojana
(c) Jawahar Rozgar Yojana
(d) Prime Minister’s Rozgar Yojana
Ans. (b)
Explanation:
• Nehru Rozgar Yojana (NRY) was launched in
1989 during the tenure of Prime Minister

Rajiv Gandhi, with the primary aim of
providing employment to the urban
unemployed and underemployed poor.
• The programme focused on urban poverty
alleviation through both self-employment
and wage employment components.
• It was specifically designed for towns and
cities, distinguishing it from rural employment
schemes like IRDP and JRY which focused on
rural areas.
• It had three key sub-schemes:
o Urban Micro Enterprises Scheme:
Focused on self-employment through
setting up of small enterprises,
providing training and infrastructure
support for urban poor beneficiaries.
o Urban Wage Employment Scheme:
Focused on wage employment
through the creation of socially and
economically useful public assets,
applicable in urban local bodies with
populations over 1 lakh.
o Housing and Shelter Upgradation
Scheme: Focused on upgrading
housing and shelter in low-income
neighborhoods, training beneficiaries
in construction skills, and improving
living standards in towns with
populations between 1 lakh and 20
lakh.
• These measures were intended to reduce
urban unemployment and improve living
conditions through both direct employment
generation and infrastructure development.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q9.The contribution of India’s small-scale sector to
the gross turnover in the manufacturing sector since
1992 has been of the order of
(a) 40%
(b) 34%
(c) 30%
(d) 45%
Ans. (b)
Explanation:

•

Since the 1991 economic reforms, small-scale
industries (SSIs) have played a crucial role in
India’s industrial structure.
• In 1992, the contribution of the small-scale
sector to the gross turnover in manufacturing
was around 34%, reflecting its strong base in
labor-intensive production.
• Over the years, the share of SSIs has
remained substantial, with the sector
contributing:
o Over 40% of gross turnover in the
manufacturing sector.
o Around 45% of manufactured
exports.
o Around 25% of total exports from
India.
• SSIs are key in generating employment,
promoting entrepreneurship, and equitable
distribution of income.
• Government support through credit policies,
reservation of products, and infrastructure
development helped this sector expand
significantly during the liberalization period.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q10.National Income is the
(a) Net National Product at market price
(b) Net National Product at factor cost
(c) Net Domestic Product at market price
(d) Net Domestic Product at factor cost
Ans. (b)
Explanation:
• National Income refers to the total income
earned by the residents of a country from
production of goods and services, including
income from abroad.
• It is derived by calculating Net National
Product (NNP) at factor cost.
• NNP at Market Price – Indirect Taxes +
Subsidies = NNP at Factor Cost, i.e., National
Income.
• Market price includes indirect taxes and
excludes subsidies, while factor cost reflects
actual income received by factors of
production.

•

This is the most accurate measure of the
nation’s earning, as it accounts for the
income generated by land, labour, capital,
and entrepreneurship.
• In India, Central Statistical Office (CSO) (now
NSO) estimates national income using this
method.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q11.The Minimum Alternative Tax (MAT) was
introduced in the Budget of the Government of India
for the year
(a) 1991-92
(b) 1992-93
(c) 1995-96
(d) 1996-97
Ans. (d)
Explanation:
• Minimum Alternate Tax (MAT) was
introduced in 1996-97 by Finance Minister P.
Chidambaram.
• MAT was introduced because many highprofit companies were not paying taxes,
taking advantage of various exemptions and
incentives under the Income Tax Act — they
were referred to as “zero-tax companies.”
• MAT ensured that companies with book
profits above a certain limit must pay a
minimum tax, even if their taxable income
after deductions was low.
• Initially, MAT excluded the power and
infrastructure sectors, and later refinements
included exemption of export profits and
introduction of carry-forward credit system.
• The MAT rate and provisions have been
amended multiple times since its
introduction. It has become a significant part
of corporate taxation policy.
• MAT applies mainly to companies under the
Companies Act, and ensures minimum
contribution to tax revenues, aligning with
the principle of tax equity.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1998

Q1.Schemes of
(i) Urban Micro Enterprises,
(ii) Urban Wage Employment, and
(iii) Housing and Shelter Upgradation are parts of
(a) Integrated Rural Development Programme
(b) Nehru Rozgar Yojana
(c) Jawahar Rozgar Yojana
(d) Prime Minister’s Rozgar Yojana
Ans. (b)
Explanation:
• The Nehru Rozgar Yojana (NRY) was launched
in 1989 to address urban unemployment and
underemployment among the poor.
• It focused on self-employment ventures,
wage employment programmes, and shelter
upgradation schemes in towns and cities.
• The scheme had three main components:
o Urban Micro Enterprises (UME):
Provided training, credit, and
infrastructure support to urban poor
for small businesses.
o Urban Wage Employment (UWE):
Created socially and economically
useful public assets in towns with
population >1 lakh.
o Housing and Shelter Upgradation
(HUS): Focused on low-income
neighborhoods and economically
weaker sections for better housing
and construction skills.
• The urban focus distinguishes it from schemes
like IRDP and JRY, which were rural-oriented.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q2.The contribution of India’s small-scale sector to
the gross turnover in the manufacturing sector since
1992 has been of the order of
(a) 40%
(b) 34%
(c) 30%
(d) 45%
Ans. (b)
Explanation:

•

The Small-Scale Industries (SSI) sector has
been a vital contributor to India’s economy
since liberalization in 1991.
• Since 1992, the SSI sector contributed about
34% to the gross turnover of the
manufacturing sector.
• Over time, this contribution increased to
around 40–45%, showing the sector’s
resilience and importance in exports and
employment generation.
• The sector contributes 45% of manufactured
exports and provides employment to over
110 million people, making it the backbone of
India’s industrial base.
• It plays a key role in balanced regional
development, promoting entrepreneurship
and reducing income disparities.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q3. National Income is the
(a) Net National Product at market price
(b) Net National Product at factor cost
(c) Net Domestic Product at market price
(d) Net Domestic Product at factor cost
Ans. (b)
Explanation:
• National Income is the total money value of
all final goods and services produced by a
country’s residents during a year, calculated
at factor cost.
• It is equivalent to Net National Product (NNP)
at factor cost.
• NNP at market price = GDP + Net factor
income from abroad – Depreciation.
• To get NNP at factor cost, we subtract
indirect taxes and add subsidies to NNP at
market price.
• This measure reflects the actual income
received by factors of production (labour,
capital, land, and entrepreneurship), hence
considered the most accurate for national
income accounting.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q4. The Minimum Alternative Tax (MAT) was
introduced in the Budget of the Government of India
for the year
(a) 1991-92
(b) 1992-93
(c) 1995-96
(d) 1996-97
Ans. (d)
Explanation:
• Minimum Alternate Tax (MAT) was
introduced in 1996-97 Budget by Finance
Minister P. Chidambaram.
• Objective: To ensure that “zero-tax
companies” (profitable companies showing
zero taxable income due to exemptions and
deductions) pay a minimum share of their
book profits as tax.
• The concept was “if a company can distribute
dividends to shareholders, it must pay at
least some tax to the government.”
• Initially exempted power and infrastructure
sectors, but later extended to developers and
SEZ units.
• Over time, MAT became a significant tool for
tax equity, ensuring broadening of the tax
base and preventing tax avoidance.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q5. Consider the following statements:
The price of any currency in international market is
decided by the
1. World Bank.
2. demand for goods/services provided by the
country concerned.
3. stability of the government of the concerned
country.
4. economic potential of the country in question
Of these statements:
(a) 1, 2, 3 and 4 are correct
(b) 2 and 3 are correct
(c) 3 and 4 are correct
(d) 1 and 4 are correct
Answer: (b)
• The price of any currency in the international
market is determined by the forces of

demand and supply, not by an institution like
the World Bank.
• The demand for a country's currency is
directly linked to the global demand for its
goods and services (exports) and the desire
for foreign investment in that country.
• The stability of the government is a critical
factor, as political instability can lead to
economic uncertainty and deter foreign
investment, thus weakening the currency.
• While a country's economic potential is a
long-term factor, the immediate price is
more directly influenced by current demand
for its goods/services and government
stability.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q6. A consumer is said to be in equilibrium, if
